#4966. The Week Jensen Huang Fell: How a Spy Scandal Toppled Nvidia’s CEO

The Week Jensen Huang Fell: How a Spy Scandal Toppled Nvidia’s CEO

In the span of a few days, the most powerful executive in artificial intelligence went from untouchable to unemployed.

Jensen Huang, who built Nvidia from a graphics-card maker into the roughly $4 trillion engine of the AI boom, resigned Thursday after 33 years, felled not by a bad earnings report but by a scandal with the texture of a spy thriller.

Here’s how a defining week for AI, and by extension crypto, unfolded.

The crisis began with reports that Huang had grown close to Alice Huang (no relation), an executive at Singapore-based Megaspeed, one of Nvidia’s largest Southeast Asian customers, whom U.S. intelligence officials reportedly suspected of gathering information for Beijing.

For a company at the center of the U.S.-China chip fight and a looming debate over tighter export controls, the national-security overtones were impossible to ignore. Nvidia’s board opened an independent investigation into whether Huang had been the target of a foreign intelligence operation, though it said it had no reason to believe company secrets or national security were compromised.

On Nov. 8, Huang apologized for being “unfaithful” to his wife, acknowledging the relationship.

What might have been a single lapse quickly snowballed. The New York Times reported a second affair, with Kimberly Powell, Nvidia’s vice president of health care, who reported to Huang.

By Thursday, Huang was out as president, CEO and board member. Chief financial officer Colette Kress was named interim CEO, and longtime director Harvey Jones took over as board chair. The abruptness stunned an industry accustomed to treating Huang as synonymous with Nvidia itself.

Then, a day after his resignation, Reuters reported a third relationship, an “intimate” one with a second unnamed Nvidia employee, citing two people familiar with the matter. Reuters did not identify her, and Nvidia declined to comment. Huang has acknowledged only the Alice Huang affair, deflecting when the Times asked about Powell.

Nvidia’s stock cratered more than 14% at the open on Thursday before clawing back to close down about 4.6%. Wall Street quickly sorted winners from losers: rivals like AMD and Broadcom fared better, while companies tightly tied to Nvidia’s business took the brunt.

The reaction underscored how much of the AI trade, and the crypto market that increasingly moves with it, rests on a single company.

The scandal then rippled outward. Singapore barred Alice Huang from returning to the country, Alibaba shares slid on reports it was a Megaspeed customer, and congressional committees had already sought a closed-door briefing from Huang over his Megaspeed ties.

The loudest names in tech, AI and crypto called the ouster a blunder, with one founder branding it “a massive mistake.” Inside Nvidia, an internal suggestion-box post drew more than 1,600 comments, many pleading for Huang’s return. “If you believe in the mission, bring back THE boss,” one wrote.

The board has moved on regardless. According to the Financial Times, it approached Arm CEO Rene Haas, a former Nvidia executive who led Arm through its 2023 IPO, as a potential successor, though Nvidia called speculation about any candidate “premature.” Analysts have also floated Nvidia’s Ian Buck and AMD’s Lisa Su.

Nvidia’s chips power the AI data centers whose explosive buildout has become intertwined with crypto, from AI-agent commerce to the mining firms pivoting to host AI workloads.

Leadership turmoil at the company that supplies the picks and shovels ripples outward, and it lands amid a broader reckoning over AI’s role in everything from cyberattacks to data-center politics.

Nvidia reports earnings Nov. 17, with Kress at the helm and the permanent CEO search still underway, a nervous moment for a market already rattled by a sharp week-ending selloff.

For now, the company that defined the AI era is leaderless at the top, its founder’s legacy clouded by scandal, and the question hanging over Silicon Valley is whether anyone can fill the role Huang made uniquely his own.

#4964. RWA protocol stUSDT winds down 3 years after $2.3B TVL peak

The stUSDT team did not disclose a reason for winding down the RWA protocol but assured holders that all underlying assets remain fully backed.

Real-world asset (RWA) protocol stUSDT has begun winding down operations, nearly three years after reaching a $2.3 billion total value locked (TVL), ending rewards and opening a six-month redemption window for tokenholders.

The team said that interest accrual on stUSDT ended on Saturday, with holders of stUSDT and its wrapped version, wstUSDT, able to redeem through its official portal until May 14, 2027. The team said requests submitted after the deadline will be handled through customer support in monthly batches through August 2027.

The team did not provide a reason for the shutdown but said all underlying assets remain fully backed. Holders with tokens on exchanges or DeFi platforms must transfer them to personal wallets before redeeming.

Cointelegraph reached out to stUSDT for more information but did not receive a response before publication.

Meanwhile, JustLend DAO said it would phase out supply and borrowing functions for its wstUSDT market as part of the transition.

Launched in July 2023 on Tron, stUSDT allowed users to deposit USDT and earn yields from investments in real-world assets, including short-term government bonds. The protocol later expanded to Ethereum.

According to DefiLlama data, stUSDT’s TVL peaked at about $2.3 billion in November 2023 before declining substantially in subsequent years. It stood at about $8.6 million on Sunday, down from roughly $60 million a year earlier.

Despite its name, stUSDT is not affiliated with stablecoin issuer Tether. In September 2023, Tether denied any affiliation with stUSDT, saying the protocol was an independent project.

#4960. Bitcoin ETFs Lose $2.1 Billion in November as Outflow Streak Hits Seven Days

Bitcoin ETFs Lose $2.1 Billion in November as Outflow Streak Hits Seven Days

Bitcoin ETF investors are still heading for the exits, and the price slide is speeding up.

U.S. spot Bitcoin ETFs shed $185.4 million on Friday, according to Decrypt’s ETF tracker, extending their outflow streak to seven straight trading days.

The funds lost about $829 million this week after shedding nearly $1.3 billion the week before, bringing November’s outflows to roughly $2.1 billion.

The selling so far this month has been relentless. The funds bled $1.4 billion over five sessions earlier in the streak as investors cut risk. Cumulative net inflows since launch have slipped to $55.3 billion, and total net assets fell to $94.9 billion, below the $100 billion mark they held through much of October.

Other crypto funds haven’t escaped the rout. Ethereum ETFs took in a modest $2.3 million on Friday, snapping a six-day outflow streak, but still lost about $336 million on the week and roughly $702 million so far in November. Solana ETFs shed about $28 million this week, though they retain $1.6 billion in cumulative net inflows.

Spot crypto ETFs hold the underlying asset, such as Bitcoin or Ethereum, and trade on stock exchanges like ordinary shares, giving investors exposure through a regular brokerage account. Bitcoin funds have drawn $55.3 billion in net inflows since debuting in January 2024. Because issuers report flows daily and much of the money comes from institutions and financial advisers, the figures have become a closely watched gauge of investor sentiment.

The outflows have tracked a sharp sell-off. Bitcoin traded near $64,500 over the weekend, down 6% over 24 hours and about 15% on the week, according to CoinGecko, and it has lost more than 22% over the past month.

Ethereum fell 8.4% to about $1,790, and Solana dropped 12% to roughly $70. Total crypto market value slid 4.5% to about $2.3 trillion.

Bitcoin hit a 12-week low this week as fears of further Federal Reserve rate hikes erased its summer rally, and concentrated liquidations pushed the price below $69,000 earlier in the week.

Not every institution is retreating. JPMorgan’s holdings of Bitcoin and crypto-linked securities are approaching $1 billion, according to its most recent SEC filing. However, those purchases predate the more recent market downturn.

#4953. How Wall Street Is Racing to Claim the Onchain Dollar

How Wall Street Is Racing to Claim the Onchain Dollar

For years, stablecoins were crypto’s business, dominated by Tether and Circle and used mostly to trade digital assets. That era is ending.

Over the past few months, and especially the past few weeks, the biggest names in traditional finance have moved en masse to claim a piece of the onchain dollar. Here’s the map of a land grab that’s accelerating fast.

The clearest signal came this week, when Goldman Sachs, Citigroup, Santander, and Deutsche Bank were reported to be among banks committing roughly $270 million to a joint dollar-stablecoin venture codenamed “Project Faro.”

The capital funds five years of operations for a U.S.-incorporated issuer targeting a 2027 launch, part of a 21-institution coalition that has swelled from an initial group of 10. The goal is explicit: challenge Tether and Circle, which together still control about 85% of a roughly $290 billion market.

While the consortium plans, SoFi has shipped. Earlier this week, its SoFiUSD, billed as the first stablecoin from a U.S. national bank, expanded onto Polygon this month alongside Ethereum and Solana, reaching the bank’s 17 million-plus members.

SoFi also launched SoFi Earn, a tokenized deposit paying 4.40% yield that converts into SoFiUSD, and already settles its entire card program over Mastercard’s network.

Meanwhile, the federally chartered crypto bank Anchorage Digital has positioned itself as a settlement backbone.

Anchorage’s tokens can now settle transactions on Visa’s network, starting with Western Union’s USDPT, and it recently anchored both sides of the U.S.-Mexico stablecoin corridor, custodying tokens across one of the world’s largest remittance routes.

Mastercard has been the most aggressive incumbent among payment giants.

It launched a Global Money Platform with acquired stablecoin firm BVNK, rolled out a Stablecoin Security tool to screen payments for fraud, and unveiled Agent Pay for Machines, letting AI agents transact in stablecoins.

Visa, meanwhile, has expanded its own stablecoin settlement program across multiple blockchains.

The crypto-native incumbents, not to be left out, are also racing to entrench.

Circle struck a deal to bring USDC settlement to banking rails in Latin America, starting with a Colombia pilot, part of a broader expansion into dozens of emerging markets.

The catalyst is regulation. The GENIUS Act, signed in 2025, created the first U.S. framework for dollar-pegged tokens, giving regulated institutions a clear path into a business they had largely watched from the sidelines.

The Donald Trump administration has framed dollar stablecoins as a tool to extend the dollar’s reach and global hegemony, and banks that once dismissed crypto now see a settlement business they can’t afford to cede.

Much of this remains early. Project Faro has no token, no CEO, and no final name. Tether and Circle still dominate, and bank-issued tokens face a steep climb to win share.

But the direction is unmistakable: the onchain dollar, once crypto’s turf, is now contested ground, with Wall Street, the card networks and the stablecoin incumbents all racing to own the rails money moves on.

#4951. Coinone warns of phishing, identity theft after customer data breach

The South Korean crypto exchange said potentially exposed information includes passwords, bank account numbers, national identification numbers and crypto wallet addresses.

South Korean crypto exchange Coinone warned customers of potential phishing scams and identity theft after an attacker breached its outsourced customer support provider, exposing information shared during customer service interactions.

On Sunday, Coinone said an attacker accessed servers operated by MetaM on Thursday and viewed or extracted customer support records. Coinone said it learned about the breach in the same day.

According to Coinone, the information potentially exposed names, phone numbers, email addresses, dates of birth, bank account numbers, passwords, national identification numbers, home and wallet addresses and transaction IDs.

Coinone said the affected data varies by customer, and people who never contacted its support center were unaffected. It did not disclose how many customers were affected.

The exchange urged customers who shared passwords during support interactions to change them and warned that fraudsters could impersonate Coinone using details from previous conversations.

Coinone said its account, trading and wallet infrastructure operates separately from MetaM’s servers, with no evidence of direct compromise. The exchange said it has reported the incident to South Korean authorities.

The disclosure comes as South Korea moves to strengthen financial-sector cybersecurity. In October, the Financial Services Commission identified security deficiencies at 130 of 555 financial firms reviewed.

On Thursday, the regulator said it would allow crypto exchanges to suspend withdrawals when hacking is suspected, alongside calls for round-the-clock monitoring.

#4947. Bitcoin falls to 4-month low near $62K as crypto selloff deepens

The global cryptocurrency market capitalization fell 4% to $2.26 trillion over 24 hours, while Ether and Solana posted double-digit weekly losses.

Bitcoin fell to a four-month low near $62,000 on Sunday, extending a selloff across major digital assets before recovering some of its losses.

On Sunday, Bitcoin (BTC) dropped to a 24-hour low of $62,080.91, according to CoinGecko, its weakest level since July. The asset later rebounded above $65,000 but remained down nearly 15% in the last seven days.

The downturn extended beyond Bitcoin. CoinGecko data showed Ether (ETH) down 19.5% over the past week, while Solana (SOL) lost 20.7%. The total value of the cryptocurrency market fell about 4% over 24 hours to $2.26 trillion, according to CoinGecko.

Market sentiment also deteriorated. The Crypto Fear & Greed Index fell to 10 on Sunday from 13 on Saturday and 20 on Friday, remaining in “extreme fear,” according to Alternative.me.

The move followed Friday’s slide below $70,000, as analysts reported weakening investor sentiment and concerns over institutional outflows and tighter liquidity.

The latest decline came after US spot Bitcoin exchange-traded funds posted $2.35 billion in net outflows over two consecutive weeks. The funds lost $1.07 billion during the week ended Nov. 13, following $1.28 billion the previous week. BlackRock’s iShares Bitcoin Trust extended its outflow streak to 11 trading sessions on Friday.

#4937. Enterprise blockchain is going to come back from the dead

Opinion by Paul Brody, founder and chief executive, Nightfall Networks

Do you remember Pets.com? It raised $110 million from Amazon and others to build a business where people ordered pet toys and dog food online.

The company crashed and burned, and everyone learned an important lesson: you can’t profitably ship a 50-pound bag of dog food to a customer’s door.

As online retail entrepreneur Ed Baum asked in a 2001 postmortem: “Did Pets.com’s business plan assume the invention of transporters to magically move bags of pet food without shipping costs?”

Please, whatever you do, don’t share this with the people at Chewy; they might not realize their business model doesn’t work. Chewy reported $12.6 billion in sales and $223 million in net income in its latest fiscal year.

The real lesson of Pets.com wasn’t that shipping dog food was a bad idea. It’s that the idea arrived before the logistics, customer habits and unit economics required to make it work.

As it was with chew toys and dog food, so it may be with enterprise blockchains.

Collectively, the crypto industry probably incinerated just as much money trying that between 2017 and 2023 as Pets.com. Maybe more. But the dozens of companies that went after enterprise blockchain were not delusional; they were just too early.

It’s not hard to understand the appeal of enterprise blockchain solutions, at least superficially. Nearly all enterprise systems today tend to fail at the boundaries between companies. There are two failure modes: mode 1, the OG failure mode, where collaboration information is constantly out of sync.

In this world, enterprise IT systems are islands, communicating asynchronously with each other by digital text messages and emailed spreadsheets. It’s cheap and easy to set up, if you don’t mind always having slightly wrong information.

Failure mode two is the costly, real-time proprietary integration. Powerful industry leaders politely request their key suppliers and partners to join their closed but always-on networks.

Chewy reported $223 million in net income in its latest fiscal year. Source: Chewy.

These are very costly, but they work well and leave everyone with a kind of business version of Stockholm syndrome, where you come to appreciate the thoroughness and efficiency of the digital choke-hold you are in.

Blockchains offered a better path forward: shared information and business logic across an open ecosystem. All the benefits of a proprietary system in terms of real-time visibility and efficiency, without the lock-in.

Sometime around 2017, this started to look like a very obvious good idea. Investment poured in, consortia launched, press releases were drafted and released, and then pretty much everything crashed and burned. TradeLens, We.Trade, Contour, B3i, ASX Chess, Everledger. All came and went in just a few years.

No company wants their competition to see what they’re doing in detail. Mostly, this meant building a private/permissioned chain and then doing lots of non-blockchain-like things to it and in it to try to keep information private. The results were uniformly slow and expensive and failed to gain traction.

It’s hard to understate the bloodbath of companies and startups that tried and failed to make enterprise blockchain work. A quick web and history search turned up 18 different ones, some of them well-known.

What few survivors of this are left have “de-chained” themselves — giving up the blockchain theater and reverting to a classic software-as-a-service (SaaS) model. Many companies that tried and tested these permissioned enterprise systems as users came away from their experience with a clear learning: we tried blockchain, it didn’t work.

That’s unfortunate because everything is different now.

Blockchains have scale. Ethereum in 2020 handled 1 million transactions a day and costs per transaction periodically spiked to $30-50 when demand was high. Today, most transactions get done under a penny and capacity is ramping toward billions of transactions a day. Nobody talks about the “trilemma” anymore. Ethereum is decentralized and it is scaled.

Related: Ethereum’s Glamsterdam upgrade launches on Sepolia testnet

Finally, there’s the money. It’s all moving onchain. Regulators around the globe have blessed stablecoins and the result is a tsunami of liquidity, with transaction volume reaching almost $1.8 trillion in June.

If we combine the operational benefits of blockchains with the financial ones, we get something that enterprises have never really had before: a single ecosystem where you can not only buy, sell, and move assets, but get paid for them as well.

There was just under $1.8 trillion in adjusted stablecoin transaction volume in June. Source: Visa

The return on investment from every single component of this equation is big. Combined, they can yield colossal advantages. McKinsey partners even found that digital supply chain tools could make inventory reductions of 35-75% possible through better planning.

More conservatively, from my experience in the field, just switching away from the existing cascades of spreadsheets and electronic data interchange (EDI) messages can allow firms to run most supply chains with about 20% less inventory at the same service level. That’s worth billions in working capital in most industries.

It gets better from there. Smart contracts reduce the time required to handle many business contract rules from days to minutes and cut the costs of administration in half.

Microsoft began moving its Xbox video game network onto smart contracts for handling royalties and cut the time to access monthly royalty statements from 45 days to four minutes. All that while handling more than 2 million transactions a day.

And once you have inventories, contracts, purchase orders and invoices onchain, you can go after working capital directly as well.

When you can prove your purchase order originates from a top-quality buyer, you can access credit for less. And when you have full visibility into inventory and delivery status, you can factor receivables immediately and efficiently.

The possibilities and the returns are big. The use cases are obvious. It’s time to try again.

Magazine: What Democrats’ US midterm sweep means for the CLARITY Act

#4935. Elon Musk Joins 160,000 Backing Petition to Bring Jensen Huang Back to Nvidia

Elon Musk Joins 160,000 Backing Petition to Bring Jensen Huang Back to Nvidia

Elon Musk has thrown his support behind a campaign to reinstate Jensen Huang as Nvidia's CEO, as the ousted chipmaker co-founder reportedly weighs a comeback just days after resigning.

"A petition is underway to reinstate Jensen and I'm signing it too," the Tesla and SpaceX CEO tweeted on Friday. Musk had previously said it would be "an honor" to hire Huang.

A petition is underway to reinstate Jensen and I’m signing it too

— Elon Musk (@elonmusk) November 13, 2026

The "Stand with Jensen" open letter calls on Nvidia's board to bring Huang back while an independent investigation establishes the facts. It has drawn more than 160,000 signatures, including Epic Games CEO Tim Sweeney and Y Combinator CEO Garry Tan, CNBC reported, though the site notes that signatories' affiliations are self-reported and unverified. More than 7,500 signatories identified themselves as Nvidia employees, The Guardian reported, a figure it said was equivalent to 18% of the company's workforce.

The campaign follows internal calls from Nvidia staff for Huang's return.

Huang has discussed the possibility of returning with confidants, the Financial Times reported, citing multiple people familiar with the matter. One ally told the newspaper they were assembling a "war room" of Silicon Valley executives to lobby for his reinstatement and would seek support from the White House, adding that the effort had Huang's backing.

Even supporters acknowledged the obstacles. "Having an affair with a subordinate is a fireable offence," one person involved in the effort told the FT.

Two major Nvidia shareholders, Altimeter Capital's Brad Gerstner and Atreides Management's Gavin Baker, urged the board in an open letter on Friday to leave "all options on the table, including the potential return of Jensen Huang."

Huang resigned as Nvidia's president, CEO and board member on Thursday, capping a week of reports of affairs with Alice Huang, a former executive at Nvidia customer Megaspeed whom U.S. officials suspect of spying for China, and with an Nvidia executive who reported to him. Chief financial officer Colette Kress is serving as interim CEO.

The campaign faces resistance in Washington. Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent said on Sunday that Nvidia should look outside the company for its next CEO. "They need a dramatic shake-up," he told Fox News.

Bessent said on Sunday Morning Futures that questions over Nvidia's compliance with U.S. export restrictions were the bigger issue, and that the company should be judged on whether it had "intentionally ignored the warning signs" of shell companies in Singapore. He said he wanted answers "in the next 10 days from the board and from the outside counsel." Huang also faces congressional inquiries into Nvidia's chip exports to China, as well as a proposed shareholder class action.

Nvidia's board has approached Arm CEO Rene Haas as a potential successor, the FT has reported. "We are in the process of selecting a search firm for the NVIDIA CEO role. We have not formally engaged any potential candidates," an Nvidia spokesperson told CNBC.

Analysts are divided on Huang's chances. "We see no chance Jensen comes back to Nvidia as CEO," Dan Ives of Yorkville Ives & Co. said in a note cited by CNBC, while Seaport Research Partners' Jay Goldberg said Huang could stage "a second act" if he gets through his legal troubles. Former White House communications director Anthony Scaramucci was blunter: "The FBI doesn't take petitions," he tweeted.

Nvidia reports quarterly earnings on Tuesday, the first under Kress.

#4930. Vitalik Buterin Now ‘a Total Bull’ on leanPCS as Fast Track to Quantum-Safe Ethereum

Vitalik Buterin Now ‘a Total Bull’ on leanPCS as Fast Track to Quantum-Safe Ethereum

Ethereum co-founder Vitalik Buterin has thrown his weight behind a proposal he says could make the network quantum-safe much faster than expected.

"Over the past few weeks I've gone from thinking leanPCS is total bull to being a total bull," Buterin tweeted on Monday, in a lengthy explainer on how Ethereum could handle the much larger signatures and proofs that quantum-resistant cryptography requires.

Over the past few weeks I've gone from thinking leanPCS is total bull to being a total bull. So let me explain some of these buzzwords in more detail.

At the core, 8288 is about hyper-scaling data and execution for any computation that needs to get performed on a piece of data…

— vitalik.eth (@VitalikButerin) November 16, 2026

In short, leanPCS is a proposed way for Ethereum to bundle many large, quantum-safe signatures and proofs into a single compact proof, so that the full data never has to be stored onchain. It is built on polynomial commitment schemes, or PCS, the core mathematical building block of STARK proofs.

Quantum computers are expected to eventually break the elliptic curve cryptography that secures Ethereum and Bitcoin accounts today. The quantum-safe replacements are far bulkier: Buterin said signatures would grow from 65 bytes to between 1 and 6 kilobytes, while zero-knowledge proofs would balloon from a few hundred bytes to around 128 kilobytes. "This is unsustainable to put on chain," he wrote.

Buterin's answer is EIP-8288, a draft proposal he co-authored with developer Thomas Coratger in June, which would aggregate those signatures and proofs before they ever reach a block.

Under the design, users publish their signatures or proofs, and network nodes repeatedly bundle everything they have seen into a single STARK, a type of zero-knowledge proof, that attests the underlying data is valid. These aggregate proofs are themselves combined until a single "master proof" verifies everything in a block. Only short claims, rather than the full signatures or proofs, would need to be stored onchain.

He laid out three ways to build it, depending on what users submit. The simplest, leanSPHINCS, would support a single type of quantum-safe, hash-based signature, costing users only a small amount of gas per signature, but it couldn't do anything else.

Buterin's preferred option, leanPCS, offers a third route. It would offer the same generality as leanSTARK but "could potentially be implemented very quickly," he said, because far fewer design choices are needed, and leanSTARK could then be added later.

"So there is a route to getting 'the cryptographic proof singularity' much faster than any of us expected," Buterin wrote, "and that route runs through 8288 with leanPCS."

Buterin linked to SPHINCS-, a family of hash-based signature schemes optimized for Ethereum, which was published on the Ethereum Research forum in June with input from Buterin and Ethereum Foundation cryptographers. The post shows that a standards-aligned quantum-safe signature can already be verified on Ethereum for about 150,000 gas, without any protocol change, compared with about 3,000 gas for an ECDSA signature today, according to an earlier Buterin estimate.

Under protocol-level aggregation, a future "leanSPHINCS" verification would cost about 3,000 gas, according to the post.

#4928. US sellers drive Bitcoin crash, Jensen plots Nvidia comeback: Hodler’s Digest

Anchorage Digital data suggests the Bitcoin selloff is being driven by US investors. A “war room” has been set up by supporters to reinstate Nvidia boss Jensen Huang.

Bitcoin bottomed after the US midterm elections in 2018 and 2022 — and the great Bitcoin crash of November 2026 looks like history repeating. Bitcoin plunged more than $10,000 this week, and is currently trading at $65,116 for a 12.4% loss.

According to Anchorage Digital researcher David Lawant, the call is coming from inside the house. “BTC sell pressure continues to come primarily from the U.S,” he noted, positing a chart highlighting showing continued heavy net taker sell flow primarily on Coinbase compared with overseas exchanges.

The US spot Bitcoin ETFs have seen heavy outflows, losing $2.35 billion over the past two weeks, and influencer Axel Bitblaze noted that Bitcoin fell in the hour before the US open on all but two of the past fifteen days.

Binance founder Changpeng Zhao rebuffed the idea that Binance is behind the crash. “No, Binance did not sell crypto. I bought crypto in the past few days,” he said. He attributed the crash to money shifting to the Anthropic IPO, the Bitcoin ETF outflows, security fears about crypto in general in the wake of the Binance hack, and hot inflation data raising expectations of interest rate hikes. Markets are now pricing in a quarter point Fed hike in December.

Bitcoin fell to a four month low near $62,000 on Sunday but has since rebounded above $65,000. The Crypto Fear and Greed Index fell to 10, which is Extreme Fear.

History provides some good news in that Bitcoin more then doubled in price within 12 months of the 2018 and 2022 midterm elections.

The Financial Times reports that Jensen Huang is plotting a comeback to the top job at Nvidia with allies, following a determined push in Silicon Valley to pressure the board to reinstate him.

Nvidia shares plunged after Huang resigned as CEO and from the board on Nov. 12, with Chief Financial Officer Colette Kress named as interim CEO. The resignation came shortly after news broke he’d had two other affairs, including one with a subordinate.

Multiple people confirmed Huang has been discussing the idea of returning with confidants, even though an independent probe by the board is examining allegations he was romantically involved with a Chinese spy who helped ferry chips to China.

A petition to reinstate Huang has garnered more than 100,000 signatures, including 5,600 current Nvidia employees, along with SpaceX boss Elon Musk and Epic Games CEO Tim Sweeney.

The turmoil at Nvidia has weighed heavily on AI stocks and risk assets like cryptocurrency and the story still has a long way to run, with Congressional moves to investigate the issue and three bills being advanced to tighten AI chip export restrictions to China. According to the New York Time, intelligence officials reportedly briefed President Trump about the allegations Alice Huang was a Chinese spy a year ago, but he did not take any action.

On Wednesday, the group outlined “pqai27,” a proposed roadmap that aims to protect Ethereum against threats from quantum computing and artificial intelligence.

At Devcon, researcher Justin Drake said he hopes to complete the transition in 2028 while the published roadmap still targets December 2029.

Japan’s Metaplanet and crypto treasury company Upexi are also marked for deletion, while Ether treasury firm Sharplink is on a watchlist. The changes take effect after market close on Nov. 30.

“Index inclusion meant ordinary passive investors, like someone holding a global index fund in their pension, were getting leveraged Bitcoin exposure they never chose. MSCI just fixed that,” Ryan Kirkley, CEO of Global Settlement Network, told Cointelegraph. Strategy fell 6.49% on Thursday and are down by more than one quarter over the past month.

#4924. The Best Hoodies for Men, Tested by Style Editors

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

We recommend options from Alo and Champion.

AS A MEN’S HEALTH style editor, I spend most of my office days wearing business-casual and semi-dressy fits. But when I clock out, I have a closet stacked with hoodies that I love to slip on. From freezing winters in New Jersey during my teen years to long days on campus at Howard University, they’ve always been a core piece in my wardrobe. Thankfully, hoodies have come a long way since then—gone are the days of big, boxy sweats that make you disappear into your clothes. Today, the best hoodies for men aren’t just better in fit, fabric, and function—they actually look good, too.

A hoodie is basically your favorite long-sleeve tee or crewneck—but with a hood attached. Pullovers give you that classic, laid-back vibe, while zip-up styles make it easy to throw on or peel off without messing up your hair. The best hoodies for men are made from soft, smooth fabrics like cotton or fleece that feel great on your skin, and some even come with moisture-wicking tech if you’re hitting the trails, the gym, or just dealing with unpredictable weather.

As Men’s Health editors, we practically live in sweats during the fall and winter, whether we’re heading to the gym, running errands, going on work trips, or curling up on the couch. Over the last five years, our team has tested countless options, from OG brands like Abercrombie and Champion to newer athleisure labels like Alo and Vuori. These are the hoodies that left a lasting impression—and are still in rotation.

In typical Alo fashion, the brand’s Accolade Hoodie nails that sweet spot between streetwear and luxury. From the chrome branding on the chest to the hefty cotton-polyester fabric that feels soft and substantial, it has that premium vibe you notice the second you pull it on. The french terry exterior has this plush, fuzzy texture that amps up its comfort factor and makes you want to lounge in it all day.

This one’s got a boxy, oversized cut that gives me full freedom to move how I want, whether I’m reaching overhead to grab a coffee mug and bending to tie my sneakers without the fabric pulling under the arms. Despite the larger relaxed fit, I don’t feel stuffy or like I’m swimming in fabric. Plus, it has an exceptional warmth-to-weight ratio, keeping me cozy without overheating during those weird in-between weather days. It's ideal for guys who prefer a looser feel, whether you're layering over a tee or a crewneck. If you prefer a slimmer fit, I’d recommend going down a size (or even two).

I’ve worn mine to grab breakfast in Soho and to and from workouts, and it always looks on point. While it comes in a bunch of slick tones, I’m especially into the rich brown. This color makes it feel a bit dressier than your average hoodie and on trend for fall, so I’m never shy about wearing it out in public.

Take the brand's relaxed-fitting style hoodie, which doesn't feel too loose or baggy. We found there's plenty of room in the chest and at the waist, meaning you can easily layer long-sleeve T-shirts and still receive a great deal of breathability. Plus, the cotton-blend fabric has a hint of stretch, so it's perfect for guys with broader shoulders and bigger biceps.

The best hoodies for men are produced from high-quality materials that will keep you warm and comfortable. Most styles are made of a mix of fabrics like cotton fleece, terry cloth, or a cotton-polyester blend. If you work out in a sweatshirt, opt for a lighter option with moisture-wicking and quick-drying fabrics designed for layering.

Ideally, your hoodies shouldn't fit too tight, but they also shouldn't be too loose either. The fit should align with your lifestyle. Below, we've broken down the four most common types on the market:

This is your standard, go-to fit. It's not too tight or too loose, offering a comfortable amount of room without being baggy. It's versatile, working well for both casual wear and layering.

As the name suggests, this one's more fitted through the body and sleeves, giving a sleeker look. It's great for those who want a more streamlined, modern silhouette without all the extra bulk.

This fit gives you more room than regular, often with a slightly dropped shoulder or looser sleeves. It's comfortable and easygoing but still looks intentional, offering a bit of room to breathe without going full-on oversized.

This one's all about the dramatic, roomy look, intentionally loose with plenty of space in the body and sleeves for a laid-back streetwear-inspired vibe. It's perfect for layering or just lounging around in something super comfy.

Hoodies can range from lightweight to heavyweight fabrics depending on the weather conditions. Lightweight materials may be better for indoor use, while heavyweight fabrics are better for outdoor activities. So consider the fabric and bulk for your weather needs.

#4923. German Lawmakers Back Customs Powers to Seize Crypto of 'Unclear Origin'

German Lawmakers Back Customs Powers to Seize Crypto of 'Unclear Origin'

Germany's parliament has approved sweeping new powers for the country's customs agency, allowing it to seize assets, including crypto, whose lawful origin is in doubt, even when no criminal investigation is underway.

The Bundestag passed the Customs Financial Justice Act, or Zollfinanzgerechtigkeitsgesetz, on Thursday, November 12, the German government said. Under the law, customs can more easily seize assets where there are "justified doubts" about their lawful origin, an instrument the government describes as a "suspicious wealth order." Customs will also be able to better monitor digital cross-border payment routes and secure crypto assets.

"With this, we are sending a clear signal to criminals: we are getting tougher," German Finance Minister Lars Klingbeil said, according to the government.

The powers apply to "significant" assets, which typically means those worth more than €100,000, according to the government's bill. Because owners of such assets are usually able to explain where they came from, while the state struggles to prove an illegal origin, the government argued that it is reasonable to ask owners to disclose that information. "Almost every law-abiding citizen will be able to explain where these assets came from," the bill's explanatory notes say, as summarized by the Bundestag's news service.

Klingbeil has said customs could hold assets for up to 180 days without a conviction, during which owners would have to show they were obtained legally. "The Porsche and the Rolex are then gone for now," he said, according to the Berliner Zeitung. Any permanent confiscation would require a court ruling. Following an amendment by the governing coalition, that decision will be made by a panel of finance court judges rather than a single judge.

The law also reorganizes Germany's customs administration, strengthens its Financial Intelligence Unit and gives customs a legal basis to use technologies such as artificial intelligence. It forms part of an action plan against organized crime that the government adopted on February 25, 2026.

Germany has seized large amounts of crypto before, and has been quick to sell it. In 2024, authorities in the state of Saxony began selling the nearly 50,000 BTC they had seized from operators of the movie piracy website Movie2K, moving hundreds of millions of dollars' worth at a time as the sell-off continued. Blockchain analytics firm Arkham later said authorities had failed to seize a further 45,000 BTC linked to the site. The new law would give customs a way to seize crypto of unclear origin without first having to tie it to a specific crime.

German authorities have also gone after laundering infrastructure. Last year, they joined Swiss police in shutting down Cryptomixer, a mixing service that had laundered more than $1.4 billion in Bitcoin since 2016. Separately, the finance ministry has drafted a 25% tax on crypto gains from 2027.

The Bundestag's finance committee backed the bill on Wednesday with votes from the governing CDU/CSU and SPD, as well as the far-right AfD. The Left Party voted against, and the Greens abstained.

The Greens said Germany's money laundering problem is estimated at €100 billion, and that the government's expectation of only €308 million in additional revenue showed the gap between its ambitions and the law. The Left Party said the confiscation process was "built for the cash courier, not the banker."

The law still needs to pass the Bundesrat, the upper house of parliament that represents Germany's states.

#4918. Luxor CEO warns of AI ‘dark forest’ following Bitcoin miner hijack

Attackers redirected customers’ Bitcoin mining power through Luxor’s Commander platform over the weekend, prompting its CEO to issue a warning to crypto businesses.

Bitcoin mining services provider Luxor has warned that AI-enabled cyberattacks are becoming increasingly difficult to defend against after attackers compromised its mining-management platform and redirected customers’ computing power to another mining pool over the weekend.

In an X post on Sunday, Luxor CEO Nick Hansen said the attack came through a “sophisticated AI-enabled vector,” without specifying how AI was used in the attack, warning that these types of attacks will only get more frequent over time.

“At this moment, anyone running any sort of substantial business, especially in crypto, is inundated with AI-enabled attacks,” Hansen said in an X post. “This does not seem to be slowing down; rather, it’s speeding up as models get better and more attackers enter the arena.”

The warning comes as security researchers have documented the spread of AI-assisted hacking tools, raising the prospect of faster attacks against businesses holding money or controlling valuable infrastructure.

“Abliterated models are running 24/7 against your entire surface, looking for a little crack, which they then use to (almost instantly) expand,” added Hansen.

Hansen said Luxor has had to put the majority of its feature work on hold in order to harden its cybersecurity. The company has also acquired its own GPUs to run unrestricted open-weight AI models aimed at improving its security posture.

“I urge anyone running a similar service to do the same.  It’s your only defense in this new AI-powered dark forest. ‘The only thing that stops a bad guy with a gun is a good guy with a gun.’”

Initial reports suggested that about 64,000 mining machines representing 13 exahashes per second were redirected to the OCEAN mining pool on Saturday.

Luxor has not confirmed either figure, saying the incident affected just over a dozen customer accounts using its Commander mining-management tool.

Luxor said it patched Commander within two hours of detecting the unauthorized changes and began restoring affected machines to their original pool settings. It pledged to compensate customers in full for the redirected mining power and publish a post-mortem.

Cointelegraph contacted Luxor for comment.

Related: Coinone warns of phishing, identity theft after customer data breach

“We have explored the issue extensively, and it was isolated to the Commander tool. None of Luxor’s other services (including Luxor Pool and LuxOS) were affected,” said Luxor.

Initial reports suggested the attackers received no mining rewards. However, a Bitcoin address identified by CoreX as associated with the incident subsequently received two OCEAN payouts totaling approximately 0.162 BTC, worth about $10,500.

Hansen’s warning follows a string of reported AI-assisted intrusions targeting crypto businesses.

Earlier this month, India’s cybersecurity agency warned that a group called Crafty Falcon was using AI-powered tools to attack financial companies, including cryptocurrency exchanges, stealing wallet keys and siphoning funds.

Blockchain analytics firm Chainalysis disclosed in October that an attacker used what it called “offensive agentic techniques” to access its systems. The breach exposed password hashes, live tokens and other customer information.

Anthropic said in a September report that multiple groups had developed “automated exploit foundries” with AI, accelerating the pace of vulnerability research, testing, and exploit design.

#4916. Emideltide Keeps Getting a ‘No’ for Compounding. What’s the Problem?

Why the government’s renewed interest in peptides excludes this one.

THE TRUMP ADMINISTRATION has not been shy about its pro-peptide stance. US Health and Human Services Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. has pushed to make six popular yet unapproved peptides available for compounding to people with a prescription. A recent FDA advisory committee meeting voted in favor of adding six out of seven peptides to a list of substances that U.S. compounding pharmacies are allowed to manufacture and supply.

Six out of seven. So what happened to that last peptide? Emideltide, also dubbed DSIP, is a peptide people have turned to for better sleep. And it was the only peptide the FDA advisory board voted against moving out of the Category 2 list—a substance considered to carry significant safety risks, with compounding pharmacies prohibited from making it. Here’s why.

EMIDELTIDE IS A nine-amino-acid peptide originally isolated from the blood of sleeping rabbits during electrically induced sleep in the late 1970s. Since its discovery, a synthetic peptide version has been administered via injection or a nasal spray.

Emideltide reportedly helps you fall asleep faster and extend the time spent in deep sleep. It’s also been used to avoid waking up multiple times throughout the night and anecdotal reports online have mentioned more vivid, Inception–like lucid dreaming. With better sleep, people online using emideltide have mentioned feeling less stress, more refreshed upon waking, and faster recovery after a grueling workout.

MOST RESEARCH LOOKING into the peptide’s role in sleep, however, took place in the 1980s and 1990s—with mixed findings.

For example, a 1984 case study reported on a 35-year-old guy with 10 years of narcolepsy who had reduced sleep attacks, less daytime napping, and more alertness with weekly emideltide injections administered in the morning.

One clinical trial had 16 people take an emideltide injection or a placebo while measuring how long it took for them to fall asleep once they’re tucked into bed. People who had an emideltide injection said they felt more tired and actually went to sleep quicker than the placebo group. That should be great except when they repeated the experiment a second night, the placebo group knocked out quicker than the peptide group.

Another study in 1992 found the peptide helped chronic insomniacs feel less tired. However, there was such little difference in the time it took to fall asleep and stay asleep in the emideltide group versus the placebo group. The authors of the 1992 study said any small improvements with the peptide were probably “due to an incidental change in the placebo group” and “not likely to be of major therapeutic benefit.”

BEYOND SLEEP, EMIDELTIDE has been explored in animal studies for potentially protecting against cancer and improving motor ability after stroke. Another realm of interest is how emideltide might help with curbing withdrawal after addiction.

A 1984 study found emideltide injections helped reduce the severity of opioid withdrawal symptoms in 97 percent of people with addiction along with alcohol withdrawal symptoms in 87 percent of people with alcohol addiction. Although more peptide injections were needed in people with opiate addiction.

In 1998, researchers gave a single injection of emideltide to seven people with opioid dependence. While they reported improvements in opioid withdrawal, the team also noted “it remains an open question whether DSIP could be an effective alternative detoxification approach.” Some of the issues were the small sample size, lack of a placebo group for proper comparison, and that each person knew they were getting an emideltide injection. The lack of randomization makes it hard to say for sure that a treatment actually worked or if people subjectively felt better because they knew they were getting treatment.

WE KNOW EMIDELTIDE doesn’t act like a sedative that slows down the brain and body’s function. But how the peptide exactly works is still up for debate.

The peptide is often considered a sleep-regulator rather than a suppressant. We know emideltide doesn’t act like a sedative or sleeping pill that slows down the brain and body’s function. But how the peptide exactly works is still up for debate.

One possible mechanism is that the peptide increases delta-wave activity. These are your slowest brain waves at 0.5 to 4 Hz that are indicative of deep sleep. Deep sleep is linked to better memory, energy, and all that good stuff you get from a good night’s sleep. There may also be some neuroendocrine effects going on that could explain why people feel calm enough to pass out. A small study back in the ’80s injected a single dose of emideltide in 11 guys and found lower levels of ACTH, a hormone that stimulates your adrenal glands to release the stress hormone cortisol (though this mechanism has been disputed).

The FDA advisory meeting relied on studies that focused on emideltide’s cross-talk with the opioid system in the brain. While the peptide doesn’t directly interact with opioid receptors, evidence points to emideltide somehow being able to trigger the release of endorphins. This may also provide painkiller properties (which is why it’s been proposed as a way to suppress alcohol and opioid withdrawal syndrome).

EMIDELTIDE WILL PROBABLY stay banned—and found only through the sketchy gray market—because there’s not enough information to know if emideltide might do more harm than good.

Since emideltide interacts with the opioid system, there is a possibility that it could lead to addiction. Right now, there are no animal or human studies that prove emideltide wouldn’t worsen addictive behaviors.

There’s also not enough data on whether repeated emideltide use would cause other safety issues. While the small sleep trials did not report any serious side effects, withdrawal studies noted headaches, nausea, and vertigo. After the second injection, some had low blood pressure although the researchers also said it’s unclear whether those symptoms were because of the peptide or from withdrawal altogether.

Jocelyn Solis-Moreira, MS is the associate health & fitness for Men's Health and has previously written for CNN, Scientific American, Popular Science, and National Geographic before joining the brand. When she's not working, she's doing circus arts or working towards the perfect pull-up.

#4913. Ledger Brings Clear Signing to Solana, Ending 'Blind' Signing for Jupiter Swaps

Ledger Brings Clear Signing to Solana, Ending 'Blind' Signing for Jupiter Swaps

Ledger has brought clear signing to Solana, allowing users of its hardware wallets to read exactly what they are approving before they sign a transaction.

"Until now, most Solana DeFi on a Ledger meant signing blind," the company tweeted on Monday. Users can now verify a swap on decentralized exchange aggregator Jupiter "in plain words, showing what you send and what you get before you approve," it said.

Would you approve a swap you can’t read?Clear Signing is finally live on Solana. Until now, most Solana DeFi on a Ledger meant signing blind. Now you can verify a Jupiter swap, in plain words, showing what you send and what you get before you approve.

Clear Signing now covers… pic.twitter.com/BuW3m7y5GG

Blind signing, in which users approve transactions displayed as unreadable data, has long been a weak point for hardware wallet users, since it leaves them unable to spot a malicious request. Ian Rogers, Ledger's chief human agency officer, likened it to "signing a blank check" in a presentation at Solana's Breakpoint conference.

A Jupiter swap that previously meant scrolling through 13 hard-to-read screens on a Ledger device now takes three, showing what the user is giving, what they're getting and who they're dealing with, Rogers said. "It's a huge step for every Solana user," he added.

Clear signing now covers nine Solana apps and 18 of the 22 most common actions users take on the network, according to Ledger. It was built with the Solana Foundation and Anza, the developer of Solana's Agave validator client, with Jupiter and lending protocol Kamino as the first supported apps.

Rogers also pitched the company's tools for AI agents. Ledger's approach is that an agent "never holds the keys; you do," he said, comparing it to a teenager who has to ask parents for the family car. The agent requests approval when it needs to move funds, and the user signs off on a Ledger device.

Because that means an agent has to stop and wait, Ledger last month launched Agent Intents, a single queue where approval requests collect so agents can keep working while users approve them when convenient. It works with Pay.sh, a service that lets AI agents pay for APIs on Solana, Rogers said.

He also showed off Ledger Atom, the touchscreen device based on Android's open-source code that Ledger announced last month. When it's time to sign, the device switches its screen to Ledger's secure chip, so secrets stay hidden from Android, Rogers said. Atom will be the first hardware beyond the Solana Seeker phone to carry the Seeker platform, bringing its more than 1,500 apps to the device, he added.

Ledger has previously teamed up with MoonPay on tools to let users control AI agents' crypto spending with its hardware wallets.

The launch comes weeks after Ledger investigated a suspected $87 million drain of user funds, which it later said was tied in all confirmed cases to a reseller, Cryptobilis.

#4912. Harvard, Dartmouth keep Bitcoin ETF stakes steady as fund gains 42% in Q3

Harvard’s gold ETF share count rose nearly 20-fold in Q3, while Dartmouth kept its positions in Solana and Ether staking ETFs unchanged.

Harvard University and Dartmouth College reported unchanged share counts in BlackRock’s spot Bitcoin exchange-traded fund (ETF) at the end of the third quarter as a Bitcoin rally lifted the value of both stakes.

Harvard held 3.04 million shares of the iShares Bitcoin Trust ETF (IBIT), worth about $144.1 million at the end of September, while Dartmouth reported 201,531 shares valued at $9.5 million, according to Form 13F filings with the US Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC).

IBIT gained about 42% in Q3 as Bitcoin (BTC) rallied nearly 43%. Both posted their strongest quarterly gains of 2026 through September.

Harvard has reported the same IBIT share count since the end of March, after cutting its holdings by 43% in Q1 and 21% in Q4 2025. Dartmouth has reported the same 201,531 shares since first disclosing its Bitcoin ETF position in Q4 2025.

Harvard increased its share count in the State Street’s SPDR Gold Shares ETF (GLD) nearly 20-fold in Q3, to 1.16 million from 58,862. The position was worth $442.2 million at the end of September.

Dartmouth again reported 304,803 shares of the Bitwise Solana Staking ETF and 178,148 shares of the Grayscale Ethereum Staking ETF. The positions were worth $4.9 million and $4.5 million, respectively, at quarter-end.

The steady university holdings contrasted with moves by major financial companies. Jane Street reduced its IBIT shares by 19% in Q3, while JPMorgan Chase increased the number of shares it held by 9.6%, according to their latest quarterly filings.

#4910. DBS, JPMorgan’s Kinexys launch 24/7 transfers of tokenized deposits

Ant International is the first customer to use the service, which lets clients transfer US dollar deposits between Singapore and the US.

DBS and JPMorgan launched a service on Monday that lets clients transfer tokenized US dollar deposits between the two banks around the clock.

Tokenized deposits are bank deposits represented on the blockchain. The service connects DBS Token Services with JPMorgan’s Kinexys platform for transfers between Singapore and the US, according to a press release.

Ant International became the first client to use service to manage its cash across markets.

The launch supports the Monetary Authority of Singapore’s Project BLOOM, which aims to make it easier to use tokenized deposits and regulated stablecoins for payments in Singapore and across borders. The central bank launched the project in October 2025.

DBS and JPMorgan co-lead Project BLOOM’s working group on transfers between blockchain platforms. The two banks first revealed plans to develop a blockchain-based tokenization framework for onchain transfers in November 2025.

More than half of the 422 business leaders DBS surveyed across 11 markets said their companies used tokenized deposits or planned to do so, according to the bank’s Oct. 28 report.

#4909. Morning Minute: Crypto Hits Extreme Fear as Bitcoin Plunges

Morning Minute: Crypto Hits Extreme Fear as Bitcoin Plunges

Morning Minute is a daily newsletter written by Tyler Warner. The analysis and opinions expressed are his own and do not necessarily reflect those of Decrypt. And check out our daily news show 'FOMO HOUR' covering all of the top stories and market action.

😱 Crypto Hits Extreme Fear as Bitcoin Plunges

The Crypto Fear and Greed Index printed 10 on Sunday. A month ago it read 63, which is Greed. A week ago it was 28. It has been sliding every day since, and 10 is about as low as that gauge goes.

This weekend’s price action certainly was extremely fearful. Bitcoin touched roughly $62,000 before stabilizing in the mid-60s, down about a quarter from its early-October high and down something like 9% in three days. ETH fell under $1,850. Solana, XRP and dogecoin are all down more than 25% on the month. Bitcoin is now trading below its 200-week moving average, a level that has historically acted as the floor beneath every cycle, and it lost its 50-week average a week earlier.

It wasn’t just majors. Zcash fell to $800. Hype fell to $62. VVV went sub-$17. Nothing was spared.

The billion-dollar question of course—why is Bitcoin (and crypto) selling off so hard?

CZ addressed the question directly on Sunday, saying “No one knows for sure. Correlation does not prove causation.” But he did give potential reasons.

No one knows for sure. Correlation does not prove causation. Here are some potential reasons.

Money shifting to the Anthropic IPO: https://t.co/jYwVi4huAY

ETF outflows: U.S. spot Bitcoin ETFs saw about $1.9 billion in outflows over seven trading… pic.twitter.com/XL2aB1ZL9W

— CZ 🔶 BNB (@cz_binance) November 15, 2026

The reasons he listed: money rotating into the Anthropic IPO, ETF outflows, a run of security incidents including the Binance hack itself plus drained iPhone-linked wallets, a Chrome exploit and a Microsoft outage, hot US inflation raising the odds of another Fed hike and strengthening the dollar, and political uncertainty after midterm results that point toward stricter crypto policy.

Those are all pretty valid. Some of those overhangs remove soon (Anthropic IPO), some will fade over time (political uncertainty), but the cyber attack threat will remain until Bitcoin and broader crypto can present a real solution. And if we are faced with a hiking cycle in 2027 due to inflation, that will certainly be a continued headwind.

So where does this leave us? Nvidia reports Tuesday with an interim CEO while its former one openly campaigns to return. The Fed meets December 9 with inflation running hot enough to put another hike in play. And Bitcoin is sitting under a technical level that has held for three cycles.

Extreme fear has marked bottoms before. It has also marked the middle of declines. Now we wait for something other than sentiment to turn.

#4908. The Potential Benefits of Using an Anglo First Name

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 14, 2026 | Reviewed by Margaret Foley

Many Psychology Today readers are familiar with the dispiriting findings of studies conducted by social scientists: When they apply for jobs or housing, persons with ethnic minority names (like Lakeisha and Abdullah) are generally treated less well than persons with majority group names (like Daniel and Jennifer).

Will foreigners in the United States and Americans with foreign-sounding names be treated better if they adopt an Anglicized name? Would the immensely successful Nvidia executive Jensen Huang have been less successful in business if he had used his birth name, Jen-Hsun?

Two University of Kansas researchers, Xian Zhao and Monica Biernat, have conducted three studies that investigate the potential benefits that may accrue to foreigners who choose to use an Anglo name.

In the first study, 102 Americans—almost all of them White—looked at four photos of Chinese students who were attending college in the United States. Some participants saw photos that were labeled with an Anglo name like Andrew. Others saw photos that showed a Chinese name like Xiang. The participants used a 7-point scale to rate the similarity of each target “to Americans.”

On average, the pictured individuals with Anglo names were rated as more similar to Americans, and the ones with Chinese names were rated as less similar. The difference wasn’t large—about a half-point on the 7-point scale—but it was statistically significant.

In the second study, more than 400 college professors around the U.S. received an e-mail from “a student from China” who asked to meet and talk about the possibility of assisting with the professor’s research. Some professors received an e-mail in which the student introduced himself as Xian Zhao. Others received an e-mail in which the student introduced himself as “Xian Zhao, but you can call me by my English name Alex.” In every other way, the e-mail requests were identical.

The different names affected the likelihood of getting a response. The “Alex” version received responses from 62 percent of the professors, while the “Xian” version received a response from just 53 percent.

In the third study, 184 undergraduates—almost all of them White—listened to a four-minute mini-lecture about affective forecasting. The lecturer introduced himself as a student from China who was pursuing a doctorate in social psychology at the university. In one version of the recording, the lecturer said, “Hi everyone. My name is Jian.” In another version, he said, “Hi everyone. My name is John” (an Anglicized version of Jian). In every other way, the recorded lectures were identical.

The participants were told to listen carefully to the lecture and then candidly evaluate the speaker because the department was “screening and selecting Ph.D. students to be instructors.” The participants also indicated the degree to which they support components of multiculturalism (wanting to learn from other cultural groups, for example) and the degree to which they believe that people who come to the United States should change their behavior to be more like Americans.

The participants evaluated the lecturer more favorably when he used an Anglo name and less favorably when he used his original Chinese name, but only among participants who rejected multiculturalism and believed foreigners should assimilate to local norms.

The opposite pattern was observed among participants who endorsed multiculturalism and did not endorse assimilationism. Those participants actually evaluated the lecturer more favorably when he used his original Chinese name.

The differences observed in these studies were not large, but the bottom line seems clear: Chinese students in the United States—and perhaps other international students—can potentially benefit by choosing to use an Anglo name, especially in more parochial parts of the country.

This strategy, however, may also have a downside. In an earlier post, I reported the results of a 2018 study conducted by the same two Kansas researchers. In that study, Chinese students in the U.S. who adopted an Anglicized name reported slightly lower levels of self-esteem.

It seems that adopting an Anglo name can be a double-edged sword for foreigners. Some employers may evaluate Jian Li slightly lower than John Lee, but Jian may feel somewhat better about himself than John does.

Zhao, X., & Biernat, M. (2017). Welcome to the U.S. but change your name? Adopting Anglo names and discrimination. Journal of Experimental Social Psychology, 70, 59-68.

#4906. BTC price tags 18-week low into Nvidia earnings: Three things to know in Bitcoin this week

Bitcoin traders awaited Nvidia Q3 earnings after BTC price selling pressure saw a dip below $62,000 at the weekend.

Bitcoin (BTC) traded around $64,500 early Monday after a sell-off in thin weekend trading sent it to a four-month low of $61,950.

Data from TradingView showed that BTC/USD fell to $61,950 on Bitstamp, a level last seen on July 13. The pair briefly rebounded to $66,000 before easing to around $64,500 during Monday’s Asian trading session.

BTC/USD one-hour chart. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

The drop pushed Bitcoin below its 200-week simple moving average (SMA) of $68,186. This cemented concerns about Bitcoin’s rebound to $87,000, which began in August, as flipping the 200-week SMA from resistance to support has been a prerequisite for previous bear-market recoveries.

“Deviating below the 200-week SMA has historically been the key to building out a Bear Market bottom formation,” crypto trader and analyst Rekt Capital said Saturday on X.

BTC/USD one-week chart with 200-week SMA. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

From Friday to 10 am UTC on Monday, Bitcoin open interest fell by around 9.1% to $20.98 billion, according to onchain analytics platform CryptoQuant.

A CryptoQuant contributing analyst drew comparisons to similar open interest drawdowns in February and June, considering whether Bitcoin was forming a local bottom.

“If the flush is complete, a bottoming zone may be near. If shorts keep piling up, even a modest rally could squeeze them. The $59K-$60K summer low remains the next key test,” the analyst wrote on Sunday.

Bitcoin open interest. Source: CryptoQuant

Bitcoin perpetual futures funding rates briefly turned negative near the weekend low, meaning traders with short positions paid those with long positions.

Bitcoin funding rates. Source: CryptoQuant

Nvidia remains in the spotlight this week as the tech giant reports third-quarter results after US markets close Tuesday amid ongoing controversy over former CEO Jensen Huang.

On Tuesday, interim CEO Colette Kress will lead the company’s earnings call. Nvidia previously forecast quarterly revenue of around $108 billion, and analysts expect earnings per share to nearly double year over year.

Huang is reportedly considering a return to Nvidia. He resigned last week after reports that he had an affair with a chip executive whom US authorities suspect of spying for China. Beijing denied the espionage allegation.

The Financial Times reported that a “war room” of tech CEOs and others was lobbying for his reinstatement, while an open letter calling for his return has garnered more than 165,000 signatories, including Tesla and SpaceX CEO Elon Musk.

“A petition is underway to reinstate Jensen and I’m signing it too,” Musk said in an X post on Friday.

The efforts face friction from US Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent, who said that Nvidia should hire its next CEO from outside the company.

“This makes me think that as the board conducts a search for a new leader that ​they need to go outside the ​company, that just [with] what was going on inside ‌the ⁠company, they need a dramatic shakeup,” he told Fox News.

Nvidia shares closed Friday at $162.85, down 12.7% for the week. On Thursday, shares fell to $148.80, their lowest since mid-2025, before recovering some of their losses.

Nvidia stock one-week chart. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

Nvidia headlines rattled tech stocks and crypto markets last week. Investors will be watching the company’s outlook for signs of how demand for AI chips is holding up.

Fed officials will speak throughout the week as traders look for clues about the December rate decision.

#4904. Singapore, Germany test cross-border FX settlement with CBDCs

Project Simbolon aims to reduce the cost and time of cross-border settlement and promote common standards for payments and FX

The Monetary Authority of Singapore (MAS) and the Deutsche Bundesbank used wholesale central bank digital currencies (CBDCs) to test settlement of euro–Singapore dollar trades across their separate digital asset test environments.

As part of Project Simbolon, the two central banks used payment-versus-payment settlement, meaning the final transfers of each currency depended on the final transfer of the other, MAS said in a Monday press release. Zero-knowledge proofs allowed each system to verify whether settlement conditions were met without directly integrating the central bank ledgers.

The project aims to make cross-border transfers between Singapore and Germany faster and cheaper. It also aims to promote common standards for cross-border payments, foreign exchange and securities transactions tied to tokenized assets.

The test was the first milestone under a November 2025 memorandum of understanding between the two central banks on tokenization and cross-border settlement. It also advances broader efforts to make tokenized financial markets more efficient under Project Guardian, a tokenization initiative MAS launched in May 2022.

“Project Simbolon paves the way for eliminating settlement risk in global foreign exchange transactions,” said Sabine Mauderer, first deputy governor of the Bundesbank.

In September, the Eurosystem launched Pontes, a system that lets financial institutions settle wholesale tokenized asset transactions in central bank money.

#4903. 'Nobody Knows Who's in Charge': Boris Johnson Stands by Bitcoin Skepticism at Breakpoint

'Nobody Knows Who's in Charge': Boris Johnson Stands by Bitcoin Skepticism at Breakpoint

Former UK Prime Minister Boris Johnson is sticking to his doubts about Bitcoin, telling a room full of crypto developers that he still can't see who is in charge of the world's largest cryptocurrency.

"As far as I can see, nobody knows who's in charge, apart from Yakimoto Nakasori, and I don't know where to find him," Johnson said at Solana's Breakpoint conference in London, deliberately mangling the name of Bitcoin's pseudonymous creator, Satoshi Nakamoto. "If six billion got stolen from some cryptocurrency, I wouldn't know who to apply to, or how to get the money back. That's my problem."

Johnson was asked about a column he wrote in March for the Daily Mail, in which he called Bitcoin a "giant Ponzi scheme." The column drew pushback from the crypto industry, including Strategy executive chairman Michael Saylor, who tweeted that Bitcoin has no issuer, promoter or guaranteed return.

Bitcoin is not a Ponzi scheme. A Ponzi requires a central operator promising returns and paying early investors with funds from later ones. Bitcoin has no issuer, no promoter, and no guaranteed return—just an open, decentralized monetary network driven by code and market demand.

— Michael Saylor (@saylor) March 13, 2026

On stage, Johnson admitted he is "not a great crypto buff" and holds little, if any, crypto himself. His view was shaped by a man he met in his Oxfordshire village who had lost thousands and kept paying more in the hope of recovering his losses, he said. "If the search for the greater fool had reached the wilds of South Oxfordshire, then probably there was something not quite right about this thing," Johnson said.

He also questioned the name itself, noting that "crypto" comes from the Greek for "hidden." "I don't think it's a good sell to tell people that this thing is concealed," he said.

Johnson drew a sharp line between Bitcoin and the technology behind it. "Please don't think that my Luddite anxiety about crypto in any way means that I wish to slow down the development of blockchain, or the lead that London should rightly have in that sort of technology," he said. When it comes to blockchain and tokenizing assets, the UK has "got to be at the forefront," he added.

Moderator Gareth Jenkinson, head of multimedia at The Block, pushed back when Johnson suggested Bitcoin was valuable to drug dealers, arguing that blockchains are far more traceable than cash. Asked by Johnson who guarantees Bitcoin, Jenkinson replied: "No one has to guarantee it. That's the beauty of the protocol."

Lord Ross Kempsell, a Conservative member of the House of Lords who previously worked for Johnson, said more than 8% of UK adults now hold some form of crypto asset, and that institutional adoption of blockchains is growing in the City of London. That ownership figure has fallen, however: the Financial Conduct Authority's latest consumer research, conducted in August and September 2025, found 8% of UK adults own crypto, down from 12% in 2024, though still double the level in 2021.

The UK is "on the precipice of creating a lot of regulation" for the sector, Kempsell said, pointing to the FCA's work on its regulatory perimeter and the Bank of England's coming stablecoin framework. "I share Boris's anxieties about the risks," he said, but within that perimeter, "there's a lot of scope here for a high growth industry."

In June, the FCA finalized its rulebook for crypto firms ahead of a mandatory regime due to take effect in late 2027, days after the Bank of England eased its stablecoin rules, scrapping planned individual holding caps in favor of a £40 billion issuance limit per coin. The Bank has since been handed a legal duty to foster stablecoin innovation, and it has set out a shared vision for tokenization with the FCA.

Kempsell also argued that being outside the EU's Markets in Crypto-Assets (MiCA) regulation allows the UK to set its own rules for crypto. The EU itself is set to revise MiCA in 2027 to cover foreign stablecoin issuers.

#4901. HKMA, Swift explore tokenized deposits for cross-border bank payments

HKMA and Swift will explore linking Project Ensemble with Swift’s ledger for cross-border payments using tokenized deposits in multiple currencies.

The Hong Kong Monetary Authority (HKMA) and Swift will explore connecting their payment infrastructure to support cross-border transfers using tokenized bank deposits, the authority said Monday.

The work will examine links between Swift’s blockchain-based ledger and the settlement infrastructure of Project Ensemble. Participating banks include Bank of China (Hong Kong), Standard Chartered Bank (Hong Kong) and HSBC, according to the announcement.

Tokenized deposits are bank balances represented on a blockchain. They remain claims on the issuing commercial bank, unlike central bank digital currencies, which are claims on a central bank. The proposed Swift connection would explore how separate payment systems could work together across borders in multiple currencies.

The collaboration follows a Nov. 2 upgrade to EnsembleTX, the pilot phase of Project Ensemble, enabling banks to settle tokenized deposit transactions around the clock using tokenized central bank money. The pilot launched in November 2025 and initially relied on Hong Kong’s dollar-denominated real-time gross settlement system.

In July, Swift said its blockchain-based ledger was ready for initial use and that it was preparing to pilot tokenized cross-border payments with 17 major banks, including Citi, DBS and HSBC.
