#4900. How to Increase Psychological Safety Amid Chronic Illness

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 14, 2026 | Reviewed by Margaret Foley

How safe do you feel in this moment? Pause what you are doing, close your eyes, take a couple of deep breaths, and tune in to your body. Start at your feet and work up, scanning your body with your mind’s eye. Are there places where your muscles feel tense? Is your breathing shallow? Is it hard to tune in to your body because your mind will not settle? Don’t try to change anything right now. Just notice the sensations you are having in this moment.

Psychological safety is defined as “a person’s capacity to experience the self, the body, and the interpersonal environment as sufficiently safe to sustain openness, affect regulation, and adaptive engagement" (Poli & Miccoli, 2026). The body “is one of the main channels through which safety, threat, and dysregulation are experienced" (Poli & Miccoli, 2026).

Safety is a continuum. We feel safe (and unsafe) by degrees. Typically, we have conscious awareness of when we are at either end of this continuum. We know when we feel safe, for example, when our muscles are relaxed, and our thoughts are pleasant. At those moments, we enjoy the positive sensations in our body that let us know, “I am happy. I am safe.” Conversely, we know when we feel very unsafe. Our heart rate goes up; we may feel nauseated or light-headed; breathing may feel labored. We interpret these sensations as a lack of safety.

The middle of the safety continuum is where we spend the vast majority of our time. Without the extremes of body sensations that occur at either end of the continuum, we often tune out the body, putting it outside of our consciousness. Paying attention to body sensations in the middle of the safety continuum can lead to a deeper relationship with the body and greater psychological safety.

Many people living with illness describe their relationship with their body as challenging. Lack of trust in the body, anger at the body, and alienation from the body are all sequelae of chronic illness (Taylor, Morton, & Cogan, 2026). Questions like “How safe do you feel in this moment?” are complicated by the bodily mistrust that chronic illness perpetuates. Illness “disrupts...the felt sense of psychological safety" (Taylor, Morton, & Cogan, 2026).

In order to grow psychological safety, we must have an intentional relationship with our body. Take a few moments and ascertain what that relationship currently looks like. Write a few sentences responding to each of the following statements. How true or false does each statement feel to you?

Now that you have a sense of your current relationship with your body, write a few sentences about how you would like this relationship to be different. Some ideas might be: “I’d like to feel less hostile toward my body” or “I’d like to appreciate my body for the things it can do, rather than be solely focused on the ways it disappoints me.”

If you were seeking to improve a relationship with another person, you’d likely (a) spend intentional time with them, and (b) have open communication with them. Similarly, in improving your relationship with your body, you will want to carve out intentional time together and increase honest communication (both listening and feeling heard).

In order to relate harmoniously with our body, we have to get to know it. In order to know it, we have to spend intentional time with it. What you do during this time is less important than your non-distracted presence. You could take a shower or bath, go for a walk, do a yoga session, or simply sit quietly and follow your breath. You want to be present during your time with your body. Notice without trying to change the sensations that come up.

You’re also going to want to communicate with your body during these “being with” sessions. You can speak aloud or in your mind, or journal. Just put words to the sensations you have, being as honest as possible. You might say, “This is really stupid. I feel fidgety and anxious. It’s frustrating to be with you, body.” Perhaps you have a different experience and say, “I feel vulnerable and shy. I’m not used to showing up in my body like this.” It may be that you feel a lessening of tension, such that you say, “Wow, this feels nice to be with my body, undistracted by other concerns.”

Open your mind, too, to the ways your body is communicating with you during these sessions. Be curious. Maybe your body is whispering, “I’m tired. I do the best I can, and I don’t feel very appreciated.” Perhaps your body is screaming, “I hurt! I’m angry and agitated, and I need some relief!” It’s possible that your body is saying, “I feel you tuning in to me, and I have been waiting a long time for your presence.”

Make it a habit to communicate with your body in the ways described above. Your body is constantly changing, and your experiences of tuning in to it will be variable. Stay open and curious; let yourself be surprised.

Psychological safety depends upon a connection between body and psyche. Working to deepen that connection is an important tool in managing chronic illness.

Poli, A., & Miccoli, M. (2026). The Role of Self-Compassion and Body Perception in Predicting Psychological Safety. Journal of Clinical Medicine, 15(13), 5177.

Taylor, P., Morton, L., & Cogan, N. (2026). Feeling unsafe in one’s own body: The impact of illness on psychological safety and social engagement. International Journal of Environmental Research and Public Health, 23(2), 148.

#4899. ‘The Gilded Age’ Star Ben Ahlers and Todd Snyder Know the Power of a Good Uniform

For the label’s latest campaign, the 30-year-old actor takes fall style to Scotland’s Isle of Islay.

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

TODD SNYDER makes some of our favorite menswear, and for its latest collaboration, the New York brand crossed the pond to Scotland. The draw? Lagavulin, the distillery that’s been making smoky Scotch whisky on the island of Islay for more than 200 years.

The partnership marks Snyder’s 15th anniversary, with clothes that borrow from Islay’s rugged landscape. Standouts include a brown suede jacket lined in Lagavulin’s own tartan and Fair Isle zip-ups in the earthy shades of the Scottish moors. Pretty great reasons to look forward to sweater weather.

Fronting the campaign is Ben Ahlers. As footman-turned-businessman Jack Trotter on The Gilded Age, the 30-year-old actor knows his way around heritage menswear. Still, these relaxed layers are pretty easy to picture on the streets of New York. We caught up with him about what playing Jack taught him about getting dressed—and which piece he’d bring home.

MEN’S HEALTH: You spend a lot of time in period costumes on The Gilded Age. Has anything from Jack’s wardrobe made its way into how you dress off-screen?

BEN AHLERS: Jack has a consistency to the way that he dresses. As a footman, he had a uniform, and as a young businessman, he has a pretty steady throughline through his looks—a base structure with really well-made clothing and then accents like a tie or a tie pin or a ring that can allow for a sense of creative expression. I find that I like a level of consistency in my own personal dress as well.

More than anything, the greatest benefit of living inside of the clothing that Kasia [Walicka Maimone, the show’s costume designer] and her incredible team make for us every year is this awareness of the way clothes not only make you present to the world but also how they make you feel within them.

MH: Is there a menswear trend from that era you wish more guys would embrace today?

BA: I would love to see some of the creativity come out in menswear and in the accent work, like a tie pin. Some of the tie pins we have on this show are just so well-crafted and specific. The heads of canes the men wore back then also have such an artistry to them, and there aren’t a lot of avenues for that sort of expression in modern menswear. It’d be nice to see that creative voice or risk-taking come through in the subtle accents that we wear in our day-to-day life.

MH: Your role in Death of a Salesman got people talking about your physique. How did you prepare?

BA: For Death of a Salesman, I knew earlier on in the development process that they wanted to go the direction that they did for the top of the show, and so I knew that I was going to be kind of exposed every night. I really tried to go to the gym during the rehearsal process because I knew once we opened the show that it was going to be hard to stay as active—the show was just so draining.

The couple of months up to opening, I really made a practice of five days a week of cross-training, lifting, running, and yoga, and trying to keep my body as nimble and buoyant as possible, because the subject matter and the daily grind of showing up to the theater every day can really take a toll.

During the run, a lot of my focus was on rest, recovery, sauna, cold plunge, diet, stretching, because I’d already built the foundation of the physicality. Throughout the five-month run, it came down to being sure that I was healthy and recovering as best as I could.

MH: What happened between takes on the Todd Snyder shoot that we’d never know from looking at the campaign photos?

BA: Between takes, I had a blast with Boo George, our photographer. He is such a character and one of the best energies on set, and really delivered in the work. The whole crew from Todd Snyder and Lagavulin were so supportive, and it was such a fun little escape for all of us to exist on this island in the most beautiful landscape imaginable.

I really enjoyed getting to know a lot of the locals there. Many of the people there worked at the distillery, and I got to see firsthand how the whisky was actually made. People were so proud to share their culture as well as their craft with the world.

MH: Which piece from the collection would you wear back home in New York, and what would you pair it with?

BA: My favorite piece from the collection is the Todd Snyder x Lagavulin suede stable jacket, inspired by the texture and color of the mossy peat that coats Islay. It’s a perfect fall jacket, and I’d pair it with Todd Snyder’s relaxed barrel jean in khaki, which I think would really pop.

This interview has been edited for length and clarity.

#4898. Binance Pay Later offers credit backed by Bitcoin, Ether and BNB holdings

Eligible users can access up to 1,000 USDC in credit against their Bitcoin, Ether or BNB for purchases via Binance Card or QR Pay.

Binance said Monday it would offer eligible users in Argentina and Mexico up to 1,000 USDC in spending credit through Pay Later, a payment feature backed by their cryptocurrency holdings.

Pay Later allows eligible users to access up to 1,000 USDC (USDC) in spending power against their Bitcoin (BTC), Ether (ETH) and BNB (BNB) token holdings as collateral, without selling the underlying assets, Binance said in an announcement shared with Cointelegraph. Borrowing is limited to 50% of the eligible collateral’s value, meaning $2,000 in BTC is required to access a maximum $1,000 credit line.

Purchases are interest-free until the repayment deadline on the first of the following month. Binance’s published FAQ lists a one-day grace period and interest of 0.1% per day, compounding daily, on overdue balances.

Borrowing lets users make purchases without first selling their crypto, but falling prices can put those holdings at risk. Binance can liquidate collateral when the outstanding loan reaches 90% of its value, even before repayment is due. It will initially launch for users in Argentina and Mexico, with a gradual rollout planned for additional markets.

“Traditional consumer credit often relies on income documentation and credit history, which leaves people who hold assets but have no formal credit file with fewer options,” said Thomas Gregory, VP of payments and fiat at Binance, adding that asset-backed credit offers an alternative.

#4897. The 17 Most Beautiful Ski Resorts to Visit This Winter—Including Three in the US

When winter arrives, the world’s most idyllic mountain towns transform into playgrounds for serious adrenaline seekers. But, as the most beautiful ski resorts prove, the appeal of an extraordinary alpine escape extends well beyond its slopes. Why kick off your ski boots just anywhere when you could do so in a castle-like hotel in the Swiss Alps or a secluded Japanese ryokan tucked among snow-covered forests? Sure, the world-renowned runs are worth traveling for, but sometimes so are the ski hotels themselves. Think Michelin-starred restaurants, award-winning spas, art-filled interiors, and architecture that makes the most of its dramatic surroundings. Here, AD rounds up 17 of the most beautiful ski resorts to visit this winter, whether you’re an alpine ace, a beginner ski bum, or purely an après enthusiast.

Getting to Zaborin Ryokan is an adventure in itself, which means you know it’ll be good. After landing at New Chitose Airport in Sapporo, it’s a two-hour drive to the ryokan, which is hidden in the Hanazono woods of Hokkaido. Arriving at the property feels like discovering some sort of secretive lair from a Bond flick—a series of low-lying villas covered in snow suddenly reveal themselves. Built on natural hot springs, each of the villas has its own indoor and outdoor onsen, plus floor-to-ceiling windows and a private balcony with views of Mount Yōtei, the area’s dormant volcano, in the distance. Make an appointment with the in-house matcha master and prepare your taste buds for a multicourse Japanese kaiseki meal. If you can tear yourself away from your absurdly serene suite long enough to ski or snowboard, the ryokan will arrange your transfer to and from Hanazono Niseko ski area, just seven minutes away by car.

Modernist design meets mountainside adventure at Pendry Park City, a ski-in, ski-out luxury resort where “hitting the slopes” means basically just stepping outside. Tucked in Canyons Village at Park City Mountain—the largest ski resort in the US, with over 7,000 skiable acres spread across two connected mountains—this slopeside sanctuary is right next to the brand-new, high-speed Sunrise Gondola. After a day appreciating the powder, guests gather at Après Pendry for fondue and crème brulée espresso martinis either by the fire or outside. Alternatively, they head up to the rooftop for a dip in the year-round heated pool and hot tub, plus immaculate mountain views.

Located in St. Moritz, Switzerland, the Carlton Hotel boasts 60 luxurious suites that blend old-world grandeur with contemporary elements. The resort is also home to the Michelin-starred restaurant Da Vittorio.

The Gstaad Palace in Switzerland opened in 1913 and has catered to jet-setters and stars such as Elizabeth Taylor, Grace Kelly, and Marlene Dietrich throughout its long history. The castlelike hotel overlooks the streets of Gstaad, which are filled with high-end boutiques.

The Pines Lodge, a RockResort is a rustic Colorado retreat with ski-in, ski-out access set above Beaver Creek Village. After a long day on the slopes, guests can enjoy views of the Rockies from the acclaimed Grouse Mountain Grill.

Architect Mario Botta designed the striking spa at the Tschuggen Grand Hotel in Arosa, Switzerland. The alpine retreat, which was built in 1929, features 130 rooms and suites with opulent interiors by Carlo Rampazzi.

Designed by architect Sybille de Margerie, the intimate Cheval Blanc Courchevel in Courchevel, France, is the ultimate in alpine luxury. The resort, which is owned by LVMH chairman Bernard Arnault, is also filled with blue-chip artwork by Michel Othoniel, Xavier Veillant, and Bruno Peinado, whose Trojan Horse sculpture is seen here.

Founded by railway tycoon William Cornelius Van Horne in 1888, the Fairmont Banff Springs is set within Banff National Park and offers stunning views of the Canadian Rockies. The sprawling hotel, which was inspired by a Scottish baronial castle, includes 764 rooms and suites and nine restaurants and bars.

The St. Regis Aspen Resort in the Colorado Rockies received a $40 million makeover by interior architect Lauren Rottet in 2012. Set at the base of Aspen Mountain, the hotel includes 179 rooms and suites outfitted with Ralph Lauren beds and desks and marble-lined baths.

The ski-in, ski-out Hôtel des Dromonts in Avoriaz, France, was built in the 1960s by architect Jacques Labro, who was inspired by the work of Le Corbusier and Charlotte Perriand. The interiors of the distinctive angular building were recently refreshed and upgraded by designer Jocelyne Sibuet.

Munich-based firm Oikios Architect took inspiration from pine cones for the design of the AlpenGold Hotel in Davos, Switzerland. The oval façade uses over 790 curved, overlapping steel panels to create the scaled appearance. Inside, 216 tasteful guest rooms overlook the city—home to the World Economic Forum—and surrounding mountains.

Offering a unique combination of Asian-inspired design motifs and alpine rustic touches, The Chedi Andermatt is one of Switzerland’s most wonderful offerings. The boutique hotel is located 4,747 feet above sea level and includes a 25,833-square-foot spa to take advantage of when you’re not out on the slopes.

With rustic and understated architecture and interiors, Le Chalet Zanier is designed to mimic the natural beauty of the surrounding Mont Blanc mountain range. The resort is made up of a 12-room main structure as well as a separate three-bedroom private cottage. Like the snowflakes that top the roofs, no room is exactly like any of the others.

Located in a densely wooded area, Forestis is situated exactly where the Austrian monarchy established a tuberculosis sanatorium in 1912. Designed with simplicity in mind, the rich stone, wood, and glass of the hotel are inspired by the nature that surrounds it.

#4896. Strategy buys $33M in Bitcoin, spends $228M on STRC buybacks

Strategy bought 485 Bitcoin for $33.4 million and spent $228.1 million repurchasing STRC preferred shares, largely funded by new common stock sales.

Michael Saylor’s Strategy bought 485 Bitcoin for $33.4 million last week while spending nearly seven times as much repurchasing STRC preferred shares.

The company purchased the Bitcoin (BTC) at an average price of $68,887 between Monday and Sunday, bringing its holdings to 850,849 BTC, according to a Monday filing with the US Securities and Exchange Commission.

Strategy said it raised $244.7 million by selling 2.09 million common shares (MSTR), using $33.4 million to buy Bitcoin, $206.3 million to fund STRC repurchases and $5 million to its separate cash balance. It funded the remaining $21.8 million in buybacks with interest earned on its cash and short-term investments.

The company repurchased 2.28 million STRC shares during the week. STRC is Strategy’s variable-rate preferred stock, which pays regular dividends to shareholders.

Strategy reported a $4.75 billion US dollar reserve after using $18.9 million to pay preferred stock dividends. Its separate cash balance stood at $151.2 million as of Sunday.

The company also maintained STRC’s annual dividend rate at 12% for December, saying it intends to keep the rate steady until the shares demonstrate sustained trading near $100.

Strategy spent $188.3 million repurchasing STRC shares and $81.9 million buying Bitcoin the previous week.

Strategy’s MSTR stock fell 8.45% to $102.2 in Monday’s premarket trading, while STRC declined 0.78% to $99.1, according to Yahoo Finance. The losses followed MSCI’s confirmation last week that it would remove Strategy from its global equity indexes after the Nov. 30 market close.

#4894. Etsy Says Gah! The Handmade Marketplace and Playful Fashion Label Team Up on a Home Collection

Since its founding in 2015, Lisa Says Gah has become known for its bowtie-pasta cardigans, checkerboard coats, and swans. Today, the California-based label is releasing its first home collection, which has a similarly playful ethos: “The Lisa Says Gah world really naturally extends into the home,” says founder Lisa Bühler. “Home and clothing ultimately tell the story of who you are.”

A pin board and two eclectic frames from Frame Mavens comprise a third of the collection.

Founder Lisa Bühler co-judged the Etsy Design Awards and selected three winners to co-design Etsy Says Gah!, her brand’s first foray into pieces for the home.

The collection is a collaboration with Etsy, a platform Bühler often uses for her own inspiration (things that make her shout: Gah!). “When you go to Etsy, you’re always going to find something that delights you,” she says.

Lisa Says Gah has stocked independent designers alongside its own line for more than a decade. This year, Bühler co-judged the Etsy Design Awards, and selected three winners to co-design Etsy Says Gah!, a nine-piece collection exclusively available via Etsy. Bühler says she prioritized “unexpected, collected things that you just don’t automatically go to when you think of homeware.”

UK-based Cait Mellor executed Bühler’s mood board through crochet in a floral table runner, a horseshoe decal, and swan coasters.

The home collection’s foundational inspiration comes from Lisa Says Gah’s two fall lines, Pastoral Daze and Swan Dance, which pair horses and swans with paisleys and cabin-inspired knits. Bühler worked with the Etsy designers by sharing mood boards, giving notes when she wanted more of a motif or a different color combination, and trusting each maker with her own medium. “They’re the expert[s],” she says. “The process felt like really trusting and putting it in their hands.”’

Each maker applied the same mood board differently. Jacqueline Sanchez, a Houston-based ceramicist who won this year’s Grand Prize for a vase covered in more than 1,500 hand-applied scales, sculpted the swan in relief on a pale pink vase wound with green vines. She also set a horse’s head in a blue baroque cameo on a cream mug and dotted a wavy platter with tiny fruit as a nod to the brand’s food prints. “[I liked] having the freedom to incorporate elements that feel really true to my work,” says Sanchez. The vase is a piece Bühler keeps coming back to: “You would just be happy to have it on the table without flowers,” she says.

The vase is the piece Bühler keeps coming back to: “You would just be happy to have it on the table without flowers,” she says.

“Because it’s our first home collection, we wanted it to feel like something you collect over time in your house versus a uniform grouping,” says Bühler.

Cait Mellor, a UK-based seller, worked the swan and horse elements into the open grid of filet crochet: swans on a set of coasters, a horseshoe under the word Lucky on a small tapestry, and flowers down a cream table runner. Her goal is to “bring this traditional art form into a more contemporary setting.” Bühler is already picturing the coasters at dinner parties, where “everyone is going to talk about the swans.”

Emma Miller and Kristen Kellar, chosen for their fabric-wrapped frames, focused on color and pattern rather than the animals. They made a burgundy frame with black polka dots on a blue background, a frame featuring blue pixelated flowers that look almost cross-stitched, and a pin board that uses pink fabric crossed with orange ribbon.

The home collection’s foundational inspiration comes from Lisa Says Gah’s two fall lines, Pastoral Daze and Swan Dance, which pair horses and swans with paisleys and cabin-inspired knits.

Bühler didn’t want the nine pieces to look like a matched set. “Because it’s our first home collection, we wanted it to feel like something you collect over time in your house versus a uniform grouping,” she says. Each item costs less than $200, and with the holidays coming—with endless-feeling opportunities for hosting and gifting—now is a great time to start collecting.

#4893. How Does AI Make Us Stupid?

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 14, 2026 | Reviewed by Margaret Foley

Current versions of artificial intelligence have created so much well-deserved excitement. However, along with all that progress has come a wave of apprehension about the destructive potential of using AI. Fears that AI could be harming us. Fears that it might be making us stupid.

I wrote about this possibility in 2019, in a post I titled "The Second Singularity," and I am not happy to see my predictions coming true.

However, this post asks a different question. Now I am looking back to 2003 and the original publication of my book The Power of Intuition. This was before large language models (LLMs) such as ChatGPT and Claude and the generative AI technologies. My book examined different facets of intuition/expertise. Chapter 16 was titled “Smart Technology Can Make Us Stupid.” The smart technology I was writing about was simply the 2003-style information technology, not current LLMs, but the mechanisms I identified seem quite relevant today.

In that chapter, I wrote, “Information technologies are intended to make us more productive, but their use carries a penalty. The computer programs, the decision support systems, the search engines, databases, and shareware we use can diminish our intuition and expertise. They can make us stupid.” I worried that the 2003-era technologies could disable our expertise, or at least slow down our acquisition of expertise, or, more ominously, teach dysfunctional skills that interfered with expertise.

But how would that work? In 2003, I identified several possibilities.

Recognizing patterns. That’s something we are good at—spotting patterns in data, seeing trends, seeing anomalies, spotting nonevents. But information technology “helps” us by doing the analyses for us, since the high data flows make it impossible for us to examine all or even most of the data. So we are removed from direct access to the data and removed from our ability to spot patterns.

For example, in one project we conducted on weather forecasters, a technologist at a weather forecasting conference explained how his system helpfully smoothed the data in order to highlight trends. Most of the audience appreciated this assistance, but the experienced forecasters in the room winced—they wanted to see the noise because that was where turbulence was going to affect the weather picture. The smoothing was removing a critical cue.

Searching for data. That’s another important human ability—to adaptively search through data. However, information technologies often organize the search space in advance and define the basic level of detail. But in complex settings there is no basic level of detail, and the appropriate level of detail depends on what you are searching for and what you are finding. The relevant data depend on how you are interpreting the situation, a phenomenon called the relevance realization process (Vervaeke et al., 2012)—skimming through the large amounts of data to identify what matters. Information technologies can reduce their users to passive recipients of data rather than active sensemakers.

Mental models. As we gain expertise, we are acquiring richer mental models about the phenomena we are interacting with. But information technology can weaken our mental models by keeping us tethered to the original categories we started our searches and analyses with. Decision-makers may have to set up categories in advance, and subsequent events may tie them into these categories rather than allowing and supporting the evolution of categories based on new experiences.

The story behind the data. As we gain expertise, we learn more about how the data were collected, and we become sensitive to limitations in the data collection process. However, information technology often makes it difficult to trace the story of the data and to appreciate how the data were collected and synthesized—the pedigree of the data. Borders et al. (2024) presented a mental model matrix that highlighted the importance of understanding the limitations and boundary conditions of a system in order to work effectively with it. However, if we don’t know the pedigree of the data, we will struggle to identify boundary conditions.

Adaptability. This is a critical function of expertise—being able to pivot based on discoveries about what is really going on in a situation. Unfortunately, many information technologies can go off the rails if we don’t follow the prescribed procedures for interacting with them or if we don’t follow the steps that have been laid out. As a result, it can become more difficult for us to improvise when new and unexpected conditions arise. When we stray from the prescribed path, we are likely to be bombarded with error messages, discouraging us from adapting.

Active stance. With expertise comes an active stance of taking charge and making things happen. Yet information technologies can shift us into a passive stance, unthinkingly accepting the recommendations of the system. Here the link to LLMs is particularly strong, as users relying on LLMs contentedly accept their products, often without doing any kind of due diligence.

These are the attributes of expertise that information technologies seemed to jeopardize, in my 2003 analysis. Fast-forward two decades. Most of these attributes also seem threatened today by the spread of generative AI. I leave it to readers to gauge how closely the generative AI dysfunctions map onto the earlier information technology problems. At the very least, I hope this post will be helpful in unpacking what may be contributing to today’s generative AI dysfunctions. The challenge is to better understand the basis of expertise in order to find ways to safeguard and enhance it.

Borders, J., Klein, G., & Besuijen, R. (2024). Mental model matrix: Implications for system design and training. Journal of Cognitive Engineering and Decision Making, 18(2), 75-98.

Klein, G. (2004). The power of intuition. New York, NY: A Currency Book/Doubleday. Hardcover edition titled Intuition at Work (2003).

Vervaeke, J., Lillicrap, T.P., & Richards, B.A. (2012). Relevance realization and the emerging framework in cognitive science. Journal of Logic and Computation, 22(1), 79–99. https://doi.org/10.1093/logcom/exp067

#4892. Banks are banning AI agents over security... and competition concerns

AI agents are set to automate everyday banking tasks — but banks aren’t ready to give up control of the customer relationship just yet.

Banks in the United Kingdom have drawn a line over how much access artificial intelligence should have to their customers’ money.

Four banks, including HSBC, Barclays, Lloyds, NatWest and Nationwide, have all moved to block or restrict third-party AI agents from accessing customer accounts, especially where they can make transactions without customer involvement.

There’s no evidence yet of AI agents accessing UK bank accounts just yet, but large lenders are worried about what happens if an automated system mismanages customers’ money — and who would be on the hook.

The banks might have other, unspoken concerns too.

Edgars Nemše, chief executive of GenLayer Foundation, an AI-focused blockchain infrastructure project, says routine banking tasks like paying bills, moving money and finding better rates could all be handed to agents.

Unfortunately for banks, this means agents would be able to seamlessly find customers a better deal elsewhere as soon as it’s published on the web.

“For plain accounts, that removes the switching costs and makes the space even more competitive,” Nemše tells Magazine.

That scenario could threaten more than just customer loyalty. In September, Apollo chief economist Torsten Slok warned about a possible “agentic bank run.”

If swarms of agents start moving household cash from low-yield checking accounts to alternatives offering higher returns en masse, that would drain banks of the low-cost funding they rely on to make loans. And the speed and scale “would be a problem for the entire financial system.”

Related: Coinbase CEO touts agentic finance as Base tops 100M AI payments

JPMorgan Chase chief executive Jamie Dimon has also warned against allowing external agents unrestricted access to bank accounts. “You don’t want rogue agents,” he said on an October earnings call.

Is an agentic bank run coming? Source: Apollo

Regardless of short-term moves to hold back the tide, AI agents look more and more likely to revolutionize banking within a few years.

Neeraj Makin, group head of strategy analytics at Emirates NBD, predicts agents will usher in a new era of around-the-clock banking and near-instant payments as normal by 2036.

“Forget about waiting for a payment for 24 hours or inter-country payments taking three days… all of that will be in seconds, atomic,” he says, with AI agents taking over most financial decisions.

Ferdinand Dabitz, chief executive of Augustus, an AI-native banking and payments company, sees a similar shift in how customers interact with financial institutions.

“Every consumer will have 10s and every business will have 100s of agents talking to their bank,” Dabitz says.

A human interface will still remain important for managing those agents and providing a layer of trust, “especially in commercial banking,” he says.

Related: Agents can trade 24/7 — but Yat Siu warns you could lose access at any time

Emirates NBD’s developer experience team argued in July that financial institutions should build their own frameworks for agentic AI, rather than allowing individual teams to develop agents without common controls.

Its Leap framework is designed to govern how agents authenticate with core banking systems, access sensitive data and carry out actions, with audit trails, monitoring and human oversight built in.

Emirates NBD says banks should build their own agentic frameworks. Source: Emirates NBD

However there are still major concerns around the reliability of agents to make major financial decisions on their own.

A recent Bank of England and FCA survey found that while 80% of responding firms had deployed AI agents, just 2% used agents with broad autonomy, identifying data security, third-party dependencies and risks like hallucinations among their biggest concerns.

The AI takeover likely won’t happen overnight; “it will come in steps,” Nemše says.

“Today I use an agent to review my own and my company’s finances every month and queue the payments, and I approve them. The next step is the natural one: once you trust it, you let it spend small amounts without asking.”

Routine bills, subscriptions and the agent’s own running costs would be among the first tasks to become autonomous, he says.

Dabitz says corporate treasury management could be an area delegated to agents, but says banks will need to be involved in developing these early applications.

While consumers may enjoy the upside of automating their finances, who’s on the hook when an agent makes a mistake?

Nemše says banks need to offer “delegated access as a normal feature” to avoid customers sharing logins with agents. Customers could set permissions and spending limits, and agents could make low-value transactions independently while requiring approval for larger amounts.

But existing dispute processes may struggle if agents begin making thousands of transactions on behalf of each customer. “Even a small share going wrong is a lot of disputes,” he says. “The processes for handling them were built for people making a few payments a week. That part still has to be built.”

The US Office of the Comptroller of the Currency issued a supervisory framework for banks’ use of what it calls “super intelligence,” including agentic systems, on Nov. 4.

OCC’s Super Intelligence: Supervisory Framework. Source: OCC

The framework is meant to help bank examiners assess how institutions use the technology, and does not introduce new binding requirements for banks. Scrutiny should reflect the potential consequences of an agent’s actions, its level of autonomy and the degree of human oversight.

So an AI system helping a bank employee draft a report would present a different risk from one authorized to move customer funds without approval, and the banks that get that balance right are likely to come out on top.

#4891. Diana Ross at Home: 10 Photos of the Pop Diva Off-Duty

As the breakout star of the Supremes and later a solo sensation, Diana Ross proved herself a force to be reckoned with. The Detroit native’s booming career allowed the singer-slash-actor to fashion her own residences in her own singular style: A statement purple sofa, a shag-lined conversation pit, beaded curtains, and a mirrored piano were among the features that might’ve stood out elsewhere, but all appeared seamlessly at home with The Boss. As she declared in her 1979 anthem of independence: “There’s my chair / I put it there / Everything you see / Is with love and care / It’s my house and I live here.”

Through 12 number-one singles with The Supremes, six number-one hits as a solo artist, a Lifetime Achievement Award from the Recording Academy, and a Presidential Medal of Freedom, Ross apparently managed to maintain a sense of normalcy in her domestic life. “My mom is such a mom,” Ross’s daughter, actor Tracee Ellis Ross, said on The Kelly Clarkson Show. “I had a really regular upbringing.” Read on for a roundup of the icon in her offstage moments.

Ross poses on a floral sofa in her Miami suite in this photo, dated March 17, 1970. It was a big year for the songstress: She performed her final concert as lead singer of The Supremes that January, and her debut solo album was released in June.

The singer unwinds on the sofa during an interview.

When these portraits were snapped, Ross was in the middle of a nine-day run of shows at Miami’s Eden Roc hotel—her official debut solo show after a preview run in Framingham, Massachusetts. “Welcome to the ‘Let’s see if Diana Ross can do it by herself club,’” the vocalist said during the performance, journalist Shirley Eder wrote in a March 20, 1970, issue of The Philadelphia Inquirer. Eder also reported that “Diana takes a long time to unwind after a performance… she screens movies in her suite almost nightly.”

A pregnant Ross leans against her reflective piano.

Ross, eight months pregnant with Tracee in this 1972 photo, leans against the mirrored piano in her Beverly Hills home during an interview about her first film role: Billie Holiday in Lady Sings the Blues, directed by Sidney J. Furie. Ross’s depiction of the jazz singer garnered her a Golden Globe and an Oscar nomination, among other accolades. “She’s the greatest improviser I’ve ever known,” Furie told The Hollywood Reporter in 2021. “Some of the main scenes were improvised…. She was the consummate pro and not a diva at all.”

The musician at home with her daughter Rhonda.

Ross lounges in her Beverly Hills home alongside her eldest child, Rhonda, in 1972. The “Ain’t No Mountain High Enough” singer’s career was launched by Motown founder Berry Gordy, and the two shared a relationship that was romantic as well as professional. Rhonda was born in 1971 as a result of that liaison, but Ross married music executive Robert Ellis Silberstein two months into the pregnancy, and he raised Rhonda as his own. The couple welcomed two more daughters together: Tracee, who was born not long after this photo was taken, and Chudney, who was born in 1975.

Ross at home taping an appearance for an ABC special.

In the mid-1970s, The Wiz star was once again photographed on the purple sofa in her Beverly Hills living area, which was complete with a white-shag-lined conversation pit and a mirror-bedecked fireplace. According to a 1972 New York Times profile of the artist, her 6,833-square-foot home had 30-foot-tall ceilings and an Andy Warhol tapestry of Marilyn Monroe. The grounds also hosted a swimming pool and a tennis court.

Ross with daughters Tracee (left) and Rhonda.

The Ross family’s Beverly Hills pad boasted five bedrooms and nine bathrooms. According to The Los Angeles Times, the property was built in 1964 for art collector Donald Factor. Because its walls maximized space for paintings, it was reportedly known in the neighborhood as “the house with no windows.” The conversation pit abutted a window adorned with a beaded curtain, as shown in the background of this photo of Ross with Tracee and Rhonda. Both daughters followed in their mother’s footsteps as performers: Tracee is an actor (who recently won her first Daytime Emmy for the program Solo Traveling with Tracee Ellis Ross), while Rhonda was nominated for a Daytime Emmy for her role in the soap opera Another World.

Ross, flanked by her daughters, cradles infant Chudney.

A newborn Chudney also made an appearance during the mid-’70s shoot, as seen in this snapshot. Ross and Silberstein divorced in 1977, and the singer went on to marry Norwegian businessman Arne Naess Jr. in 1986. With Naess, she welcomed two sons, Ross and Evan. The “I’m Coming Out” singer and her second husband split in 2000.

In 1978, a newly single Ross welcomed Barbara Walters to her Beverly Hills home for an interview about her career and life as a single mother in front of a window overlooking the backyard pool. “Her success has brought her every material comfort,” Walters narrated in the television special. “Believe it or not, within 24 hours before we came here she completely refurnished the living room.” The television special offered a glimpse of the conversation pit, now done up with dark maroon carpet and a navy sofa with white cushions.

Ross dressed to impress for her birthday party.

The “Upside Down” singer celebrated her 38th birthday with a party at her Beverly Hills residence in March 1982, as seen in this glamorous snapshot. Later that year, she released Silk Electric, her 13th studio album, and was honored with a star on the Hollywood Walk of Fame.

Ross’s daughters were in attendance, as shown in this photo of her bustling birthday party. The 13-time Grammy nominee sold the Beverly Hills property three years later for $1.75 million. These days, the 82-year-old icon lives at Quarry Farm, a property in Greenwich, Connecticut. In 2021, she released her 25th studio album, Thank You.

#4890. Andy Baraghani’s New NYC Restaurant is Full of Design Surprises

“I’ve always been obsessed with design, since I was a kid,” says Andy Baraghani. “I love how restaurant interiors can take you to a different place.”

At Ditto—the all-day café he is opening in New York’s Flatiron—the chef, cookbook author, and YouTube star has given that obsession free rein. With each design decision—from the vintage glass-prism Holophane pendants in the front window to the intense blue (tinted to match Benjamin Moore Buckland Blue) of the rear dining area’s Venetian plaster walls—he asked himself: “Would I actually do this in my home?” If the answer was yes, it passed the test.

The interior of Ditto, with Venetian plaster walls in a shade matching Benjamin Moore Buckland Blue, and Model S22 chairs from Galvanitas.

For Andy Baraghani, design is something of a family affair. His husband, Keith Pollock, is a former AD editor and now a West Elm executive. But opening a restaurant was never part of the plan. In fact, when Michael Silber, a restaurant investor behind New York hot spots like Borgo, Penny, and Bistrot Ha, approached him with the idea of a laid-back restaurant open for breakfast, lunch, and dinner, Baraghani admits his first reaction was: “I have no interest.”

It wasn’t for lack of experience. He had started his career in restaurants—from Chez Panisse in Berkeley, where he worked as a teenager, to Estela in New York—before becoming a food editor at Bon Appetit and winning a James Beard Award for his cookbook, The Cook You Want to Be.

But he bonded with Silber over their shared love of greenmarket cooking and decorating deep dives. When it came to Ditto, he says, he wanted the space to convey his personal style. It helped that he and Silber were on the same page. “Andy and I talked about styles ranging from Bauhaus, de Stijl, and classic modernism to 1970s and ’80s aesthetics from places like Italy and France,” Silber says.

They brought in like-minded collaborators: interior designer Eric Wink and architect Alta Indelman. Wink, a close friend of Baraghani who helped with the design of his and Pollock’s home, says the partners told him that New York’s much-missed City Bakery, which was two blocks uptown from Ditto’s 16th Street location, was an inspiration. “They wanted it to feel casual and approachable, with a bit of a New York diner quality,” Wink says.

Holophane pendant lamps hang in the restaurant’s front window.

The space, in a 1905 former factory (its last incarnation was an agnès b. store), is divided into three zones. In the front section, visitors can shop at a market area with hand-picked groceries. A glossy white counter with Bauhaus curves anchors the middle section; this is where customers line up, take a number, and either carry the food out or dine at laminate-topped tables under a trio of oversized custom fabric pendants. The menu, handwritten on a roll of butcher’s paper and updated daily, features dishes like pomegranate spoon salad and what Baraghani calls “the Platonic ideal of granola,” with tahini, maple syrup, and yogurt drizzled with fig-leaf oil.

The third zone, the one painted blue, features an open kitchen and café tables pulled up to a banquette that runs the length of the room. “There’s that good chaos in the middle and the back is a calmer moment,” says Baraghani.

Indelman, a veteran collaborator with New York restaurateurs like Andrew Tarlow and Gabe Stulman, was tasked with giving the space a utilitarian, high-tech ambience. She clad the walls and the open kitchen in patinated stainless steel and preserved industrial elements like structural arches and polished concrete floors.

But it was Baraghani and Silber who took the lead on honing the visual details of the interiors. “We went through 50 possibilities to find the right chair,” Silber says. The one they selected—a 1960s Dutch industrial chair, the Model S22 from Galvanitas—was sourced from Amsterdam Modern in Los Angeles.

Silber also spent hours on eBay bidding on midcentury ceramic bud vases. “They were each under $20 but put them together on a shelf and you have your own little Giorgio Morandi tableau,” he says.

White wall speakers and a French ceramic mask hang above the banquette, tables, and stools upholstered in Maharam’s Stripe It fabric.

The no-nonsense decor also features white wall speakers, created for the space by sound artist Tom Bloxam, and programmed with playlists created by Michael Scanlon of Chandelier Creative, which oversaw Ditto’s branding. But there are quirkier moments too. When it came to choosing fabric for the custom stools, Baraghani insisted on Maharam’s Stripe It, which he felt had a “Baja California” vibe. “We needed that twist,” he says. For his part, Wink specified his favorite stainless-steel toilet for the restrooms—Neo-Metro’s wall-mounted MiniLoo. “I added a custom wood top to soften it a bit,” Wink says.

Meanwhile, a pair of massive midcentury French ceramic faces that hang on a wall opposite the menu of the day are on loan from Baraghani’s own home. “They anchor the space and add a touch of craft,” he says. “I just knew they needed to be here.”

#4889. To Grow Up, First Grow Down

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 14, 2026 | Reviewed by Margaret Foley

Our culture likes easy measurements of maturity, such as chronological age, number of achievements, financial stability, and the ability to adapt to conventional norms for having a job and a place to live. However, a person can meet these criteria for growing up and still have little or no psychological maturity. The word maturity comes from the Latin word matures, meaning “ripen.” We can understand ripen as meaning “moving beyond early stages of development.” But before we can ripen, we may need to grow down.

When we grow down, we confront early attitudes and beliefs that strengthen neither our relationship with ourselves nor our relationship with life. Here are a number of these attitudes and beliefs.

Growing down is a process of letting go or dying, where denial and certain attachments are challenged and gradually let go. It often takes multiple acts of letting go before an attachment is released. Let’s look at how the growing down process can translate into ripening or growing up.

Maturity can so often be such a vague concept that we don’t know what it is or whether we can actually influence it. Everyone seems to want to “grow up” into a flourishing adulthood, yet it can help to take “growing down” seriously because it shows us what we need to let go of and get ready for. It's not much different from clearing land of rocks and debris in order to prepare for planting and growing. The goal is to take some of the mystique out of becoming an adult. We owe it to ourselves and to others to attend first to some of our more primitive attitudes before we find ourselves merely pretending we are adults.

#4888. Here’s what happened in crypto today

Need to know what happened in crypto today? Here is the latest news on daily trends and events impacting Bitcoin price, blockchain, DeFi, Web3 and crypto regulation.

Today in crypto, Harvard and Dartmouth reported unchanged share counts in BlackRock’s Bitcoin ETF, mining services provider Luxor warned of more frequent AI-enabled cyberattacks and real-world asset protocol stUSDT began winding down operations nearly three years after reaching $2.3 billion in total value locked.

Harvard University and Dartmouth College reported unchanged share counts in BlackRock’s spot Bitcoin exchange-traded fund (ETF) at the end of the third quarter as a Bitcoin rally lifted the value of both stakes.

Harvard held 3.04 million shares of the iShares Bitcoin Trust ETF (IBIT), worth about $144.1 million at the end of September, while Dartmouth reported 201,531 shares valued at $9.5 million, according to Form 13F filings with the US Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC).

IBIT gained about 42% in Q3 as Bitcoin rallied nearly 43%. Both posted their strongest quarterly gains of 2026 through September.

Harvard has reported the same IBIT share count since the end of March, after cutting its holdings by 43% in Q1 and 21% in Q4 2025. Dartmouth has reported the same 201,531 shares since first disclosing its Bitcoin ETF position in Q4 2025.

Bitcoin mining services provider Luxor has warned that AI-enabled cyberattacks are becoming increasingly difficult to defend against after attackers compromised its mining-management platform and redirected customers’ computing power to another mining pool over the weekend.

In an X post on Sunday, Luxor CEO Nick Hansen said the attack came through a “sophisticated AI-enabled vector,” without specifying how AI was used in the attack, warning that these types of attacks will only get more frequent over time.

“At this moment, anyone running any sort of substantial business, especially in crypto, is inundated with AI-enabled attacks,” Hansen said in an X post. “This does not seem to be slowing down; rather, it’s speeding up as models get better and more attackers enter the arena.”

The warning comes as security researchers have documented the spread of AI-assisted hacking tools, raising the prospect of faster attacks against businesses holding money or controlling valuable infrastructure.

Real-world asset (RWA) protocol stUSDT has begun winding down operations, nearly three years after reaching a $2.3 billion total value locked (TVL), ending rewards and opening a six-month redemption window for tokenholders.

The team said that interest accrual on stUSDT ended on Saturday, with holders of stUSDT and its wrapped version, wstUSDT, able to redeem through its official portal until May 14, 2027. The team said requests submitted after the deadline will be handled through customer support in monthly batches through August 2027.

The team did not provide a reason for the shutdown but said all underlying assets remain fully backed. Holders with tokens on exchanges or DeFi platforms must transfer them to personal wallets before redeeming.

Cointelegraph reached out to stUSDT for more information but did not receive a response before publication.

Meanwhile, JustLend DAO said it would phase out supply and borrowing functions for its wstUSDT market as part of the transition.

Launched in July 2023 on Tron, stUSDT allowed users to deposit USDT and earn yields from investments in real-world assets, including short-term government bonds. The protocol later expanded to Ethereum.

According to DefiLlama data, stUSDT’s TVL peaked at about $2.3 billion in November 2023 before declining substantially in subsequent years. It stood at about $8.6 million on Sunday, down from roughly $60 million a year earlier.

Despite its name, stUSDT is not affiliated with stablecoin issuer Tether. In September 2023, Tether denied any affiliation with stUSDT, saying the protocol was an independent project.

Cointelegraph is committed to providing independent, high-quality journalism across the crypto, blockchain, AI, and fintech industries.

All news, reviews, and analyses are produced with full journalistic independence and integrity. For more details on our standards and processes, please read our Editorial Policy.

#4887. Gwyneth Paltrow Is Launching a New Podcast—Take the First Look Inside the “Goop Guide” Studio

Viewers tuning in to Gwyneth Paltrow’s new podcast, The Goop Guide, will be forgiven for assuming the show was filmed in a room inside her serene and stylish Montecito home. The setting for her new venture (launching on Netflix and Spotify November 11) upends the paradigm of a podcaster on a sofa next to a potted fern.

The Goop Guide is indeed recorded inside a studio, but Paltrow approached the space with just as much intention as her own home, enlisting interior designer Marie Carson to bring it to life. “I wanted it to feel like a beautiful, elevated nook of somebody’s old stone house somewhere,” Paltrow tells AD of the space.

“If we can’t be recording in my home, I want to give guests the feeling of comfort and coziness,” Paltrow says of her goal for the studio’s design.

The catch? Carson only had mere days to turn the sterile studio into something far cozier and conducive to the wide-ranging conversations Paltrow hoped to conduct during the show, which launches with Zoe Saldana as the first guest. “We started to decorate remotely and I looked at it and thought, This is not the direction,” Paltrow says of the space’s first iteration. “And so I called Marie and said, ‘We’re filming in, like, a week and I need you to come and fix this.’”

It wasn’t the tight timeline that initially gave Carson pause. “At first I said, ‘Oh my gosh, I’ve never done a set! What does that mean?’” recalls the designer. “But when Gwyneth said she wanted it to feel like a beautiful room in her home, I thought, Well, I can do that.” Previously, the two had collaborated on a space at Paltrow’s Brentwood property, which meant there was already a creative shorthand between them—incredibly helpful when they had only days to outfit the space before shooting commenced. “Gwyneth has such a strong direction of what she wants and such excellent taste,” Carson says. “So I was able to take her notes and run with it.”

The focal point for the studio’s design is a late-18th-century Flemish tapestry. “It added life to the space,” says Carson.

The Goop Kitchen founder wanted the focal point of the design to be a late-18th-century Flemish tapestry, whose landscape imagery features a palette of muted greens and taupes. “I thought it would be beautiful because of what they’ve lived through and what they’ve seen,” Paltrow says of tapestries. “I love them because you think about all the life going on in front of these things for hundreds and hundreds of years.” To complement the hanging, Carson added a 19th-century Belgian trestle table from Meg Turner Gallery, antique chairs reupholstered in Loro Piana brushed cashmere, and a handwoven hemp and silk rug sourced from Mansour. “Marie’s execution is flawless,” Paltrow says. “She has a real knack for finding the perfect thing that has the right dimensions and warmth.”

Paltrow commissioned a painting of magnolia from artist Rob Reynolds. The sconces are from Goop’s collection with CB2 and the wicker stools are from Galerie Provenence.

A close-up of one of the podcast chairs: an antique sourced from a shop in Pasadena and reupholstered with Loro Piana cashmere.

In the handful of shows Paltrow’s filmed so far, it appears her guests (which include Rick Rubin, Ke Huy Quan, and more) feel similarly at home. “Everyone who walks in is like, ‘I’ve done so many podcasts and I’ve never seen one like this! It feels like a real room and I feel so at ease.’’’ she says. Considering Paltrow will be filming dozens of episodes inside the space, she’s happy it’s comfortable for her as well. “I would definitely live in this room,” she says. “I think about this room all the time now when I’m not in it because I’m so obsessed with it.”

The sizing kit cost applies to the ring at checkout, so it’s technically free.

Also, heads up: if you have an FSA or HSA plan, Oura Ring 5 is covered.

The Oura Ring 5 is Oura's latest offering, and a few upgrades earned it a spot on our Best Smart Rings list. This version has no noticeable sensors, while previous models had three interior nodes that could be a bit uncomfortable. It still collects health data seamlessly.

Oura recommends wearing the ring on your forefinger, since that's where your pulse is strongest, which can mean slightly better data, but any finger works. I know a few people who use their Oura Ring as their wedding band.

If you're unsure about your size, it could be a good idea to order one of Oura's sizing kits. It’s now $10 on Amazon, and that gets applied to your future ring purchase, so technically it's "free." And it allows you to get an early jump on the sale.

When you purchase on ouraring.com the process of getting a sizing kit is a little more involved. You order the ring first, and then they send you a sizing kit to use to get your correct size in the system. Depending on which route you choose for the sizing kit, you'll use that same route to purchase your ring when you decide to buy.

You might not be looking for a super-slim smart ring. If you’re used to wearing rings with some weight, or you just like something with a little more heft, the Oura Ring 4 is the first version I think is worth keeping, even if you’re buying a new Oura Ring 5.

#4885. 15 Best Doormats That Are Durable and Design-Forward (2026)

Displaying a welcoming doormats at your home’s threshold is a crucial opportunity to make a strong first impression on visitors. But it’s not just about wooing your guests upon arrival—they should also have some staying power against the elements and an onslaught of muddy boots this winter.

Thankfully, we found plenty of heavy-duty, high-quality doormats that are both handsome and durable. Many of these options feature natural fibers made from coir, which is mildew-resistant, absorbent, and can survive any kind of weather conditions and foot traffic it endures. Eco-friendly jute is another natural material common among doormat designs—favored for its organic look and its ability to trap dirt while still looking clean. Our top picks include doormats we’ve tested in our own homes as well as a few exciting options from brands we trust, so we don’t suggest (figuratively) dragging your feet on these great designs.

Ruggable’s entire door mat catalog is machine washable, which feels like a non-negotiable for an item literally made to trap dirt. Senior commerce editor Nashia Baker found a kindred match in this pick, telling us that “the balance of whimsical lines and shapes makes walking through the door a harmonious experience.” She also noted that it’s “incredibly durable,” and after months of high-traffic it “can handle any wet or muddy shoes that land on it without getting waterlogged.” Non-slip backing keeps it (and you) from sliding around.

Lean into a modern nautical vibe (think: this contemporary Long Island escape) with Serena & Lily’s chunky mat that looks like it was woven from stolen sailboat rope. The natural/coastal blue colorway offers just a hint of color, while monochromatic options that weave in multiple shades of blue and white make a more vibrant statement.

Sometimes all you need to make a statement is a simple pattern. This honeycomb design is subtle, mildew-resistant, shed-free, and fade-safe. A rubber backing means there won’t be any slipping and it’s designed to hold water—the raised border design helps contain up to 1.5 gallons of water per square yard.

Quilt-like lines feed into a larger geometric pattern in this doormat design that still feels simple but very modern. The grid of black rectangular lines reads as a neutral, and the elongated shape covers more area than some of its peers on this list, so we’d recommend it for an entry hallway or wider doorsteps.

This Heymat doormat is a durable option for covered porches and entryways. Not suitable for true outdoor use (on an uncovered patio or anywhere susceptible to the elements), this Heymat doormat is highly absorbent and soft underfoot. Shopping market editor Kate McGregor’s family owns a few mats from this brand, which they use as an oversized entryway mat in the mudroom for muddy boots and dog paws, as well as a bathmat in the guest bathroom. “This multi-colored mat has held up so well against summer mud and dropped birdseed, despite its pastel grid design,” she says. “Even after five years, the colors have stayed vibrant, and the mat never gets soggy.” Plus, whether sprayed down or shaken, she notes that the cleanup is very easy. “I might protest if my mom ever decides to replace it.”

A departure from the traditional rectangular doormat shape, this Toast alternative offers colorful texture in one compact oval shape that’s fully reversible. This one is crafted from woven and braided jute strands that are ideal for trapping loose dirt and debris. For those looking for an alternative to standard brown jute styles, these alternating patterns are pretty mesmerizing.

If you’ve got more floor space to fill in your greeting room, smaller sizes of The Citizenry’s jute rugs could be exactly what you’ve been looking for. At 24” x 36,” this hand braided mat has a refined look thanks to the contrasting border design, and it’ll be a nice surface to station wet or damp shoes so you’re not damaging the floor.

In our doormat quest, we also found this woven gem from Matilda Goad’s MG&Co. with looped fringing that looks like scalloped edges. The deep burgundy tone will help hide visible mud stains (though the jute weave can also be easily hosed down when it’s time for a clean). It’s also available in a longer, thinner silhouette to help cover sprawling patios.

Floral without feeling overly whimsical (no shame if that’s your vibe, though), Anthropologie’s sweet, embossed doormat is a simple option for anyone with an already-decorated entryway. With an abundance of five-star ratings, lots of reviews praise the design's versatile look and durable material makeup.

The raised, slatted design of Crate & Barrel’s unfinished teakwood doormat keeps moisture, grime, and dirt away sans-bristles. It’s also great for indoor use, perhaps as a minimalist bath mat. Be mindful that teak patinas over time due to weather and regular wear and tear, shifting from a golden wood to a grayish color.

The bold tulip motif on this doormat has a cheerful Scandi-folk style that can brighten your front entry even in the dead of winter. When it starts to look a little worse for wear, the mat easily lifts off its non-slip base for machine washing. In a spot that receives near-constant foot traffic, the ability to refresh it from season to season will be a godsend.

Danish brand HAY is known for their lively color combinations, and their doormats are no exception. With interlocking strands of navy and pink rope, bookended by charming red trim, this pick welcomes guests in with a jolt of maximalist energy. The vegetable fiber strands offer a strong and durable natural alternative to plastic: Shake it out and spray it down with water to clean it and keep the colors fresh.

A stylish coir braid gives this under-$30 doormat a higher-end look. The thick weave creates an almost padded texture, designed to expertly track dirt as your shoe treads catch on the material. With its lattice-like appearance, we envision this doormat at the front door of your Cotswolds-inspired farmhouse, or perhaps a coastal home that could use another natural texture to contrast the white and blue color palette.

Leave it to Sarah Sherman Samuel to create a great doormat. The AD100 designer, arguably most known for Mandy Moore’s Altadena home, offers a twist on the traditional rectangle. Uneven stripes with cutouts give your entryway a modern edge. The coir is dependable, and the black accents help to disguise the inevitable mess.

CB2’s doormat selection is predominantly composed of versatile Chilewich designs—like this monochromatic pattern that feels like a fashionable take on television static. Designed with functionality first, the shag mat uses antimicrobial yarn to keep itself tidy. It also has a no-slip backing to keep it fixed near your doorstep, even when your shoes need a serious wipe down.

What should you look for in the best outdoor doormats?

Although design is important, you also want to make sure that your doormat is low profile enough to fit under your door and also has a non-slip backing to keep your guests from busting it before they make it into your home. (Talk about a first impression!) Materials like PVC with rubber backing are key for the latter, while 100% coir doormats give your entryway a more natural look and are more ideal for trapping dirt and debris.

The answer is a tentative yes. You’re always going to want to see what the manufacturer recommends and it, of course, depends on where you live. Doormats are always made for outdoor use, but various climates will wear down the materials differently. Frequent rain and snow might soften jute while constant sunshine will fade most textiles. An easy solution, if you aren’t sure what your mat can withstand, is to drag your doormat into the mudroom or somewhere with a bit of shelter whenever there’s big weather on its way.

#4884. I Was Making an Important Point, but Now It’s Gone

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 14, 2026 | Reviewed by Margaret Foley

Have you ever been in a situation where you knew exactly what you wanted to talk about, but when the moment arrived, you couldn’t think of what you had planned to say?

That’s what happened to Jen* when she made her first big presentation at a new job. She practiced the talk repeatedly, to the point at which she felt confident that she could make it without a mistake. Yet when a senior colleague interrupted her to ask her to clarify what she had just said, her brain went blank.

“I felt so stupid,” she said. “It was a simple question. I knew the answer. But my mind left the room. My brain froze completely.”

Many factors can contribute to this kind of “brain freeze,” which fits into a category of cognitive problems called “word retrieval difficulties.” There are a number of possible causes, both physiological and psychological, but there is also the odd and amazing role of unconscious dynamics that can make our brains stop working just when we need them to work at top capacity.

The brain is a complex organ that controls thought, memory, emotion, touch, motor skills, vision, breathing, temperature, hunger, and every process that regulates our body. When any of these factors is in play, the complex system of neurons through which the brain stores and recovers words may not be firing as quickly as you would like.

Anxiety—already a complex emotion—can be a lot more complicated when unconscious thoughts and feelings play a role. These are thoughts and feelings that go on outside of our awareness. They are often behind behaviors that we can’t explain to ourselves.

For instance, Jen’s brain freeze during her presentation could certainly have been caused by her anxiety about proving herself to her new colleagues and bosses. But contributing to her anxiety may have been some thoughts and feelings that she kept hidden away, even from herself. It’s very hard for us to uncover these hidden emotions on our own, because we push them out of our minds and into what Freud called the “unconscious” mind.

Today we think of these ideas as “split off” or “dissociated” from awareness.

The psychoanalyst Christopher Bollas gave the name “unthought known” to the thoughts and feelings, memories, and ideas about ourselves that we don’t let ourselves know about.

Sometimes we relegate information, without realizing it, to the unknown part of ourselves, to protect ourselves from old, painful memories. But we can also use this powerful tool to step away from a side of ourselves that we don’t like. This emotional process almost always happens outside of our awareness.

The presence of unconscious thoughts and feelings does not necessarily rule out physiological causes for memory loss. If it happens to you on a regular basis, it may still not be a cause for concern, but it’s important to have a medical professional check to make sure it’s not a symptom of a medical problem.

If this type of memory loss happens only on occasion, even if you aren’t in psychotherapy, you can start to look for some patterns that reveal unconscious reasons for behaviors and feelings that you can’t explain. And once you understand what’s going on behind the scenes in your psyche, it’s much easier to do something about it.

If you consistently go blank at work, for example, you can start to track the specific moments when it happens. When do you forget? Is it with a particular person or group of people? Does it happen in certain situations?

As Jen began to sort through some of her unacknowledged thoughts and feelings about the presentation, she realized that she was not only trying to prove herself to her new colleagues, but also secretly trying to prove to her family how well she was doing.

She wanted to impress them, particularly her father and her older sister. “I imagined telling them how well I had done,” she said. “I love the expression on my dad’s face when he tells me he’s proud of me. But that’s something I know and think about all the time. Maybe I’m too much of a daddy’s girl.”

After a few minutes of talking and thinking, Jen said, “I think maybe part of the problem is that a secret part of me also wanted to make my sister feel bad. She always does well at everything, and I’m always feeling like I can’t quite match up to her. I wanted her to feel the same way for once.”

As Jen talked about this dynamic with her sister, something she knew but did not like to think about, she had to face her competitiveness head-on. “It’s something I don’t like about myself,” she said. “I mean, it’s OK to be competitive. But it’s not OK to want to hurt the person you’re competing with. It makes me feel so bad about myself.”

We suspected that Jen had shut down just as she was beginning to show off. Of course, we can’t know, but it seemed likely that her internal dialogue connected showing off at work with a wish to hurt her sister by rubbing her success in. Because Jen was so critical of this part of herself, she may have shut it down, stopping her brain in the process.

Once we had talked out the meaning of Jen’s brain fog, we began to explore Jen’s competition with her sister. It had many different components, and it took a while, but eventually Jen realized that she could succeed without hurting her sister. The next time she made a presentation, she did not fog up.

It’s not always easy to know what your unconscious mind is thinking. But if you decide to do some internal exploration, start with the idea that the "unthought known" is something you don’t like about yourself. Once you’ve located the difficult thoughts, it’s easier to change your behavior. And the “unthought known” is much less likely to sabotage you.
