#4882. 8 Best Hotel Mattress Picks for a Five-Star Staycation (2026)

The hotel mattress probably isn't on your vacation itinerary, but it can make or break the trip. A lumpy, unsupportive bed can derail even the most carefully planned getaway. A really good one, though, might become an unexpected highlight—the kind that has you looking forward to bedtime and quietly checking the mattress label before checkout.

Recreating that experience at home doesn't require another plane ticket. Some of our favorite properties, including design-forward waterfront retreats and charming bed-and-breakfasts, put just as much thought into their mattresses as they do their interiors. Many turn to brands like Saatva, Ostermoor, and Casper to outfit their guest rooms; others, like the W Hotels, have signature mattresses you can purchase directly. We've rounded up our favorites, such as options with plush pillow tops, supportive coils, and pressure-relieving foam, to help you bring home the best part of your stay. The room service, unfortunately, is still up to you.

For consistency, all the prices reflect queen-size mattresses.

A stay at the Ritz-Carlton is typically reflected by its refined, elegant bedrooms. The property’s eponymous bed invites the same feeling. At 14 inches, it’s a higher-profile mattress that can help make the bed the centerpiece of the room. One of the standout qualities is its coil system, as it includes 900-plus individual springs for great edge support to help keep the sides from sagging as well as promote motion isolation—a necessary factor for couples who don’t want to sense each other’s every move.

Serta is one of the most well-known mattress manufacturers for high-end hotels, such as the Waldorf-Astoria. While many luxury properties feature custom beds from the namesake brand, it doesn’t mean you can’t shop at popular retailers such as Amazon. You’d never guess it, but this premium bed is priced under $500 (making it the most affordable on our list). While Serta offers memory foam and hybrid versions of its beds, this pillow-top innerspring mattress has been a favorite in our shopping writer Julia Harrison’s space, and she says it lives up to its “Five-Star” name.

It’s filled with three zones of coil support that keep her free of back and neck problems, and the quilted cover gives her something soft to dream about each night. “I’m really not one for pomp and frills in my mattress—I’m a firm believer that a good mattress has very little going on besides a somewhat plush top to keep you from feeling any sort of spring mechanism going on inside,” she says in her mattress review. “I instantly slept well on this mattress I think because it’s quite simply: a great mattress.” Bonus: It’s a mattress-in-a-box, so it can fit easily through narrow doors and hallways once it’s delivered, and inflate to its full size in less than a day’s time after unboxing, according to Harrison. Just in case you’re on the fence, it comes with a 100-day sleep trial so you can really see if it’s right for you before committing.

The Six Bells Countryside Inn opened its doors in the bucolic Hudson Valley in 2025, and it’s already earned two Michelin Keys. In each of the 11 carefully and whimsically designed bedrooms at this retreat, you’ll find handcrafted mattresses and bedding from New York-born brand Ostermoor. Dating back to 1853, Ostermoor’s mattresses have a storied history. The beds have innovated to foam and pocketed coil inner layers and designer covers in recent years, and we can prove they’re worth the splurge.

“From the moment I plopped down on the mattress, I was amazed by how comfortable and supportive it was,” shares contributor Zoë Sessums. “It strikes a perfect balance between spring and cushion—just the right amount of give without ever feeling too soft. I consistently wake up feeling rested and supported.” Firm without feeling rigid, this mattress looks as good as it feels, too. “The tufted top, the blue and white ticking pattern, the hand-stitched details—it feels like a piece of luxury furniture, not just a place to sleep,” Sessums adds, later writing, “I would recommend this to someone who values design and craftsmanship as much as comfort.”

Saatva has found its way into charming bed-and-breakfasts and boutique retreats across the country, including Pasadena's historic Bissell House (an 1887 Victorian filled with antique furnishings) and the Cornell Inn in the Berkshires (known for its colorful, individually designed guest rooms). You can bring that same inviting comfort home with the Saatva Classic, which comes with a plush three-inch Euro pillow top, a responsive dual-coil system, and targeted lumbar support. “It’s firm (which I prefer), regulates temperature well, and is extremely durable,” our tester Erin Kaplan, vice president of communications at Conde Nast, says. “I alternate between sleeping on my side and sleeping on my stomach, and this mattress is great for both positions.”

Choose from three firmness levels—plush soft, luxury firm, or firm—, eight different sizes, and two mattress heights to find your ideal fit. Feeling extra? Saatva also offers two adjustable bases that elevate your head and feet at the touch of a button, including a premium Plus model for an even more indulgent breakfast in bed. A 365-night trial gives you plenty of time to sleep on your decision before committing.

At 1 Hotel Brooklyn Bridge, even the beds have a view. Sweeping views of the Manhattan skyline set the scene, with reclaimed wood floors, rich leather headboards, and locally crafted details like crate-inspired closets and Fresnel glass light fixtures bringing warmth and character to the rooms. With custom Keetsa mattresses in every room, you might find yourself hitting snooze despite the spectacular view.

Back at home, the Keetsa Pillow Plus offers its own take on hotel-worthy comfort. It's something of a Goldilocks mattress: not too soft, not too firm, but just right. A three-inch pillow top combines body-contouring memory foam with responsive comfort foam to help cushion pressure points, and a soft, moisture-wicking cover designed to keep overheating at bay. Individually wrapped coils provide cushioning and support while minimizing motion transfer, so even if your bedmate tosses and turns, you can stay blissfully unaware.

When husband-and-wife duo Ely and Danielle Franko transformed a dated Catskills ski hostel into The Henson, a 16-room boutique hotel, they had a pretty good idea of what makes a great night's sleep. The couple painstakingly restored the property themselves, filling its rooms with vintage furniture, locally sourced finds, and hand-applied wallpaper. But when it came to mattresses, they didn't have to look far for inspiration: They chose the same Casper Dream Hybrid they sleep on at home. In fact, they loved it so much that they insisted on king-size mattresses in every room, even the smallest ones. The hybrid design combines responsive springs with breathable foam and three zones of support that help keep your spine aligned. Its medium feel is meant to offer a little give without leaving you sinking into the mattress, making it a crowd-pleasing choice for your own guest room. After all, the best hosts know a good night's sleep is part of the hospitality.

Hot sleepers, here’s a mattress for you. The Four Season’s signature mattress features a couple of core cooling bed qualities: proprietary cooling gel foam layers with channeled designs that promote airflow throughout the mattress, as well as a patented DualCool top layer that draws heat and moisture away from the body and keeps the bed fresh with its antimicrobial properties. The cooling mattress is designed for sleepers who need extra structure on their bed. This posh number has targeted support for the lower back area and zeroes in on alignment regardless of your sleep style.

The W Hotels are widely popular because of the bold design across the properties worldwide. To mirror this aesthetic, you can cover the brand’s mattress with colorful bedding to let your personal style shine. When it comes to the bed itself, it is defined by a plush top quilted damask design to ease side sleepers into a peaceful slumber. It’s made for people who need extra support too, though, including an individually-wrapped coil system, five-zone support system, and comfort layers for added cushioning and pressure relief.

Which Kind of Mattress Do Five-Star Hotels Use?

Most luxury hotels use hybrid mattresses in their suites to strike the right balance of softness and support, thanks to pocketed coils and layers of foam. These are also typically medium-firm mattresses since a plush mattress can be uncomfortable for some sleep positions like stomach sleepers. Ultra-firm beds are often too stiff for side sleeping and may not align with guests' expectations who are looking for soft mattresses to sink into on their vacations.

If you want to refresh your mattress without the hefty price tag of buying a new one, a mattress topper or featherbed could be a solid transitional pick. The Ritz-Carlton and W Hotels promote one made with white duck feathers, Mandarin Oriental’s exclusive topper comprises white goose feathers, Waldorf Astoria beds feature a down-alternative fill, and Marriott’s selection is polyester.

There’s no one-size-fits-all here, so it really comes down to your preferred loftiness and weight. Duck and goose feathers are heavier than others fills, though duck is slightly less fluffy and won’t trap heat as well. Down-alternatives, like polyester, are more allergen-friendly and “a good option for those who prefer to be vegan in their home,” Danielle Trotter, the former textiles technical designer at West Elm, previously shared.

What Is the Thickness of a Hotel Mattress?

Hotel mattresses usually average 12 inches in thickness. This makes it easier to hop in and out of bed, especially when using a box spring that can add extra height. Plus, it prevents the need for deep pocket sheets.

How Can I Recreate the Hotel Bed Experience at Home?

We’ve explored the entire topic on how to make your bedroom feel like a hotel, and aside from finding a new bed with a medium-firm feel, we’ve learned that percale sheets and down pillows are pretty much a must to complete the setup. When it comes to the former, hotel sheets are usually made with organic cotton percale because the fabric looks and feels crisp, giving that refined aesthetic a boutique stay is known for. Usually a 200 thread count is a good rule of thumb to look out for since this means there’s plenty of airflow between the threads for a breathable feel. Hotel pillows are usually filled with down feathers since they’re so lofty and soft, but a down alternative is just as good in case you’re an allergy-prone sleeper (just look for materials like hypoallergenic gel fibers since they’re known for being squishy without falling flat, just like a feather-filled pillow).

What Is the Difference Between Memory Foam and Latex?

Traditional memory foam has a body-contouring effect that allows your body to sink into your mattress, reducing motion transfer (a must for bedmates!). Meanwhile, latex foam offers more responsiveness (or bounce) and overall support. As Neslihan Ozdemir, founder of Girl on the Mattress, previously told AD, latex also holds up well over time and is temperature-regulating. Latex foam mattresses (and some memory foam options) are certified organic, making them eco-friendly foam picks, too.

Known as the most common mattress type, innerspring mattresses feature a bed of coils. There’s quite a range to these mattresses: Basic versions have a grid of steel coils, while premier options have a pillow top and pocketed coil technology, meaning each coil is individually wrapped to help with motion isolation. Ozdemir added that innerspring is best for people who sleep on the edge of the bed or are heavier in weight. If you prefer a more plush mattress, but still want the support of those coils, consider a hybrid mattress, which is layered with soft foam.

#4881. Lighter pauses withdrawals after reported $10M account theft

Lighter has paused withdrawals after reporting a leaked trader API key, with Bubblemaps tracking $10.4 million withdrawn.

Decentralized derivatives exchange Lighter paused withdrawals and new pool creation Monday after a security incident it linked to a leaked API key for a trader’s account.

Blockchain analytics firm Bubblemaps said about $10.4 million in USDC had been withdrawn from the platform. It tracked funds being swapped into Ether (ETH) and Wrapped Monero XMR1, a token traded on Hyperliquid.

Lighter said its protocol and smart contracts were unaffected. Lighter’s documentation says API keys can place trades, manage positions and transfer funds between accounts linked to the same blockchain wallet. Transfers to another wallet owner require an additional signature from the account owner’s wallet.

The exchange said it activated Guardian mode as a precaution and that its security council would upgrade the contracts. It aimed to complete the work and restore withdrawals by the end of the day.

The security incident follows a string of high-profile cryptocurrency exploits this year, including the $6.3 billion Binance hack on Oct. 29, the industry’s largest to date.

Lighter and Bubblemaps identified wallet ‘0xb3f’ as the attacker’s address. Blockchain data platform Bitquery separately traced 10,393,057 USDC leaving Lighter’s Ethereum contract at 10:11 am UTC. It said most of those funds were converted into Ether through CoW Swap within minutes, followed by a series of 160 ETH transfers to wallets linked to XMR1 purchases on Hyperliquid.

Lighter’s update did not explain how the API key was exposed, what the contract upgrades would change or whether the affected trader would be reimbursed.

Cointelegraph contacted Lighter for comment but had not received a response by publication.

#4880. 7 Best Writing Desks That Are Furniture Chameleons (2026)

Regardless of whether you have a home office or not, a good writing desk is a versatile piece of furniture to have on hand. Unlike a secretary desk, which is usually equipped with a drop-front panel or concealed compartments, this type of desk has a streamlined surface and open-leg construction, making it best suited for pen-and-paper tasks. In other words, it’s less intrusive style-wise since it’s not designed to have a million compartments for cable management or bulky drawers to stash old bills.

The design options feel endless in this furniture category. You can try a larger, minimal silhouette with a slender drawer or two if you’re a creative like TV writer Mike Gauyo, who spends hours behind a writing desk in a Los Angeles studio. Or you can opt for a vintage desk to bring lived-in character to your interiors, like the styles in this A-frame Catskills cabin and Southampton, New York, enclave. Because the silhouettes are so adaptable, many can easily work as entryway console tables, dressing tables, and even sideboards to hold extra plates and silverware after all your dinner parties.

All in all, this desk setup encourages peaceful moments rooted in intention, and it might speed up your to-do list. Now is the time to knock out those holiday cards or try your hand at writing a novel.

The clean lines and neutral tones make this less-is-more desk our favorite for minimalists. Even though it has a simple look, it’s customizable—with three top colors and two wire base layers available—to help you style it perfectly for your space. Once you position it in the right spot, spread out your paperwork across the roomy, scratch-and-stain-resistant surface. (No more coffee mug rings!)

Add a bit of Wizard of Oz magic to your home office with this green, spacious desk. The emerald hue has been trending over the last few years, and designers often use it throughout interiors since it represents serenity—not a bad option for an office space. The vibrant color and petite size can also work as an accent piece, should you need something more than a console near the stairs in a foyer.

See how the wood grain detail comes through the honey oak finish. This and the notched leg detail give this McGee & Co. desk a heritage quality. We can picture this beauty tucked in on a landing nook in a cabin. Add a table lamp with bespoke detail, like a linen pleated shade, and you’ve got storybook design.

Leave it to social media star Emma Chamberlain to invite her cool-girl vibes into your home. At just under 40 pounds, this gem is ideal for a studio space or a teeny spare bedroom. Picture it in front of a little window with a cute vase of posies. Stash a journal in the petite drawer to help you carve out a little spot to daydream.

When you want your writing desk to double as a showpiece, look no further than Lulu and Georgia’s Maksim design. Stacked curves flank each side, as if the desk were a functional sculpture. The acacia wood is stained black, giving the piece more of a moody, contemporary look. Its larger footprint, a 52-inch-wide surface, provides plenty of space for desk accessories—like a black leather and wood mousepad to match the modern style.

A work surface or a mirage? The all-glass Ebern Designs writing table speaks to idiosyncratic design thanks to its minimalist, waterfall look. Should you feel like the style needs a bit more visual interest, consider pairing it with a desk chair outfitted in mohair upholstery or jewel tones.

Twist-in-leg construction is a perk when assembling an office desk that doesn’t come with white-glove delivery. The telltale midcentury-modern silhouette with angled legs and a sleek storage drawer means this style fits in with most contemporary furnishings. If you want to soften up the look, drape a throw blanket over the chair.

#4879. Zelle picks Solana for ZelleUSD stablecoin and international payments

Zelle plans to use Solana for its ZelleUSD stablecoin and international payments, with confidential transfers designed to hide payment amounts.

Zelle, the US peer-to-peer payments network operated by Early Warning Services (EWS), a consortium of banks that includes JPMorgan and Wells Fargo, is partnering with Solana to support its expansion into international payments. The company announced on stage at Breakpoint 2026 that its proprietary dollar-backed stablecoin, ZelleUSD (ZLUSD), will launch on the Solana blockchain to allow for peer-to-peer payments internationally.

The move brings one of the largest domestic payments networks in the US into the blockchain-based payments market. Zelle processed more than $1.2 trillion in US payments in 2025 and has since taken steps to establish an international business. In June, the company named India as its first international remittance corridor, with plans to allow US consumers to send money to friends and family in the country. In the same announcement, the company unveiled ZelleUSD, intended to underpin future international payment capabilities in additional markets. ZelleUSD’s issuer is Anchorage Digital Bank.

“Sending money around the world is a pain; it’s complex, it’s fragmented and it’s often unfamiliar. Over half of the Remittance services that are offered today don’t deliver money as fast as we’d like them to,” said Alexandra Lang, vice president of digital asset partnerships at Zelle. “Solana is a partner we chose because we’re here to deliver payments at scale.”

For Zelle, the decision to select Solana appears to have been driven in large part by the network’s infrastructure. “Zelle has such a massive scale in the United States, we needed a partner that can also deliver at massive scale internationally as well,” said Denise Leonhard, General Manager at Zelle in a video discussing the partnership.

“The number one priority is the responsibility that we have to make sure that payments are safe, secure, and they get to the right place [...] That piece around trust, security, proven infrastructure is the reason why we chose Solana.”

Following several years in which network outages raised questions about Solana’s suitability for financial applications, the chain has maintained 100% uptime since February 2024. This is despite a routing failure at infrastructure provider Teraswitch in August 2026, which took 29% of Solana’s network offline. According to blockchain intelligence platform Metrika, transaction finality slowed, but uptime was sustained.

On stage at Breakpoint 2026, the Solana Foundation announced the migration of Solana from beta into mainnet, bringing with it an improvement in finality speed from 13 seconds to 150 milliseconds.

Related: HKMA, Swift explore tokenized deposits for cross-border bank payments

In the payments wallet, customers will have the option to encrypt their transfer so it is shielded from the public blockchain. Institutions that are part of Zelle’s network will be able to decrypt the transfer to carry out the checks needed to maintain compliance.

“Features like this are paramount to really getting payments to scale,” said Lang.

Related: DBS, JPMorgan’s Kinexys launch 24/7 transfers of tokenized deposits

Although Solana is gaining ground in stablecoin activity, Ethereum and Tron continue to hold substantially larger pools of dollar-pegged assets. According to Token Terminal data pulled on Nov. 14, Ethereum held $150.5 billion in stablecoins while Tron held $93.2 billion, compared with $13.9 billion on Solana.

However, Solana’s stablecoin activity is growing faster in transaction frequency than on either of its larger competitors. The network recorded an 82% increase in monthly stablecoin transfers year-over-year in October, compared with 48% growth on Ethereum, while Tron’s transfer count declined by nearly 8%, according to Token Terminal data.

Zelle’s choice of Solana over both Tron and Ethereum follows other remittance providers that have expanded on-chain. Also at Breakpoint, MoneyGram outlined an integration with Solflare allowing Solflare wallet users to exchange UDSC to cash. The move expands MoneyGram Ramps on Solana, launched by the company in August.

“That’s where MoneyGram brings something different to the ecosystem: the ability to bridge digital dollars and physical cash at scale, making stablecoins more useful in everyday life,” said Luke Tuttle, Chief Product and Technology Officer at MoneyGram in a press release.

While Zelle did not provide a timeline for the launch of international payments on stage, earlier announcements indicated that the India corridor would be live by the end of 2026.

#4878. How Focus on the Mental Game Led to an Undefeated Season

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 14, 2026 | Reviewed by Margaret Foley

31-0: That was the headline-grabbing regular season record of the 2025-26 Miami RedHawks NCAA Division I basketball team. We’re talking about the first Miami University, located in Oxford, Ohio, and founded in 1809. Not Miami of Florida, a college launched in 1925

A large chunk of credit for the RedHawks’ remarkable run belongs to Head Coach Travis Steele and how he optimizes performance with the mental approach he trains. Steele was the much-deserved recipient of the 2026 John McLendon National Coach-of-the-Year Award, honoring the top head coach across all levels of collegiate basketball.

“I find this stuff (the mental game) fascinating," Steele shared in a recent interview conducted for this post. “If you can get guys to play at their peak level, man, you’re going to be really good. I’ve seen talented guys that don’t believe in themselves, and they stink. I’ve seen average players that have a high level of belief in themselves, and they’re really good.”

The difference stems from their mental approach to practice, performance, and competition.

“We [coaches] all talk about the X’s and O’s, and I could talk basketball all day long,” Steele continued. “But then you go back to the ‘why?’ piece. ‘Why can this guy have the belief, and the other guy doesn’t?’ Maybe they don’t embrace the process. Maybe they don’t handle adversity. And then how can we help them in an area, like the mental toughness piece, to overcome adversity?

“That, to me, is coaching,” Steele summarized.

Bo Schembechler, Ara Parseghian, Woody Hayes, Paul Brown, Sean McVay, Weeb Ewbank, Carmen Cozza, John Harbaugh, Walter Alston, John Pont, and Paul Dietzel: Legendary, Hall-of-Fame sports coaches, all with the common denominator of Miami University, regarded in the sports world as the “Cradle of Coaches.”

Deriving from those coaching greats is the Miami Mindset, a set of four core values, fine-tuned by Steele, that defines the RedHawk basketball mental approach. Here's a list of those Miami Mindset core values:

“We try to basically make our core values come to life rather than just being words on a wall,” Steele explained. “If we embody our four core values, that’s what we call the Miami Mindset.”

Effectively reviewing Steele’s version of the Miami Mindset would be too exhaustive for this one post. A follow-up post will delve into a detailed examination of the Miami Mindset's four core values and how Steele trains and instills them in his players.

Stay tuned for a deep dive into the Miami Mindset with Coach Steele.

#4877. Joseph Dirand Joins Forces with Peter Brant and Stephanie Seymour Brant to Reimagine a Historic 1920s Palm Beach Mansion

It began with a chance encounter and a phone call. The French architect Joseph Dirand was walking into a restaurant in New York when he ran into an old friend, the art dealer Tico Mugrabi. Dirand mentioned that he was flying to Palm Beach the next day and his friend lit up. Mugrabi pulled out his phone and made a call. “Peter, my friend Joseph is coming to Palm Beach tomorrow,” Dirand recalls him saying. “You have to meet this guy.” The following day Dirand was sitting across from Peter Brant, the fabled industrialist and publisher, and his wife, Stephanie Seymour Brant, one of the defining supermodels of her generation, in their new home. The Brants had owned the house for only a few days, and it marked the opening of a new chapter for the couple.

The Brants in front of Warhol’s White Disaster (White Car Crash 19 Times), 1963, in the great room. Jean Prouvé coffee table; sofa and chairs by Pierre Jeanneret.

Marc Newson’s aluminum Lockheed Lounge, a Tower Cabinet by Ettore Sottsass, and a Trapeze table by Jean Prouvé all stand in the loggia.

A Végétale mirror by Claude Lalanne reflects Untitled (Richard Prince and Cindy Sherman), 1980, and a 1930s settee by Jean-Michel Frank.

A pair of bespoke Philippe Anthonioz floor lamps Flank Jonathan Horowitz’s Self-portrait in “Mirror #1” (Kevin), 2012.

Brown Jordan chaise longues under umbrellas by Business & Pleasure Co. stand poolside.

There is something quintessentially American in how quickly it all unfolded, and for Dirand, it meant meeting a man he had long admired. As an inquisitive young designer he had pored over photographs of Brant’s earlier homes, and one image had stayed with him: It showed a Jean Prouvé desk beneath a Jean-Michel Basquiat painting, captured at a time when almost no one thought to set French postwar design against contemporary art. “For me, Peter was a kind of collector’s hero,” says Dirand.

Brant’s eye for art and design was formed early. His father loved classical art and considered even Picasso “way too modern,” but on family ski trips to Switzerland the teenage Brant got to know the Zurich dealer Bruno Bischofberger, who subsequently introduced him to Leo Castelli, and the two titans of the art world became the young Brant’s mentors. Brant then bought his first Andy Warhol painting in 1966, and by the time Warhol’s business manager, Fred Hughes, introduced them in November 1968, he already owned about seven of them. Warhol and Brant would go on to become close friends for the remainder of the artist’s life.

In a corner of the Great Room, Roy Lichtenstein’s The Refrigerator, 1962, faces a 1960 copper hanging cabinet by Paul Evans. a 1934 plaster vase by Alberto Giacometti sits on A Joseph Dirand Edition Iseo Gold coffee table. 1950s Hirondelles wall light by Jean Royère; pair of 1958 Diego Giacometti armchairs.

Warhol’s 1975 portrait of Murray Brant (Brant’s father) commands a wall of the paneled study. Direction armchair and Présidence desk by Jean Prouvé; Ettore Sottsass hanging lamp.

Artworks in the entrance hall include, from left, a Massangis stone lion-head fountain feature by Harumi Klossowska de Rola, 2022; Andy Warhol’s Self Portrait (Green), 1964; a Klossowska de Rola wolf sculpture; and Richard Prince’s Nurses’ Dormitory, 2002. The sanded Ceppo di Gré stone modular console is by Dirand.

In the primary bedroom, George Condo’s 2004 portrait of Stephanie Seymour Brant hangs above a 1946 Charlotte Perriand sideboard on which stands an enameled earthenware lamp by Roger Capron. A Jean Prouvé Bridge armchair joins an Ours Polaire chair and sofa by Jean Royère around Prouvé’s Tripod coffee table.

An exterior view of the 1926 Addison Mizner residence

Few people then saw Warhol’s work the way Brant did. When he hung his first Marilyn at home, visitors were shocked: “People would come to my house and think, This is the worst thing I’ve ever seen. In the 1960s, he says, you could count the serious collectors of Pop art “on three hands.”

His rule, he says, is to collect “something that was in style that’s out of style,” though never as speculation. Between their holdings and those of the Brant Foundation, which he established in 1996 to promote the education and appreciation of contemporary art and design, the collection now numbers more than 3,000 works, and he has never bought for a particular wall. What he misses in many newer collections is any sense of the person behind them. “You can see right away that this guy had a great adviser,” he says, “but there’s no madness in the collection. Every collection should have some madness.”

Carlo Mollino’s Tipo B side chair rests beneath Jean-Michel Basquiat’s Per Capita, 1981.

A painting by Glenn Ligon and a stool by Shiro Kuramata in Brant’s bath

Seymour Brant and Brant in the great room

Brant has shared much of his collecting journey with Seymour Brant, whom he married in 1995. She has built a collection of her own: Her passion is clothing rather than paintings, and she has pursued it with the same scholarship and the same sharp eye as her husband. She was a close friend of the legendary couturier Azzedine Alaïa and has one of the most significant holdings of his work, alongside historic couture that Dirand describes as extraordinary. Where Brant is the storyteller, Seymour Brant is the quieter presence, though Dirand is clear that she was integral to every decision. Her collecting has even changed the way Brant sees his own. “It’s not a dress in a closet,” he says. “It’s a piece of art, and it mixes with art.”

Brant has spent some 50 years in the Palm Beach area, first as a polo player. He and Seymour Brant had no need for a new home there—they loved the one they had as their children grew up. But when Collado Hueco, a home by Addison Mizner, the architect who defined 1920s Palm Beach, came on the market in 2020, the collector in him took over. “It was a piece of history,” Dirand says. “For him, there was no choice.” Designed in 1924 for the American businessman Paul Moore, it is one of Mizner’s boldest and most unusual houses.

In a hall, a 1974 Diego Giacometti bench sits in front of Jean-Michel Basquiat’s Untitled (Sugar Ray Robinson), 1982. The curtain rods are by Julian Schnabel.

Warhol’s The Kiss (Bela Lugosi), 1964, and a 1947 Alexandre Noll mahogany armchair in a corner of the primary bedroom. Philippe Anthonioz hanging light; Jean Prouvé tripod coffee table; Jean Royère Ours Polaire sofa.

John Currin’s Constance Towers, 2009, presides over Seymour Brant’s dressing room. Furniture includes an Ambassador chair and Éléphanteau sofa, both by Jean Royère.

In the great room, a plaster bas-relief, Support, and a carved-stone console by Alberto Giacometti are flanked by a pair of Éléphanteau chairs by Jean Royère. In the foreground is a Fondation Maeght chair by Diego Giacometti.

The house presented Dirand with a real puzzle. Asymmetrical, it spreads across multiple wings, varying from one to three levels. The main entertaining rooms had retained their original character, while the entrance and stair hall were dark and enclosed. Dirand set out to let the sunlight that moves across the house reach every part of the interior as much as possible. The collection shaped Dirand’s approach too. With so many treasured works coming into the house, the team wanted to ensure they would be protected against anything the unpredictable Florida weather could bring. What began as a renovation became, in Dirand’s words, “a reconstruction saving the historical.” Rather than demolishing and starting again, the team kept the historic walls standing, reinforced every one of them, and dug beneath to take the foundations deeper. New hurricane-proof windows were set into concrete frames, the roof was removed and rebuilt, and the original tiles and the patina of the plaster fireplace were carefully preserved. The project took about four years. Throughout, Dirand immersed himself in Mizner’s doors, ceilings, and patterns so that the new rooms would be a true homage rather than a copy.

Early on, Brant handed Dirand two thick binders, one of artworks and one of design pieces. “This is what you can use,” he said. A few days later, on holiday with his family, Dirand spread every page across the floors. “I was walking on the paper,” he says. It reversed the usual order in which the architect designs the rooms and the art arrives afterward. Here the collection came first, though never rigidly, because “a collection is also something that is alive.”

A Diego Giacometti bench and A Jean Royère Liane six-branch floor lamp in a hall

A chair and floor lamp by Diego Giacometti stand next to the fireplace in the great room.

Jean-Michel Basquiat’s Per Capita, 1981, commands a wall of the loggia. Ours Polaire chair and Yo-Yo side table, both by Royère.

A family photo and fragrance bottles line up on a windowsill in Seymour Brant’s bath.

Warhol’s Big Campbell’s Soup Can with Can Opener (Vegetable), 1962, hangs in the stairwell, which also features a Jacques Adnet wall light.

At the heart of the home are two main spaces, the great room and the loggia, which are perhaps the spaces that both Dirand and Brant are most proud of. The great room is centered around a monumental fireplace that has the grandeur of a castle. Commanding one wall is a rare carved-stone console by Alberto Giacometti, nearly three meters high and crowned with a bas-relief of a female figure, made for Jean-Michel Frank’s important 1938 commission for the Born family in Argentina. It was one of a pair, and Dirand says no one knows where the other is today. Beside the fireplace hangs Warhol’s 1963 White Disaster (White Car Crash 19 Times), one of Brant’s most recent and important acquisitions; he had wanted a Car Crash painting since the ’60s. Prouvé furniture from Brazzaville, weathered by the Congo climate, sits amid Native American and African objects, and at the center of it all is a mirror polish copper table Dirand designed himself to reflect Giacometti’s bird vase and the fireplace behind it. Brant calls the table a masterpiece.

Art in the main entrance includes, from left, pieces by Jean-Michel Basquiat, Richard Prince and Cindy Sherman, and Brice Marden. Furniture includes an Ours Polaire Chair and sofa and a kidney-shaped coffee table, all by Jean Royère, and a Charlotte Perriand stool. a 1936 Jean-Michel Frank settee stands in the background.

A glimpse into the primary bedroom, where a Jean-Michel Frank table lamp sits atop a bedside table.

A wall niche holds a 2023 ibis sculpture by Harumi Klossowska de Rola

The loggia is lighter and more playful, and its original black, yellow, and turquoise tiled floor and Egyptian-style column capitals offer a glimpse of Mizner at his most adventurous. One of the most prominent works hanging in the room is a monumental 1981 Basquiat painting, Per Capita, which Brant bought from Annina Nosei’s memorial show for the artist. At the center of the room stands an Ettore Sottsass Tower Cabinet, designed in the early 1960s, which Dirand admits he didn’t understand at first, until Brant showed him exactly how to place it. Now he can’t imagine the room without it.

Upstairs, Dirand took down walls so that the primary bedroom runs the full width of the house, capturing light from sunrise over the ocean to sunset over the garden. In Seymour Brant’s bathroom, Estremoz marble walls are carved to evoke curtains, and beyond it lies her dressing room, an intimate boudoir. Brant’s bathroom, by contrast, is a marquetry of white and gray marble built around a pair of Glenn Ligon paintings. Dirand calls the room “minimalist ornamental.”

A 2023 flamingo sculpture by Harumi Klossowska de Rola (one of a pair) stands on the terrace.

In Seymour Brant’s bath, François-Xavier Lalanne’s Singe II, 1999, sits between a stool by Diego Giacometti and a bespoke bench by Philippe Anthonioz.

Every piece in the house comes with a story, and Dirand heard many of them over years of breakfasts, lunches, and dinners with the couple. “Every single piece could be a book,” he says. During those conversations, the collaboration between Brant and Dirand grew into a close friendship. Brant’s other great passion is horse racing, and his formidable stable has included Sottsass, the 2020 winner of the Prix de l’Arc de Triomphe, Europe’s most prestigious race. One day, Brant called Dirand with news: Sottsass had a son, a young thoroughbred already winning races of his own. He had named him Dirand.

#4874. Are You Hanging On to a Negative Story That Keeps You Stuck?

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 15, 2026 | Reviewed by Abigail Fagan

This morning I was in the dog park with Bucket, my Cocker Spaniel. Bucket’s favorite thing to do is chase sticks; he’d rather chase sticks than eat, sleep, or do almost anything else. Each stick seems to bring him unabashed and unwavering delight, as is evident in his dog smile. That’s the story I’ve written for him anyway… and I’m “sticking” with it.

During this morning’s “sticking” session, Bucket discovered a gigantic, thick, and heavy, unwieldy branch, which he managed to get between his jaws and then proudly carried around in his mouth, snubbing my attempts to offer him smaller, more navigable options. After some time, I noticed that he was starting to notice the squirrels running up a tree, and then another couple of dogs arrived, and I saw him staring at them seemingly with longing, as if he wanted to say hello and play.

Several times Bucket attempted to approach the dogs or run at the squirrels, but each time he tried, he was stymied by his big stick, which kept getting caught on everything he encountered. So too, the other dogs moved away when they saw the big stick coming. Bucket continued to grip onto it because he didn’t yet realize that he had the power to open his jaws and release it. He didn’t understand, yet, that the choice was his; he was the one grasping the very thing that was causing his limitation.

We sometimes do the same thing as humans: we hold onto our self-constructed stories long after they serve us, and long after they’re true in reality. Bucket eventually dropped his stick, but we humans often don’t, and continue living out a frustrating life, unknowingly depriving ourselves of what we really want—gripping onto and remaining loyal to our story, but not to ourselves. We stick with our stories even when our stories keep us stuck and cause us suffering.

If someone’s narrative, for example, is that they are unlovable, even in the face of someone loving them, they reject that truth, dismiss what’s happening in reality, the love they’re receiving, and continue insisting on their unlovable “me” story—the very story, paradoxically, that makes them suffer. Reality be damned, they are loyal to their story, not the facts, and not even to their own happiness.

We may stick with our story, even a negative story, simply because we don’t realize that it’s a story. We’re not aware of the fact that much of what we call “me” is a story, something we construct, and can in fact deconstruct. But we think our assumptions about ourselves, our “me” story, are fundamental truth—just who we are. The problem is that our “me” story is inside the lens through which we see everything in our life. The “me” whose identity story we’ve created is the one interacting with and experiencing reality, and reporting back to us on it. And what we experience is inherently interpreted and corrupted through the beliefs of this self-made “me” lens. In order to free ourselves and take off our “me” lenses, we need to see the seer, to get behind the lens through which we’re looking. We need to ask ourselves again and again what we believe about ourselves before anything else happens; what we are coming into the situation already assuming. We have to get behind the eyes through which we’re looking at and experiencing our life. When you can look at the looker, and question the story about the “me” you’ve constructed, then things change.

Our stories are often built around wounds that we’ve suffered. If, for example. our self-story is that we have to do everything ourselves, no one can help us, it’s not because it’s fun to believe that or feel that way. At some point, we probably needed help and didn’t receive it. Our early caretakers may have been unable to give us what we needed. Maybe they didn’t care or were limited and unable or unwilling to help us. The point is we felt alone. And so we created this story of being fundamentally un-helpable. If we’re somebody who has to do everything for ourselves, and no one can help; if we can know that for sure. If it’s set in stone, then we don’t have to mourn the fact that we experienced it; it’s just what is—our karma, if you will. Knowing that this is who we are, we feel more in control; we don’t have to hope for help or be sad or angry about the fact that we didn’t receive it. We don’t have to question why this happened to us, or continues to happen now, because we reject and dismiss the help that’s offered and any evidence that we’re not “all alone.” Furthermore, we don’t ever have to grieve how much we wanted and needed help, and suffered without it. We hold onto the past, counter-intuitively, as a way to not have to mourn and accept the past.

With this as our self-story, locked in place, we can know the kinds of things that happen to us. We don’t have to face the uncertainty of each moment, how it will go, and whether or not we will receive what we need. We don’t have to be vulnerable and ask for help when we need it, because we know we’re not going to get it. Even though our story is not what we want, even though it impedes our connection with others and prevents the opportunity to feel gratitude, even though it gets in the way of our satisfaction and happiness, nonetheless, it becomes our identity and a way to protect ourselves, avoid grief, shame, uncertainty, and the hope that things really can change. Our self-story, as awful as it is, becomes a kind of security blanket. We are secure in the knowing that we won’t get what we need.

The story we’re holding onto about who we are might not be true, that is, if we stopped proving it were true. It might be something we could drop, like Bucket and his stick. But to let it go can feel even more painful than sticking with our negative story. To let it go can feel like we’re abandoning our suffering, moving from it as if it’s no longer part of us. Paradoxically, it feels too painful to move on from our story of pain.

But getting free is not about dropping your story as if that experience never happened. It is about honoring the wound that the story triggers, grieving and acknowledging what you have experienced—but—without the assumption that this present moment is going to be the same, and without the story that this is who you are, a person whose life is always “like this.” The wound matters, the story can be dropped.

Are you carrying a stick that you're ready to drop?

Nancy Colier, LCSW, Rev., is a psychotherapist, interfaith minister, and the author of The Emotionally Exhausted Woman, Can’t Stop Thinking, The Power of Off, and Inviting a Monkey to Tea.

#4872. How One Creative Couple Found Harmony in Their 1920s Los Angeles Craftsman

Nothing about where interior designer Harrison Soll is now was part of the plan. Five years ago, he was focused on his work, and he didn’t want any distractions. But when he met Rodney Burns, a stylist and owner of the legendary Los Angeles vintage gallery, Church Boutique, it set everything on a new path. They met in the fall; by New Year’s they were a couple. “I didn’t think it was going to be a relationship,” Soll says. He credits their shared interests for bringing them together: “We always found a throughline with design.”

They now live in a 1925 craftsman house in Laurel Canyon. Soll took the lead decorating the nearly 1,400-square-foot home in a combination of his and Burns’s styles with many pieces coming from Burns’s eclectic collection of vintage art, furniture, and sculpture. Burns has worked with Lenny Kravitz as a stylist and creative collaborator for the past 15 years, and the singer says he admires what Burns and Soll have created together. “The mix of Rodney’s early- to midcentury style mixed with ethnic expression next to Harrison’s bold minimalist modern approach seamlessly blend together,” Kravitz tells AD.

The backyard of Soll’s and Burns’s Laurel Canyon home is a bricolage of funky, inherited stonework and vintage pottery.

The nail art above the fireplace has a cute backstory story: Burns and Soll had seen it at a vintage shop in Santa Monica and Burns regretted not buying it. When he went back to purchase the piece, it was marked as sold. But only because Soll had already covertly bought it as a Valentine’s Day gift for Burns.

The giant Sergio Bustamante giraffe bust in the foyer is a delicate topic. “The giraffe was one of our biggest fights in decorating this house,” Burns explains. “I was determined to have that giraffe live some place in this house because he’s just fantastic, and the scale of him, and the rarity of him.” Except, counters Soll, that the placement of the giraffe is treacherous because the front door swings right in its path. “You just have to be careful,” Soll says.

The foyer—which features a collection of masks and vintage paintings—was one of the most difficult areas to style, explains Burns. The couple was trying to be selective of what pieces to incorporate from Burns’s massive collection and at one point Burns suggested putting all of his masks in storage to save for another house down the line, or passing them onto Burns’s vintage boutique, Church. Soll dissented, saying, “No, let’s put them everywhere.”

The living room features two sofas—a Joseph D’Urso for Knoll piece reupholstered in leopard fabric and a leather De Sede Terrazza. The carpet is the Antilocarpa style from Stark. A trio of fiberglass shell art was sourced through Kelly Wearstler. The art behind the sofa is a manipulated canvas garment bag by the artist Roosevelt “Rip” Woods, the first black artist to have a solo show at the Phoenix Art Museum.

As renters, there were naturally limitations on what could be achieved. Soll got permission to retile a bathroom and install a new vanity. The doe-patterned wall-to-wall carpeting in the living room was an “ask for forgiveness” move, but his landladies ended up loving it. Burns, on the other hand, needed more convincing. “The carpet took many dinners of conversations between the two of us,” Soll says.

Soll describes himself as more of a minimalist while Burns avows, “I’m a maximalist to the umpteenth power.” The couple attempted to strike a balance while still filling the space with character. Some rooms nod to Soll’s leanings toward minimalism like an office with a midcentury desk and a 1970s tapestry and the dining room where a Sergio Bustamante table and a Chinese Art Deco rug are the main attractions.

When decorating their Laurel Canyon home, Soll and Burns had to figure out how to combine their opposing styles, with Soll being more of a minimalist and Burns being a maximalist. But, says Burns, “[Soll] has made me be able to take a pause and then also make me a little less attached to some of the things that I’ve collected  and given me an opportunity to maybe show a little bit more restraint.” In the corner, a brass Maison Jansen palm tree mingles with a Sergio Bustamante monkey.

Burns, who has been styling Lenny Kravitz for over a decade, says his approach to home design and fashion are one in the same: “Building an outfit, building a room: same thing in my opinion.”

Kravitz offers these kind words on Burns: “What I love about Rodney is that he is limitless in his concepts and always thinks outside of the box. His visual vocabulary enables him to mix a vast stew of eras and genres. He pays tribute to our ancestors and uplifts new innovators. Always soulful, expressive, and elegant.”

In the dining room, Soll placed a Sergio Bustamante table on a diagonal to make enough room for the couple’s large dog. “She’s a girthy girl,” says Soll, referring to the table, not the dog. The chairs are by Paul Evans and the Chinese Art Deco rug was sourced from Ariana Rugs. The Tom Greene chandelier was sourced from Burns’s boutique, Church.

Burns’s maximalism is on display in the living room, where a couch upholstered in leopard Scalamandré mixes with zebra-hide chairs and a sinuous De Sede sofa. (That animal print couch, Soll wants you to know, also features a block of leather upholstery: “I just think it would have been too maximalist to do a full Scalamandré sofa.”)

The living room is filled with brass sculptures (a Maison Jansen palm tree and a Sergio Bustamante monkey), bronze figures sourced from an estate sale in Arizona, and several pieces of sculptural artwork.

The bedroom mixes patterns and textures with a stainless steel bed from TTMM, Paul Evans “Cityscape” nightstands, striped bedding, and antique bronze table lamps with African mud cloth shades.

The 1970s textile art in the office is a vintage find of Burns’s that Soll loves. “I used to go to Rodney’s apartment when we first started dating, and I would be like, ‘Oh my God! Like, if we break up, I’m never gonna be able to find this tapestry,’” he says.

Soll got permission from his landladies to retile the bathroom and add a new vanity. The metal Curtis Jeré artwork comes from Burns’s collection.

The bohemian architecture of the home lends quirkiness to the design. The main bedroom isn’t a perfect square, making it seem like a stainless steel bed from TTMM is placed on a diagonal but is actually following the geometry of a very funky, angular bedroom.

The couple has taken their time tinkering and adjusting the decor, taking a considered approach to collecting and combining their belongings. Soll describes the overall vibe as having an Afrocentric theme that’s also a little bit Ralph Lauren. “Our visions have mingled,” Burns says.

Still, there is one last item the couple needs to complete the design: a blanket basket. But, says Soll, “We still haven’t found one we agree upon.”

The backyard is also home to a shed (not pictured), which functions as Burns’s closet.

#4870. 17 Best Airbnb Cabins for a Picture-Perfect Outdoor Retreat (2026)

Picture this: A wood-burning stove, a claw-foot tub, no cell service, and no news. Are you in heaven? Or are you in one of the best Airbnb cabins we’ve rounded up across the US? Whether it’s an organic design marvel in Washington that would make Frank Lloyd Wright weep or a sauna-equipped “bird house” cabin in Wisconsin that piques your interest, there are so many places to enjoy the great outdoors in style. So, if immaculate cabin rentals make up your fall and winter travel mood board, look to these cozy cabins, tiny homes, chalets, and comfortable cottages for a weekend getaway. Think: elevated A-frames and Lincoln log-style beauties, fleshed out with curated amenities and thoughtful design details. Here, you can get a little lost in the beauty of the wilderness (without giving up too much in the way of modern accommodations).

Perks: Indoor fireplace, private patio, barbecue grill, sound system, security system, free parking

A steeply pitched roof and black metal siding give this tiny cabin near Oregon’s Mount Hood a striking silhouette against the towering pines. Designed by a professional architect, the 190-square-foot retreat takes its cues from shinrin-yoku, the Japanese practice of forest bathing. The exterior pairs dark metal with warm pine cladding, while oversized windows look out to picturesque woodland views. Inside, there's a sound system, queen bed, wood-burning stove, and compact kitchenette, but the real luxury is falling asleep to natural white noise from the surrounding forest. Even the shower comes with a forest view, making this a particularly appealing place to spend a weekend off the grid. From $381 per night.

Perks: Mountain views, forest views, private balcony, fireplace, fire pit, nearby hiking trails, nearby skiing, private security

At nearly 11,000 feet above sea level, this Breckenridge cabin puts you practically eye to eye with Colorado’s towering Quandary Peak. Getting there involves climbing about 60 steps through the evergreens, but the payoff is a private balcony with sweeping mountain views. Inside, the renovated retreat has a Scandinavian-inspired aesthetic, with warm wood finishes, cozy textiles, and a gas fireplace for snowy evenings. A spiral staircase leads to the sleeping quarters, while outside, a firepit offers another spot to take in the alpine scenery. Downtown Breckenridge is just 15 minutes away, though between the mountain views and the deck, there’s plenty of incentive to stay put. From $365 per night.

Perks: Mountain view, wood-fired hot tub, indoor fireplace, fire pit, private backyard, hiking, free parking

The Blue Ridge Mountains are the main attraction at Doah House, a cabin perched high above Virginia’s Shenandoah Valley. A 16-foot wall of sliding glass doors opens onto a wraparound deck, making the one-of-a-kind forest views visible from anywhere in the living room. Inside the blonde wood interiors, a wood-burning stove, heated floors, and a lofted bedroom equipped with a queen-sized bed add to the appeal. The bathroom features Moroccan tile, a rainfall shower, and a floor-to-ceiling window overlooking the trees. Outside, a private wood-fired hot tub and firepit make a strong case for spending the evening at the cabin, though Shenandoah National Park, Appalachian Trail hikes, and local wineries are all within reach. From $204 per night.

Perks: Mountain views, hot tub, sauna, fire pit, wood-burning stove, BBQ grill, heated bathroom floors free parking

Fresh eggs from the chicken coop, herbs picked straight from the garden, and a creekside sauna are just a few of the unexpected perks at Firelight at Shope Creek, a collection of eight cabins spread across 22 wooded acres outside Asheville, North Carolina. Cozy flourishes fill this 650-square-foot retreat with modern design, like a wood-burning stove, heated bathroom floors, and a spacious deck overlooking the surrounding mountains and meadows. There's also a private hot tub just steps from the king-size bedroom, ideally positioned for stargazing after dark. Guests can wander the property's private hiking trails, gather around the communal firepits, or settle into the covered outdoor pavilion, which has its own fireplace. And despite the secluded setting, downtown Asheville and the Blue Ridge Parkway are only about 20 minutes away. From $320 per night.

Perks: Hiking trails, swimming, hot tub, fire pit, sound system, fireplace, washer/dryer

During the day, a generous amount of sunlight pours into this secluded Maine cabin, illuminating thoughtful architectural details like floor-to-celing wood paneled walls and heightened ceilings. After sunset, a well-curated lighting scheme sets the mood, affording the opportunity to test-drive the property’s record player sound system and transform the main living area into a vinyl listening den. Don’t expect to spend all your time indoors, though. Outside of this modern cabin, there’s no shortage of adventures to explore, from swimming in the lake to hiking one of several nature trails close by. Boating rentals and the local golf course offer even more ways to spend your stay, as well as winter skiing or snowboarding just a 40-minute drive away. From $645 per night.

Perks: Ocean view, shared outdoor pool, sauna, fireplace, beach access

Private and serene, sweeping views of the Pacific Ocean surround this Sea Ranch cabin escape that meets the California coast. Best suited for a couples getaway or a duo vacation, an open floor plan and 30-foot-high ceilings ensure that the space doesn’t feel overly cramped, while oversized windows drench the rental in sunlight on cloudless days. Modern upgrades like minimalist furniture silhouettes, recognizable decor, and an updated kitchen bring contemporary flair to the home’s original timber frame built back in 1965. Outside, an expansive deck overlooking the nearby meadow is a natural place to enjoy morning coffee or your current novel. Walk along the bluff to one of several bordering beaches, or visit one of the private recreation centers to work on your tennis backhand or swimming skills indoors. From $681 per night.

Perks: Lake view, fireplace, BBQ grill, fire pit, free parking, washer/dryer

Tucked among the Wisconsin woodlands, MetalLark Tower is surrounded by impressive views, supplied by the nearby wildflower meadow and clandestine lake. The 800-square-foot home’s interiors are equally noteworthy, following modern Scandinavian design principles that make the space feel intentional and cozy. (Think: simple furniture silhouettes and a neutral color palette with light wood tones contrasted by stark black and grey accents). When you’re not admiring the dramatic floor-to-ceiling windows with stunning, 360-degree views of the region, the outdoor living area has lots to offer, like a lounge area, fire pit, and grill. From $379 per night.

Perks: Outdoor showers, hot tub, fire pit

Checking into this tree-flanked cabin in Wartburg feels like stepping into a luxurious nature retreat. Maybe it’s the pair of waffle robes hung outside the private hot tub deck, or the modern Adirondacks arranged around the fire pit facing the tree line. But that’s just the beginning of the relaxation you’ll experience in your secluded private rental. Here, sliding doors and windows invite you to commune with the great outdoors from every room, while the clean, Scandinavian-style interiors align with the space’s overwhelmingly calming atmosphere. The Solenne is a picture-perfect vacation spot for two, with a king-size floating bed that ensures no one is fighting for space. Consider staying here for a honeymoon, a romantic winter getaway, or a restful solo trip down south. From $219 per night.

Perks: wood barrel stove, sauna, access to 20 acres of land

Nothing says cabin quite like fire pits, fireplaces, saunas, and firewood: all key features of this off-grid cabin. Located in a rural, wooded area in New Hampshire, the 196-square-foot space is modest but well-stocked for seasoned outdoor travels. It runs on solar power, and features a kitchenette with a burner and butane stove as well as a private bathroom with a compostable toilet. It feels more like a campsite than a standard rental as you won’t have hot water or a refrigerator, but the simpler life does come with at least one special perk: You can sweat your stress away inside the wood barrel sauna. From $283 per night.

Perks: Sauna, fire pit, hot tub, washing machine and dryer

The fittingly named Bird House is a vision of Scandinavian restraint in Cascade, Wisconsin. It’s close to the cross-country ski trails of the nearby Kettle Moraine region, in case you need a quiet place to settle in after a day of adventure, and approximately one minute down the road from a winery known for its French-inspired vineyards. Glou glou! The property itself packs in a small kitchen and living area with a wood-burning stove, plus a serene bedroom and laundry room upstairs. A path trailing away from the house also leads you to a compact, infrared sauna that fits up to four people. For even more coziness, consider lighting up the fire pit outdoors or stepping into the bubbling hot tub on the back deck. From $305 per night.

Perks: Walk-in shower, outdoor deck, BBQ, fire pit,

Hi-vis SMEG appliances and colorful fiberglass chairs punctuate the white-walled, barebones look of this Scandi-inflected home in upstate New York. The small one-bedroom is linked to the property next door by a shared backyard and deck, so it’s a good opportunity to coordinate with a pal or two if you’re able to secure both places at once. In the mornings, sunlight streams in through the skylight overhead to bathe the place in warmth, while at sundown, the surrounding forests in Hunter invite you to tee up the outdoor fire pit and take it all in. From $218 per night.

Perks: full-service kitchen, hot tub, BBQ grill

The term “cozy” is often synonymous with “small.” Not so in this jaw-dropping A-frame that sleeps 10 of your luckiest pals. Surrounded by miles of woods, the location sets the stage for a tranquil escape, but those who are looking for a few activities to pass the time will be happy to discover its proximity to the Rockbridge Nature Preserve, as well as local wineries and breweries. While the white shiplap interior, floor-to-ceiling windows, and floating staircase create an airy feel, there’s also plenty of texture among the leather, wood and rattan furnishings to warm things up. From $882 per night.

Perks: rainfall shower, wood-burning fireplace

Visitors to this New England town—known to be one of the best ski areas on the East coast—often come to hit the slopes. But even those who have no interest in venturing outside on a cold winter day will relish their visit if they stay at this Scandinavian-inspired cabin. Light wood beams and moldings, thick rugs underfoot, and minimalist furnishings tempt even the most dedicated skiers to stay indoors. While you’re holed up in your new lodgings, enjoy a blissful soak under the rainfall showerhead or take in the view from the glass wall in the bedroom that offers an unobscured view during leaf-peeping season and beyond. From $545 per night.

Perks: outdoor patio, fully-stocked kitchen, luxury linens

An hour outside Boise, situated among the Rocky Mountains, lies a magical escape where you can leave all your stress on the car ride up. This A-frame cabin features luxury amenities, stylish furniture, and views for days (you can choose whether to enjoy them through the floor-to-ceiling windows or while relaxing in the hot tub on the deck). The wide-open, marble countertop kitchen is ideal for dining in with a hearty meal, while the circle of outdoor Adirondacks are the ideal place to gather after dark around the fire pit. From $225 per night.

Perks: dog-friendly, hot tub, indoor hammock

Perched above a river on a granite ledge, the amazing views are the cherry on top when it comes to this two-bedroom hideaway. Don’t sleep on the curb appeal—the asymmetric A-frame structure of the home is only the beginning of your visual journey. Inside, warm wooden walls, one-of-a-kind furniture, an indoor hammock, and a marble-clad shower await. The home is a 30-minute drive from the Mountain Loop Highway, which grants access to outdoor adventures in Big Four Ice Caves, Lake Twenty-Two, Heather Lake, and Gothic Basin, to name a few. From $466 per night.

Perks: Sound system, indoor fireplace, waterfront views

For a weekend getaway from NYC that isn’t the Catskills or Adirondacks, try the Easy A—a gorgeously appointed A-frame cabin just about a three-hour drive away on the Delaware River. This creekside property, located in the Pocono Mountains in Pennsylvania, was recently renovated with inspired decor choices like the oxblood and sky blue kitchen tiles. The entire cabin sleeps six and features indoor fireplaces and an outdoor fire pit for extra cozy stargazing at night. From $725 per night.

Perks: Electric vehicle charging, pontoon style rental, modern open kitchen

The entire interior of this two-bedroom cabin in northwestern Wisconsin deserves a round of applause, but it’s the full-size kitchen and its massive portal to the surrounding forest that we can’t stop thinking about. With some cabins, you’re sacrificing space for a larger living area (preferably centered around a fireplace)—the kitchen is often an area that gets overlooked in footprint. But not here; fully stocked with Instagram-worthy dinnerware, the dinner table easily becomes a place for refueling… and playing games… and catching up over morning coffee. The list goes on. When the weather agrees, head down to the lake for a dip. From $287 per night.

#4867. The Most Important Sign You're in the Wrong Relationship

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 15, 2026 | Reviewed by Abigail Fagan

Your new paramour seems too good to be true. Attentive and thoughtful, he or she says all of the right things and anticipates your needs and desires before you do. Yet as time goes on, you feel unsettled and insecure when you are together, and even when you are apart—due to your partner's demand for constant communication. You begin to desire time to yourself, even though you are just getting to know your new love interest. Have you suddenly become more independent, or is there something else going on?

The most important indication you are in the wrong relationship is not necessarily what your friends think about your paramour, or his or her public persona; it is how your partner makes you feel. Fortunately, we are all gifted with the intuition to detect potential discomfort or even danger when spending time with other people, especially one-on-one.

Shahida Arabi (2023)[i] investigated the impact of partner narcissism and psychopathy on partner post-traumatic stress disorder (PTSD), noting that dark personalities may shower romantic prospects with excessive attention and flattery commonly referred to as “love bombing,” as a tactic of manipulation, particularly at the beginning of a relationship. Yet she also notes that dark personalities shut down conversations or "stonewall," and even “gaslight” partners by questioning their memory, reality, or perception. Arabi explains that one narcissism subtype, grandiose narcissism, is linked with high amounts of self-esteem and self-confidence, which, as a practical matter, could cause a partner on the receiving end of such negative behavior to second-guess their perception and self-worth.

Fortunately, regardless of partner personality type, there are ways to recognize your discomfort before remaining in the wrong relationship. Here are a few clues.

If you feel uncomfortable and unsettled, it is better to pull back sooner rather than later, because mismatched affection tends to get worse, not better. Just like the dashboard warning indicators in your car are yellow before they turn red, a relational partner who green-lights your feelings regarding the progress and pace of your relationship will allow you to safely journey into a quality relationship.

[i] Arabi, Shahida. “Narcissistic and Psychopathic Traits in Romantic Partners Predict Post-Traumatic Stress Disorder Symptomology: Evidence for Unique Impact in a Large Sample.” Personality and Individual Differences 201 (February 2023): 1–6.
