#4837. 9 Best Laundry Hampers That Double As Decor (2026)

Nearly everyone has that one chair in their bedroom that isn’t really used for sitting. Instead, it’s the unofficial holding area for clothes that haven’t quite made it to the laundry room. A beautiful laundry hamper offers a much-needed alternative, rescuing yesterday’s jeans and that growing collection of gym clothes. And while a clothes basket might not be the most exciting purchase on your home decor shopping list, the right one can make a surprisingly big difference in how your space looks and feels.

Fortunately, designs have come a long way from the plastic bins you likely grew up with. Here and now, we’re seeing sculptural woven designs that could pass for decorative storage, sleek steel frames with a more industrial edge, and rolling laundry baskets that make transporting a heavy load of laundry a much easier chore. Some are compact enough to tuck into a narrow closet, while others are spacious enough to handle loads for an entire household. Below, we’ve rounded up our favorites that prove even the most practical household pieces can enhance your decor.

The handles on this trio of Quince baskets make it a breeze to transport this toteable vessel from your bedroom to the laundry room. Reviewers specifically called out that this basket is a great size for an average washing machine load. It might be pricier than your average hamper, but the quality of the durable rattan material more than makes up for it.

Handwoven in Ghana by Baba Tree artisans using durable elephant grass, Goodee’s tall storage basket’s intricate weave and structure add a surprising amount of warmth to the bedroom or bathroom. Its generous size leaves plenty of room for dirty laundry, but it could also double as a storage basket for extra blankets or anything else you’d prefer to keep out of sight. Each large laundry basket is crafted by hand, meaning no two are exactly alike.

The structured wooden frame does a lot of the work when it comes to this silhouette, reading more elegant than just having a softened hamper sadly slumped in your bathroom. Senior shopping editor Nashia Baker especially likes that the removable lid makes it easy to keep clothes out of sight and out of mind until laundry day. “This Zara Home basket is simple, but elevated,” she says. “The wooden frame is sturdy, and the same goes for the cotton basket, which is helpful for someone who stuffs their basket to the brim.” Shopping the hamper online, you might miss that the legs are equipped with brass wheels, but Baker points out it’s one of her favorite features, making it easy to move heavy loads without strain.

Quiet Town’s Canvas Laundry Hamper in pine brings a little more personality to laundry day. iIts rich green hue offers a welcome departure from the usual lineup of beige and gray laundry baskets. Double-sided organic cotton canvas and an interior layer of interfacing help the hamper hold its cylindrical shape, even when it isn’t overflowing with dirty laundry. The brand mentions that it’s easy to clean; a quick wipe with a damp cloth is all it needs to stay looking fresh.

Designed by Dario Antoniali for Italian textile brand Society Limonta, the Isacco Laundry Basket makes sorting your lights and darks dare we say… fun? Its slender iron frame supports a stretchy nylon bag that changes shape as it fills, giving it a sculptural quality that feels more like contemporary furniture than a household necessity. Two separate openings make it easy to sort clothes as you go, while the machine-washable fabric keeps maintenance simple. It’s a clever alternative to traditional divided hampers or having multiple laundry baskets.

Leave it to Yamazaki Home to make even dirty laundry look organized. The Japanese home goods brand’s take on the rolling laundry hamper has a tall, open metal frame silhouette that’s refreshingly minimal. Its built-in handle and wheels make transporting heavy loads far less annoying, and the deep basket comes in handy for anyone whose laundry pile seems to multiply overnight. There’s no removable bag or liner to wash or fuss with either.

This is the best laundry basket in terms of its ability to cameo as something other than a laundry basket. In fact, we’re tempted to use it as an impromptu side table, thanks to the smooth and sturdy wooden lid.

Hay’s line of furniture is an all-time AD favorite, so why not continue your love for the Danish brand by adding this soft-gray laundry bin with geometric lines into your design repertoire. It’s also narrow enough to fit tight spaces.

CB2’s laundry hamper skews more decorative than utilitarian. Its distinctive pebbled finish takes inspiration from shagreen, a richly textured material frequently used by French designers during the Art Deco era, giving the hamper a glamorous edge. An engineered wood frame gives it a clean structured shape, while the hinged lid keeps dirty laundry out of sight. There’s even a polyester-cotton interior laundry bag to keep clothes contained. It’s not designed to hold heavy-duty loads, but for a room where aesthetics reign supreme, this laundry hamper is one you’ll never feel compelled to hide.

#4835. What If Talking About Your Anxiety Isn't Helping?

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 15, 2026 | Reviewed by Jessica Schrader

“What’s on your mind today?” “What’s been going on lately?” “What is activated for you today?” “How have you been feeling?"

Over the years, I have cycled through various go-to opening questions in my anxiety therapy sessions. Sometimes I don’t have to say a word: A client pulls open their Notes app or a journal the moment they sit down on the couch.

Yet, even if I ask the perfect question, or my client has already prepared a list of subjects for the session, the truth is: talking about anxiety doesn’t always lead to healing.

Anxiety runs in a loop. By the time the anxious client has entered my office, they have probably gone over these worries many times. Anxiety likes to replay the same line of thinking until it blurs the border between truth and fiction.

Anxiety venting encourages speaking in extremes, which sometimes distorts our thinking. The other person in the conversation may feel the need to validate our emotions and be supportive, but our brains can interpret this social nicety as an agreement with the content of our worries.

When we engage in a monologue about our anxiety, we are effectively overthinking out loud.

Our brain is trying to self-soothe by overthinking. The hope is that thinking will lead to a solution, clarity, or insight, and then we will feel much better. But as we all know, overthinking our worries can be counterproductive at best and dysregulating at worst.

Thinking about and talking about worries can deepen anxiety. When we spiral down into our thoughts, we go deeper into our heads. We can get lost in the effort to figure out our thoughts.

As an anxiety therapist, my job is to break the cycle of overthinking and help my client spiral up into action. Sometimes, this means that we cannot spend our session talking about anxiety. We cannot analyze and replay the anxious line of thinking.

The remedy for overthinking is an action that takes you out of your head and back into your life. We change our behavior to change our mindset.

For certain people, such action takes the form of ERP (exposure response prevention). In this type of therapy, a person intentionally faces fears and resists compulsions (including overthinking).

Exposure therapy is quite different from traditional talk therapy. In this approach, a person creates and executes a brave to-do list (formally known as an exposure hierarchy). For example, a person worried about speaking up to their boss practices role-playing the conversation and then sends an email to get a meeting on the books. Someone anxious about driving at night practices driving two blocks in their neighborhood at dusk. The aim is to create a pathway toward facing fears rather than thinking and talking about them.

Exposure therapy means committing to action rather than staying stuck in the analysis of anxiety.

This is not to say that talking about one’s anxiety is never helpful. In some cases, the other person in an anxiety conversation is a valuable source of insight.

When anxiety has caused someone to depart significantly from reality, sharing one’s worries can be grounding. The second person might be able to show where the worrier has created anxious distortions—self-blaming, catastrophizing, or personalizing.

Talking about one’s anxiety also helps when someone feels very alone in their worries. Having a person to share with can help the worrier feel supported, even if the other cannot change their mindset.

Conversations about anxiety will happen, inside and outside of the therapy room. Following these guidelines can help make sure the talk is productive.

Anxious looping is all about repeating the same well-worn thoughts without making progress. Notice and stop yourself if you are doing this out loud. You are perpetuating a cycle.

Too often, we seek insight from another person, but then ignore their view. If you are deep in your anxious worry, someone else’s thoughts are likely a helpful anchor to reality. Do not immediately dismiss what you are hearing from your trusted confidante.

Discussing your anxiety can be productive to a point, but notice if it is interfering with taking real action. Anxiety can be paralyzing. Remember that spiraling up often involves taking one brave step toward a fear.

Be aware of how discussing your anxiety is taking you out of your present moment. Are you less connected to today because you are talking about fears for the future or worries about the past? Conversations can help people cope, but sometimes they pull people deeper into thought.

If you are stuck, take a break from talking about worries. Instead, today, do one small thing for yourself that brings joy.

Share this post Facebook Bluesky Linkedin Email

Natalia Aíza, LPC, is a therapist, writer, and mental health advocate. She is the author of three self-help books, including the forthcoming Anxious to Launch (New Harbinger, April 2027).

#4833. Bunny Williams Never Set Out to Collect Birdhouses—Now a Whole Village of Them Stands in Her Connecticut Garden

Among the apple trees at the edge of AD100 Hall of Famer Bunny Williams’s storied country garden in Falls Village, Connecticut, stands a church with a sensible tin roof; a thatched cottage; a pagoda; a red house flying a tiny flag that she takes for a general store; and a shuttered confection that suggests a Russian dacha. Each is raised high on its own post, finished on every side, painted and weathered like the structures of a settled town—and none has a door you could fit a hand through. It is a hamlet fashioned for birds, assembled building by building, the way real towns often grow: slowly, and without a master plan.

For many years as Williams and her husband, the antiques dealer John Rosselli, combed fields and fairs for Treillage, the much-loved shop they ran in New York City until 2015, the birdhouses kept finding her. They gathered on shelves in the hayloft of her barn. “When I had two, I didn’t know what to do with them,” she says. “Then all of a sudden there were 10.” She decided they had been in storage long enough.

A farmhouse with a side porch crafted by the late Larry Smith, a North Georgia birdhouse builder known for fashioning charmingly detailed structures with metal roofs.

An A-frame house embellished with gingerbread trim and decorative brackets

Williams, on a tree stump next to the pagoda. The notch in the base of the birdhouse indicates that it was probably designed to be mounted on a roof.

At that point, Williams and Robert Reimer, head gardener on her property, started the process of reclaiming a long-overlooked point of land. To create space and liberate a stand of existing apple trees, they removed invasive species, then planted oat grass, bird-friendly scrub plants, and fruit-bearing pawpaw trees. To create the neighborhood, she had uniform posts made—tall, “so that you look up at them,” each wrapped with a metal band to deter ambitious squirrels from climbing too high and disturbing the tenants she had in mind. Finally, up went the houses, now totaling 17.

The attraction had nothing to do with value or pedigree. “Most of these birdhouses are sort of folk art,” she says. “They were somebody having fun building an unusual structure.” Not Monticello in miniature, not a replica of someone’s own house, just unknown makers, each with “an eye and a kind of humor,” none of them trying to be perfect. The proof is in the details: the gingerbread scallops on one many-holed high-rise (“somebody had a great time with their little saw”), the thatched-roof cottage she prizes because she has never seen another like it, the weather vane crowning the would-be dacha.

A church with a brick chimney, an oversized cupola, and Gothic Revival design elements.

A rare combination house and feeder that evokes a neoclassical temple

Williams calls this one the general store.

A colonial revival house with a columned portico built by Larry Smith

“It has a kind of Russian dacha feeling,” says Williams.

What she didn’t have was history—the dealers who sold them seldom had any to pass along. The record, it turns out, is often recoverable. “The period from 1890 to 1930 is really the birdhouse’s golden age,” says Jacob Torkelson, an architectural historian and researcher at the University of Pennsylvania’s Stuart Weitzman School of Design, and among the very few scholars in the country studying these objects, whose makers and manufacturers he has painstakingly been identifying, where a name survives. Across those decades, government pattern books encouraged farmers to install birdhouses to reduce pesticide use (the birds would eat the insects), while mail-order companies offered them to homeowners as pure whimsy, not to mention a way of promoting biodiversity in their gardens. Shown photographs of Williams’s collection, Torkelson supplied some of what the dealers couldn’t. He traced one house down to the catalog page: the six-room Bungalow Martin House sold by the E.F. Hodgson Company, which listed the model from about 1917 until the firm closed in 1944. “Birdhouses are these wonderful mirrors to the human condition,” Torkelson says. “They tell us the values that we have as a society or as individuals, in our houses, in our gardens.”

A double porch house also built by Larry Smith

This pagoda-inspired birdhouse provides multiple roosting spots.

An unusual thatched-roof cottage prized by Williams, who has never seen another like it.

Williams isn’t finished collecting. Last May at Trade Secrets, the annual Litchfield Hills garden fair she cofounded in 2001 to raise funds for a local nonprofit domestic violence support agency, she stopped at the tent of Jim Sidon, whose company, Sparrow 10:29, crafts avian-size replicas of real buildings. Sidon, based outside Atlanta, came to the work after retiring from the printing industry. His first architectural birdhouse, built in 2017, was a replica of the 1792 Federal-style mansion in Newburyport, Massachusetts, where he and his wife had rented two rooms as newlyweds. It was Sidon who recognized, in three of Williams’s houses, the hand of his late friend Larry Smith, a North Georgia birdhouse builder known for his metal roofs and charmingly rustic details. Williams has asked Sidon to build her something new, though she is still deciding exactly what. Unlike her anonymous makers, Sidon numbers and signs every house. “My hope is that years from now, someone’s searching that out,” he says, “and when they open it up, they’ll see my signature.”

A dovecote-inspired birdhouse offers multiple residences

A picturesque house topped with a prominent weather vane

Drawing the eye like miniature garden follies, the houses rise on poles amid a stand of apple trees from ground planted with oat grass and bird-friendly scrub plants.

In Falls Village, the upkeep routine is minimal: Every couple of years the houses come down, receive a coat of wood preservative, and go back up into the weather. Sidon’s constructions, with cedar exteriors and sealed against the elements, ask even less. His replica of the Newburyport house has stood in his own garden for nearly a decade untouched. “I just hose it off,” he says. None of this troubles Torkelson, a preservationist by training. “These houses were made to be ephemeral,” he says. “They weren’t made to last forever, and they should absolutely be used.”

As for the intended residents? Williams thinks the birds mostly prefer making their homes in the trees, where they carry on, she says, like a symphony orchestra: “I’ve always felt that one of the reasons they don’t live in these houses is the birds think it’s suburbia. But they like the atmosphere.” Come winter, when the woods go bare and the village shows through the branches, Sidon would bet the houses do get used, songbirds crowding in two and three at a time for warmth. Williams can’t say—she has never once climbed up to look. “If they are nesting,” she says, “I don’t want to bother them.”

#4829. Why a Short Week Can Feel So Long

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 15, 2026 | Reviewed by Jessica Schrader

In interviews for my research on leaders, I asked executives to describe a difficult week. One leader talked me through an annual report, tax paperwork, fundraising, board work, and decisions about products and events. When I asked what was hard, she didn't single out any one task, but said, “It's not that those things are too much; it's just the changing of direction every day.”

She could handle the questions, meetings, and requests. What wore her down was having to stop all the normal stuff to deal with the annual report or tax filing, then switch to something else, and then figure out where she'd left off.

I drive my ninth-grade son to school, and yesterday he said it “felt like a long school week.” Interestingly, he had Tuesday off, an early dismissal Wednesday, and a late start Thursday… he actually spent less time at school. So, how could it feel longer!?

It reminded me of the leader story. He and I talked about routines and how little attention we pay to things when we know what comes next. Bottom line… it felt exhausting to have his normal schedule interrupted.

Let's go take a look at what research says about having to change directions so often.

What did our interrupted leader do? She tried to put some order around the week, setting aside one morning for product development and another stretch of time for board work. Then an event would need attention, or another responsibility would intrude. One day, a staff member left with little notice, and the website, online store, and event preparation were back on her desk. She could plan the week, but keeping it that way was basically impossible.

In 2001, researchers Joshua Rubinstein and his colleagues asked people to switch back and forth between simple tasks, like sorting shapes or doing math. Even small changes took extra time. Harder tasks made it worse, while a clue about what was coming next helped. This wasn't the same as someone interrupting you at work, but it shows that changing gears isn't free. My son knew that his strange school schedule would be back to normal the following week, but the executive had no idea what would interrupt her next.

But the lost time isn't the only issue. I get to interview spouses in my work. The leader mentioned above? Her husband saw what those days did to her. He told me she came home worn out with a long to-do list still on her mind. When she couldn't finish it, he said it “really puts a burden... a strain, on her.” Researcher Sophie Leroy studied something similar in 2009. In two experiments, she found that people had trouble letting go of a task they hadn't finished. Even after moving on to something else, part of their attention was still on the earlier job. She called that “attention residue.”

Think about sitting down to talk with an employee while you're still worrying about the budget you left unfinished.

Here's the most surprising finding to me around this subject: in 2008, Gloria Mark and her colleagues had people do typical office email tasks. Some were interrupted by calls or instant messages. The interrupted people actually finished their work FASTER… and their work wasn't noticeably worse (although their email replies were shorter). So why not be interrupted all day if you’re more productive? Those interrupted workers felt more stressed, frustrated, and rushed, and said the work took more effort.

The executive's week wasn't hard because of any one task. It was having to stop, switch, and figure out where she'd left off, over and over again. My son's week wasn't harder because of the assignments. It was having to think about what comes next and what comes after and hold that in the front of his mind.

Remember Sophie Leroy's finding about “attention residue”? In 2018, she and Theresa Glomb tested a way to reduce it. In their studies, people took a moment before switching tasks to think about where they'd stopped and how they'd pick up again. They called it a “ready-to-resume” plan. It helped people focus on the new task instead of worrying so much about the unfinished one.

So, here's how to fix a heavy, mixed-up week. You're halfway through a report when the phone rings. Before taking the call, jot down where you stopped and what you'll do next when you return. That's it. Now you can give the caller your attention without trying to remember every detail of the unfinished report.

My son's schedule will be back to normal next week. The executive can't count on that. But when she has to change direction, she can take a moment to write down where she left off. That won't stop the interruptions, but it will keep her from spending so much time trying to get back to work.

To lead sustainably, when you have to stop what you're doing, take a moment to plan how you'll start again.

Leroy, S. (2009). Why is it so hard to do my work? The challenge of attention residue when switching between work tasks. Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes, 109(2), 168–181. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.obhdp.2009.04.002

Leroy, S., & Glomb, T. M. (2018). Tasks interrupted: How anticipating time pressure on resumption of an interrupted task causes attention residue and low performance on interrupting tasks and how a “ready-to-resume” plan mitigates the effects. Organization Science, 29(3), 380–397. https://doi.org/10.1287/orsc.2017.1184

Mark, G., Gudith, D., & Klocke, U. (2008). The cost of interrupted work: More speed and stress. Proceedings of the SIGCHI Conference on Human Factors in Computing Systems, 107–110. https://doi.org/10.1145/1357054.1357072

Rubinstein, J. S., Meyer, D. E., & Evans, J. E. (2001). Executive control of cognitive processes in task switching. Journal of Experimental Psychology: Human Perception and Performance, 27(4), 763–797. https://doi.org/10.1037/0096-1523.27.4.763

#4828. The 9 Most Beautiful States in the US

The most beautiful states in the US prove just how shockingly diverse the country is: volcanic beaches, glacier-capped peaks, canyon-carved deserts, and misty rainforests define their landscapes. What people have built in those places is just as varied, from a royal palace in downtown Honolulu to Mary Colter’s stone watchtower on the rim of the Grand Canyon to the cedar-shingled studios of a modernist craft school on the coast of Maine. These nine states stand out on both counts, for the scenery that defines them and the architecture that holds its own against it. Keep in mind that no ranking can settle which state is most beautiful—they’re all worthy of a visit.

Hawaii is far from a monolithic state, with scenery changing from island to island. In Honolulu on Oahu, ʻIolani Palace, completed in 1882 for King Kalākaua, is the only royal palace on US soil—and it had electricity before the White House did.

Near Diamond Head, Doris Duke’s oceanfront estate, Shangri La, pairs 1930s modernism with architectural elements she brought from Morocco, Iran, Syria, and beyond. It’s now a museum of Islamic art, with tours departing from the Honolulu Museum of Art.

As for the natural landscape, Kauai, known as the Garden Isle, is home to the Nā Pali Coast, where green ridges drop sharply toward the Pacific. On Maui, Haleakalā’s broad volcanic summit often rises above a blanket of clouds. And on the Big Island, Hawaii Volcanoes National Park actively showcases the raw power of volcanoes—if you’re lucky, you might even catch an eruption.

Wyoming’s most famous photo moment might be the Teton Range rising abruptly above the sagebrush flats of Jackson Hole, ideally with one of Mormon Row’s weathered homestead barns in the foreground. Nearby, the tiny log Chapel of the Transfiguration, built in 1925, was sited so that the picture window behind its altar frames the peaks where stained glass would normally go.

In Yellowstone National Park, it’s all about color: Grand Prismatic Spring’s vivid rings stand out among the forested landscape, while the Grand Canyon of the Yellowstone exposes ochres and pinks in its steep walls. And steps from its namesake geyser, the Old Faithful Inn—designed by a 29-year-old Robert Reamer and opened in 1904—wraps a soaring lodgepole-pine lobby around a massive stone fireplace. It’s considered the largest log structure in the world.

Florida’s beaches are perhaps the state’s most scenic draw, but the architecture is just as distinctive. In 1979, Miami Beach’s Art Deco District, roughly a square mile of South Beach, became the nation’s first urban 20th-century historic district on the National Register, with more than 800 buildings listed. Across Biscayne Bay, industrialist James Deering’s Vizcaya, a villa completed in 1916, faces the water with formal gardens by Diego Suarez. Addison Mizner gave Palm Beach its Mediterranean Revival look; his Via Mizner, a winding pedestrian lane off Worth Avenue built in 1923, survives today.

St. Augustine, founded by the Spanish in 1565, pairs the coquina-stone Castillo de San Marcos with Henry Flagler’s Hotel Ponce de Leon, a Spanish Renaissance–style palace from 1888 that’s now Flagler College. Architecture buffs should detour to Lakeland, where Frank Lloyd Wright’s campus for Florida Southern College is the largest single-site collection of his architecture in the world. And on the Gulf Coast, Sarasota’s midcentury modernists adapted the International Style to the subtropics, most famously in Paul Rudolph’s 1953 Umbrella House.

On Maine’s coast, Acadia National Park packs a surprising amount of scenery into a small area: granite headlands, spruce forest, offshore islands, and views of the Atlantic. Look closely, and you'll notice the park’s impressive stonework, too. John D. Rockefeller Jr. financed its network of carriage roads, built between 1913 and 1940, along with 16 of the 17 stone-faced bridges that carry them over streams and gorges—no two are alike.

Don’t forget the coastal villages, from ever-popular Bar Harbor and its shingle-style and Queen Anne houses to Stonington, a working fishing town on Deer Isle. Elsewhere on Deer Isle, Edward Larrabee Barnes’s 1961 campus for the Haystack Mountain School of Crafts, an underrated masterpiece of American modernism, steps down a granite slope toward Jericho Bay, its cedar-shingled cabins and studios linked by boardwalks.

California has a little of everything, from the architectural hubs of Los Angeles and San Francisco to breathtaking natural settings. In Yosemite, El Capitan and Half Dome are jaw-dropping from the valley floor, but even more so from the top for those daring enough to make the climb. Back down in the valley, the Ahwahnee hotel, which opened in 1927, appears to be built of granite and redwood—but the “wood” is actually fireproof concrete, poured into molds and stained to look like timber. In the desert, Palm Springs is a showcase of midcentury modernism, with Richard Neutra’s 1946 Kaufmann House as its best-known example.

But the California coast is undeniably the state’s most famous landscape, and the 656 miles of Highway 1 deliver architecture along with the views. Above San Simeon, Hearst Castle, which Julia Morgan designed for William Randolph Hearst over nearly three decades, crowns a hilltop overlooking the Pacific. Continue north past the bluffs of Big Sur and storybook Carmel-by-the-Sea to the Sonoma Coast, where the redwood-clad buildings of the Sea Ranch, a planned community begun in the mid-1960s, are tucked into hedgerows to leave the meadows and bluffs open.

New York State’s famous architecture isn’t limited to the Manhattan skyline—though the city certainly pulls its weight on the architecture front. In the Adirondacks, wooded mountains surround lakes such as Lake Placid and the three Saranac Lakes. The “great camps” that wealthy families began building along their shores in the Gilded Age turned the log cabin into high style. You can still stay in one: The Point, on Upper Saranac Lake, was built in the early 1930s as Camp Wonundra for William Avery Rockefeller II and is now an 11-room Relais & Châteaux hotel, the first in North America.

In the Hudson Valley, painter Frederic Church’s hilltop villa, Olana, features a design style he called “Persian, adapted to the Occident.” The Finger Lakes offer a gentler landscape of long, narrow lakes bordered by vineyards and small towns, plus the gorge at Watkins Glen State Park, where a stone path meanders past tumbling cascades and swirling pools. And in Buffalo, you’ll find Frank Lloyd Wright’s Darwin D. Martin House, one of his finest Prairie-style designs.

The Grand Canyon is a tough act to follow, but on its South Rim, architect Mary Colter spent decades designing buildings meant to hold their own beside it. Her Hopi House, which opened in 1905, is a multistory stone building shaped like a Hopi pueblo; her Desert View Watchtower, completed in 1932, is a 70-foot stone tower modeled on Ancestral Puebloan structures, with a Snake Legend mural inside by Hopi artist Fred Kabotie.

Sedona’s red-rock buttes are spectacular on their own, but the Chapel of the Holy Cross, a modernist concrete chapel built directly into a pair of sandstone buttes and completed in 1956, gives them a focal point. North of Phoenix, Paolo Soleri’s Arcosanti, an experimental desert town of concrete domes and arches begun in 1970, is still a work in progress. And in Scottsdale, in the foothills of the McDowell Mountains, Frank Lloyd Wright’s Taliesin West—his winter home and studio from 1937 until his death in 1959—is now a UNESCO World Heritage Site.

Texas’s landscapes range from the Chihuahuan Desert to the Gulf Coast to the bayous of Caddo Lake. In Big Bend National Park, the Chisos Mountains rise above dry plains, and the Rio Grande cuts through the narrow walls of Santa Elena Canyon. In Marfa, minimalist artist Donald Judd turned a former army post into the Chinati Foundation, a museum built around permanent, large-scale installations, including his 100 mill-aluminum boxes lined up inside two converted artillery sheds.

In Fort Worth, Louis Kahn’s Kimbell Art Museum, completed in 1972, draws architects from around the world to see natural light wash down its concrete cycloid vaults; Tadao Ando’s Modern Art Museum of Fort Worth sits just across the street. Central Texas has rolling hills, and in spring, bluebonnets and Indian paintbrush color the roadsides around Fredericksburg, a town with German heritage.

Mount Rainier is perhaps Washington’s most familiar landmark, its glaciers and snowfields visible above forests and summer wildflower meadows. The North Cascades offer even more peaks, with jagged ridges rising above turquoise Diablo Lake, while the trails in Olympic National Park lead through mossy rainforest and out to driftwood-strewn beaches.

Seattle holds its own against all that scenery. Downtown, the Central Library—designed by Rem Koolhaas’s OMA with local firm LMN and opened in 2004—wraps its stacked floors in a faceted net of glass and steel, while the Space Needle, built for the 1962 World’s Fair, remains the skyline’s signature. Outside Seattle, Port Townsend’s Victorian storefronts overlook the water, and in the Walla Walla Valley, vineyard rows and wheat fields spread across rolling hills, with the Blue Mountains rising beyond them.

#4826. Jensen Huang’s Houses: Exploring the Former Nvidia CEO’s $38 Million San Francisco Mansion and Other Properties

It’s been a big week for Nvidia co-founder Jensen Huang. The billionaire announced he is stepping down as CEO of the AI hardware giant following reports that he had at least two extramarital affairs: one with a suspected Chinese spy, and another with one of his company’s senior executives (other reports allege a third affair with another Nvidia employee). Huang will reportedly testify before Congress later this month to discuss his relationship with the alleged spy. We can only assume that the Taipei City native is weathering the storm at home, but where are Jensen Huang’s houses? Even Huang himself may be wondering about the answer to that question, if a recent New York Times interview is any indication (more on that later).

Unsurprisingly, the tech titan hasn’t strayed too far from Silicon Valley, where Nvidia has been based since it was founded in the mid-’80s, with his real estate purchases. After graduating from Oregon State University, where he met his wife, Lori Huang, Huang began buying properties in California’s Bay Area, and he and Lori still own two of them. So far, the superbillionaire’s only exception to that rule has been an estate on Maui in Hawaii. Time will tell if the scandal spurs any real estate changes for him.

One thing that will presumably change about Huang’s home life in the immediate future: footing the bill for his security team. Nvidia has, until now, covered the cost of its former CEO’s private security. The company spent about $4 million in 2026 on “home security, protection during personal travel, consulting, and drivers” for Huang, per the New York Post. Though that arrangement is likely coming to an end, that shouldn’t be too big a problem for Jensen; the scandal has put Nvidia’s stock—and therefore Jensen’s net worth—in a slight flux, but he is still worth an estimated $144.7 billion at the time of publication.

Read on for a rundown of Jensen Huang’s houses dating back to his arrival in Silicon Valley.

According to The New York Times, Huang married Lori shortly after his 1984 graduation from Oregon State University, where they met as lab partners in an electrical engineering class. The newlywed graduates hightailed it to Silicon Valley to start their careers as microchip designers, and Jensen bought his first house: a 1,500-square-foot three-bedroom home in San Jose, California. It’s unclear exactly when or for how much he bought the property, but Jensen sold it in 1988 for $185,000.

That same year, Huang and Lori moved into a larger San Jose home that they bought for $338,000. With 2,300 square feet of interior living space, it had four bedrooms. Huang lived here when he co-founded Nvidia in 1993; he sold the house in 2002 for $500,000, the Wall Street Journal reports.

From there, the Huangs moved into an even more upgraded residence: a 7,000-square-foot home in Los Altos Hills, a tony area about 15 miles from the Nvidia headquarters. They paid $6.9 million for the newly built six-bedroom dwelling in 2003. According to Realtor.com, the two-story house hosts a resort-worthy swimming pool. The Huangs still own this house.

A year after buying his house in Los Altos Hills, Huang paid $7.5 million for a Hawaiian estate. The oceanfront property is located within Wailea Resort, an exclusive gated community on the south side of Maui, per the Wall Street Journal. The contemporary-style mansion reportedly has seven bedrooms across about 8,000 square feet. The Huangs still maintain this residence too.

In 2017, Huang made his most recent real estate purchase: a newly completed $38 million mansion in the high-end Pacific Heights neighborhood on San Francisco’s Gold Coast. The residence is modern in style, with a curved roofline and a limestone exterior. It boasts 11,400 square feet of interior living space, with seven bedrooms, seven full bathrooms, three half-baths, a wine cellar, an outdoor kitchen, an elevator, a home theater, a gym with a sauna, a library, numerous fireplaces, and a fifth-floor observation deck with views of the Golden Gate Bridge. The house took four years to construct, its developer told the WSJ.

San Francisco’s famous Golden Gate Bridge.

This seems to be Huang’s primary residence—although, according to a September 2026 New York Times interview, he could not tell you where it is. “There are a lot of skills that don’t matter,” Huang told the outlet in response to a question about AI weakening students’ basic math skills. “My first confession, I actually don’t know my address.… One day I had to pump gas—it was a few years ago. They needed my ZIP code, and I panicked. I didn’t know my ZIP code. I don’t know my telephone number. I forget these things. I can live with it.”

#4823. This 377-Square-Foot Island Getaway in Denmark Is a Picture-Perfect Pint-Sized Retreat

A mini vacation home in Zealand, Denmark, is a particularly appealing retreat, no matter the season. With its rugged coastlines, forests, and small villages, the western side of Zealand offers an ideal setting for a break from everyday life. Nestled among trees is a tiny house that was designed to be as unobtrusive as possible. Its compact shape, natural color palette, and large windows allow it to blend almost seamlessly into the forest. Here, nature isn’t just right outside the door—it’s always present inside as well.

Surrounded by trees, this small wooden house in Denmark blends seamlessly with nature.

Named the EcoNeo House, the home was designed by the Danish architectural firm SAGA Space Architects, both as a family home and as a prototype for tiny house-style vacation homes. Although originally conceived as a summer house, the cottage has been insulated and heated, allowing it to be used even during colder weather. At only 377 square feet, it combines the essential features of a vacation home, sleeping up to six people. The design prioritizes a thoughtful use of the available space and forward-thinking architecture that blends into the landscape.

The spacious terrace is perfect for sunbathing and dining outdoors, especially in the summer.

Inside, the living and dining areas flow seamlessly into one another. Thanks to the large windows that offer views of the surrounding trees, there’s a connection between indoors and outdoors. The kitchen is set slightly apart, but opens up into the dining area, blending smoothly into the floor plan. Designed with functionality in mind, it’s still fully equipped with all the necessary appliances.

The dining area flows seamlessly into the kitchen. Additional storage space was created below the stairs on the left, which lead to the sleeping lofts.

Instead of breaking up the floor plan with additional walls, the architects made use of the home’s height: Stairs next to the kitchen lead to two sleeping lofts that offer a private retreat for up to four people. Storage was also a priority in the design. Items not needed on a daily basis can be stowed under the stairs, a clever solution that again makes the most of the small space.

Large windows let in plenty of natural light and create a connection between indoors and outdoors. Despite its compact size, the kitchen has all the necessary appliances.

There’s even a sauna, which can be converted into a sleeping space for two additional guests if needed. The generous window front offers a view of the surrounding landscape, while a cold plunge pool on the terrace is waiting for a refreshing dip afterward.

Through the sauna’s large window, you can enjoy the surrounding nature even while relaxing. It’s an appealing retreat, especially on chilly days. There’s also a cold plunge pool on the terrace.

The choice of materials complements the understated architecture. The floor’s wide Douglas fir planks introduce a warm color and vibrant grain. The use of wood is consistently carried through to other areas of the house, creating a visual connection between the living space and the sauna. Natural materials are also used in the bathroom, including Kolmården marble, a Swedish stone distinguished by its characteristic green hue.

Wood defines the interior and lends the rooms a natural warmth. Large windows bring the surrounding nature into the home, while the dining area is spacious even within the compact layout.

Together with the stones and the greenery outside, the result is a design that deliberately avoids contrasts and excess. “When designing the summer house, every detail was intended to reflect the harmony between sustainable innovation and timeless craftsmanship,” explains Niklas Munk-Andersen, architect and partner at SAGA Space Architects.

Beanbag chairs provide extra seating and blend harmoniously into the interior with their colors.

The Douglas fir floorboards also give the clean design a natural, tactile quality. The material is more than a surface design choice, instead it shapes the way that the rooms feel.

The bathroom features natural materials and Swedish marble finishes.

The sauna can also be converted into a sleeping area for two additional guests.

The compact design also has a positive impact on the home’s carbon footprint. According to calculations by SAGA Space Architects, the EcoNeo House produces approximately 386 pounds of CO₂ annually, whereas an average vacation home for six people is estimated to emit about 2,282 pounds. This means emissions are approximately 83% lower. In addition to the small living area, bio-based materials are the primary factor contributing to this difference.

The boundary between indoors and outdoors blurs, bringing nature into focus.

The architects also designed the construction process to be as efficient as possible by digitally planning the house, precisely prefabricating its individual components, and then assembling them on-site in a modular fashion. This approach not only simplified assembly but also minimized the impact on the site.

Thanks to its small footprint, this vacation home’s emissions are 83% lower than those of the average vacation home that can accommodate six people. In addition, the modular construction minimizes the impact on the natural environment.

The EcoNeo House demonstrates that sustainable construction isn’t just a matter of the materials used. The size of a building and how the available space is utilized also play a crucial role. Though it may sound small, 377 square feet can be perfectly sufficient for a getaway home—provided the floor plan, storage space, and amenities are all carefully designed.

Although primarily intended as a summer home, this small vacation home can also be enjoyed in colder months.

A skylight lets light into the upper sleeping lofts.

The small vacation home has two separate sleeping lofts accommodating four people.

#4821. Love Beyond Words

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 15, 2026 | Reviewed by Jessica Schrader

Many popular magazines and websites offer advice on how to improve "communication,” which they claim will improve marriages. Some of those same venues regularly publish diets and weight-loss tips. You probably know the research findings about the latter—they range from unhelpful to harmful. The better weight-loss tips are as informative as the better communication recommendations. Eat less, move more; speak respectfully, listen attentively.

When couples describe the early part of their relationships, they report that they communicated very well. (Not many newlyweds mouth the great marriage lament: "We just can't communicate!") The prevalence of communication tips in the media implies that for many people, something about marriage degrades the neurons responsible for communication skills.

Of course, problems in love relationships do not occur because people are too stupid to figure out common-sense methods of communication or too dispirited by relationship experience to remember how they used to communicate. It’s misleading to say that people in distressed relationships have communication problems at all, though it can feel that way in their frustration and sadness. It’s more helpful to recognize that they have connection problems.

Communication in love relationships is a function of emotional connection. When people feel connected, they communicate fine, and when they feel disconnected, they communicate poorly, regardless of their choice of words and communication techniques.

When people are emotionally disconnected, the use of communication techniques makes them feel manipulated, and not just because the most popular ones are unnatural, more suited for a therapist's office than a living room. There is usually a hidden agenda in the use of communication techniques—to manipulate partners into giving the response we think is right.

Almost all my newly referred couples feature one partner accusing the other of misusing the communication techniques they learned from previous therapists.

"Anyone with common sense could get this.”

"I'm validating you more than you're validating me!"

I've often heard people who've been through communication therapy take great care to use "I-statements" when addressing a partner: "I feel blamed right now," which is, of course, blaming the partner for blaming. These are not communication problems. The partners communicate in subtext exactly what they mean: "You’re failing me.”

People do not fight and stonewall for lack of communication techniques. They fight and shut down because they feel like their partners don't care or aren't interested in how they feel. They fight and shut down to numb the pain of disconnection.

Feelings of connection are rooted in attunement of emotional states. Though it doesn't have to be positive (you can be attuned to your spouse at the funeral of a loved one), attunement cannot exist in a state of emotional reactivity, when a negative feeling in one causes chaos or shutdown in the other. It is extremely difficult to regulate emotional reactivity with words, even when there is no hidden motivation to convey that the other is failing or defective.

Negative reactivity can be regulated into positive attunement only through interest and caring; they have to be interested in and show sympathy for each other. Interest and caring, like all emotional states, are conveyed primarily by facial expressions, body language, and tone of voice, not by words or communication techniques.

Don't think of how to get your partner to do what you want—or, if you prefer the euphemism, how to communicate. Rather, ask yourself these questions:

Do you want to feel emotionally connected with your partner?How curious are you to learn your partner’s perspective?Do you care how your partner feels right now?What do you love and value about your partner?

Having answered those questions, decide whether your connection is more important than the topic of your communication. You must convey that you will love and value your partner despite disagreement. Anything short of this devalues the connection—it's less important than what you want to talk about—thereby guaranteeing negative reactivity.

Think of times when you felt emotionally connected to your partner. Communication was not a chore that required techniques, strategies, precise timing, or careful choice of words. You were interested. You put things awkwardly sometimes, but it didn't matter, because you cared.

Emotional connection is a mental state and a choice. Early in your relationship, you chose to feel connected—just as, if you're thinking about communication techniques now, you may be choosing to feel disconnected.

Forget about communication techniques and choose to feel connected right now. If you do, you'll have a reasonable chance of your partner reciprocating. You can then communicate better. More importantly, you will move closer to re-creating a love beyond words.

Share this post Facebook Bluesky Linkedin Email

Steven Stosny, Ph.D., treats people for anger and relationship problems. His recent books include How to Improve your Marriage without Talking about It and Love Without Hurt.

Get the help you need from a therapist near you–a FREE service from Psychology Today.

#4820. 007 Off Duty: 10 Photos of Former James Bond Actors at Home

For a secret agent, James Bond generates a remarkable amount of public discussion. Barely a day goes by without fresh speculation about who will be the next actor to take on the role of 007. Callum Turner, Jack Lowden, and Josh O’Connor are among the names linked to the role, with recent reports suggesting the search has reached final screen tests.

Whoever takes over will join the small group who have played the part since Sean Connery’s debut in Dr. No in 1962. Away from the screen, the homes of these former James Bond actors reveal more about the men in the dinner jackets. These archival photographs find them settling into family life and indulging interests that occasionally followed them onto set. Below, find a little domestic distraction while we wait for the next Bond to introduce himself.

Sean Connery relaxing in his ground-floor basement flat in NW5.

Photographed at home in England in 1955, Connery is still around seven years away from his first appearance as James Bond in this shapshot. His surroundings are modest, with little suggestion of the polished interiors his screen alter ego was about to frequent. Connery established the role of Bond across six films in during the 1960s and early 1970s.

Landing the role of James Bond required a little wardrobe planning on George Lazenby’s part. According to the official Bond website, the former model bought an unused suit made for Connery, then visited his predecessor’s barber at the Dorchester to request the same haircut. Producer Cubby Broccoli happened to be in the neighboring chair.

George Lazenby pictured at his home in Kensington, London, 1973.

His only appearance as 007 was in the 1969 film On Her Majesty's Secret Service. It gave Bond one of his most personal storylines, with Diana Rigg playing his ill-fated bride, Tracy. By the time of the Kensington photograph shown above in February 1973, Lazenby had already left the role. Seated with papers in hand, he is surrounded by books, records and a guitar propped beside the sofa. The glass-topped coffee table is crowded with objects, including a decorative horse’s head, while a substantial wooden cabinet provides storage behind him. Later that year, Roger Moore made his Bond debut in Live and Let Die.

Roger Moore reading the dictionary at his home in Los Angeles, circa 1955.

Roger Moore had yet to order his first on-screen Bond martini when he was photographed at home reading a dictionary, while working as a television actor.

Roger Moore kissing his wife, Welsh singer Dorothy Squires, outside their home in Bexley Heath, London, in 1957.

He married Welsh singer Dorothy Squires in 1953, and they lived primarily at The Mount—a mansion on Wansunt Road in Old Bexley, Kent.

The couple divorced in 1968, and a year later, Moore married Luisa Mattioli. Sherwood House in Denham, located close to Pinewood Studios, served as the couple's base during Moore's first three Bond films. In his autobiography, he described the property as “near London and perfect for a growing family.”

Its appeal extended to 11 acres of grounds, which included a tennis court, heated swimming pool and greenhouses when the house was advertised for £5 million in 2015.

When he finally took on Bond's on-screen persona, Timothy Dalton was well-prepared for the role.

Producers considered Timothy Dalton for Bond early in his career, but he felt too young for the role. When he eventually signed on for The Living Daylights in 1986, he reread Fleming’s novels to prepare for a more serious interpretation of the character.

Pierce Brosnan at home in Los Angeles in 1983.

Pierce Brosnan was making his name in Remington Steele when he posed at his Los Angeles home in June 1983, but his introduction to the world of Bond had come through his first wife, actress Cassandra Harris. She appeared in For Your Eyes Only, and visiting her on location in Corfu brought him into the producers’ orbit. Brosnan was offered the role in 1986, but was prevented from taking it due to his television commitments at the time. His debut in GoldenEye came almost a decade later, in 1995.

Pierce Brosnan playing pool with his son, Sean, at their Los Angeles home in 1992.

This 1992 photographs show Brosnan playing guitar on the sofa, shooting pool with his eight-year-old son Sean, and entertaining two dogs in the garden. Cream sofas and floral upholstery adorn the living room, while arched windows and white shutters open the space to the surrounding greenery. Taken the year after Harris died of ovarian cancer, the photographs capture Brosnan bringing up their family before Bond transformed his career.

Pierce Brosnan with his dogs outside of their Los Angeles home in 1992.

Painting had become a creative outlet for the star during Harris’s illness. Having trained as a commercial artist before acting, Brosnan returned to it in the late 1980s, recalling on his website: “I started painting again and out came every colour.” By the time he became Bond, painting was so sufficiently embedded in his life that his dressing room at Leavesden doubled as a studio during GoldenEye.

Daniel Craig attends the Bond 25 film launch at Ian Fleming's Home, GoldenEye, in Montego Bay, Jamaica, 2019.

For this final entry, the front door stays closed. Of all the former James Bond actors, Daniel Craig represents a more guarded approach to sharing domestic life. His wife, actress Rachel Weisz, explained the couple’s position in a 2012 conversation with British Vogue: “I think it’s pretty ordinary to think that marriage is private and sacred.”

Speculation over who will inherit Craig's martini glass keeps his name in the headlines even after his departure.

#4817. 19 Best Christmas Ornament Picks, Vetted by AD (2026)

There’s no universally correct way to decorate a Christmas tree, though everyone seems to have an opinion about it. Some people swear by coordinated color palettes and perfectly spaced ornaments. Others treasure the funky glass baubles that have graced their family trees for decades, a hand-carved wooden figure picked up at a German Christmas market, or the lopsided ornament made in elementary school. The sentimental ones usually come with better stories. A Christmas tree, after all, is as much a collection of memories as it is a decorating opportunity.

And yet, there’s always room for one more. Maybe it’s a handblown glass ornament, a miniature version of your favorite food, or a decoration that’s so uniquely you it somehow feels essential. Your tree doesn’t need a theme, and your ornaments certainly don’t need to match. We’ve rounded up our favorites, including sculptural glass baubles, handwoven treasures, cheerful little oddities, and keepsakes you’ll look forward to unpacking every December.

These 3D-printed ornaments have the nostalgic glow of vintage Christmas lights, even when the tree is unplugged. Designed by Austrian architecture firm Sheyn, their sculptural shapes give the classic holiday bauble a whimsical, modern twist. The watermelon and grapefruit hues are a refreshing departure from the usual red and green.

Sleek, understated, and not the least bit fussy, this Villeroy & Boch set deserves a moment beyond the tree. Tuck a few into a wreath or garland, where they won’t have to compete with a tree’s worth of tinsel and twinkling lights.

Trade sparkles for natural texture with these ornaments handcrafted by Colombian artisans in the Andes. They bring a well-traveled touch to your tree, and the best part is that each one is a surprise, with its color combination chosen by the maker. They’re also far less fragile than glass when your dog’s tail inevitably sends one flying.

If you’ve ever rearranged a vase just to catch the afternoon light, you’ll appreciate the way these ornaments from il Buco Vita play with it. Handblown in Italy from recycled glass, each one has subtle irregularities and shifting blue-green hues that take on a new life among the twinkling lights on a Christmas tree. This delicate floral shape is pretty enough to keep out long after the tree comes down.

Here’s a new tradition for the little ones: Unwrap one of these miniature porcelain ornaments each day like an advent calendar as you sing your way through the Twelve Days of Christmas. The set from Lenox includes all the familiar characters, like the partridge in a pear tree and the twelve drummers drumming. Apologies in advance for the song getting stuck in everyone’s head.

Balsam Hill’s expertise with high-quality trees extend to their collection of decorative baubles. Don’t underestimate the power of classic shapes—like a set of metallic orbs in varying sizes.

Your love of rattan doesn’t have to stop at your furniture. These woven bell ornaments bring a little natural texture to the festivities, with warm, honey-colored tones that look right at home among your Christmas tree’s evergreen branches.

You don’t need an Eichler home to have a very merry, midcentury Christmas. With their geometric silhouettes and gold detailing, these glass ornaments bring a dose of vintage glitz to the tree.

Leave it to Alessi to make Grinch green look downright elegant. These blown-glass ornaments, designed by Nika Zupanc for Alessi, dress up the famously mischievous shade with graphic stripes, playful polka dots, and touches of gold. Even the Grinch might reconsider his stance on Christmas.

Playing with sizing is key to adding dimension to your Christmas tree and these darling miniature ornaments offer a rainbow of colors to dial up the saturation on your tree. They come with mini ribbons that you’ll need to hand knot, but we think they add an extra personal touch.

Nostalgia looks best in a brass frame. With its braided border and antique-inspired finish, this ornament from McGee & Co. makes a thoughtful gift—just print a favorite photo and tuck it inside before wrapping.

When in doubt, put a bow on it. These delicate velvet bows are slightly understated; pin them to the tree in lieu of ornaments for a charming coquettish vibe or have the two join forces for maximum drama.

Since your order for the table is always a dozen oysters on the half shell, why not dish out the same for your Christmas tree? And if you’re trying to nail down a fête-themed tree, you’ll probably want some Champagne to go with it as well.

Our love of gnomes is no secret, and a set of ten might just be enough for a holiday fix. Each fabric gnome measures 5.7" x 2.2" and can be hung from your tree or placed around the house as holiday decor.

Pescatarian or tinned fish enthusiast, this glitter-flecked glass Christmas ornament is a lighthearted way to tell your favorite “girl dinner” guest how much you appreciate them. Coming in at 4" tall and 1.5" in diameter, it’s the perfect size for anchoring a branch.

You’ll thank us when you’re looking back at photos of the tree in January and admiring how dimensional it looks even from a phone screen. This set includes a collection of matte, metallic, and flocked ornaments so you don’t have to over think the process and you don’t have to go crazy buying individual ornaments.

This French bread is made of glass, so it will really shine against your tree lights. It’s got a kitschy yet vintage vibe, and it will stand out from other icicle-style ornaments.

Vintage-inspired with wintery botanical etchings, these glassy ornaments have some rustic appeal but still feel elegant. We’d pair them with some silver tinsel to really bring the sparkle.

Handblown from clear, silver glass, these finials and spheres add some metallic elegance to your tree, especially if your inspiration is a disco ball.

#4816. 6 Best Bamboo Sheets That Mirror Silk, Tested by AD (2026)

If you love the slight sheen and smooth feel of silk bedding like me, my guess is that bamboo sheets are probably up your alley too. The natural material can make your bedroom look elevated, and you won’t actually have to pay for pure silk or satin.

What makes bamboo sheets so popular is that they’re also just as good for hot sleepers: They have moisture-wicking properties, are hypoallergenic by nature, and feel cool to the touch (no disrespect to linen lovers). The lightweight weave also has a lovely drape to it. For a dramatic look, especially in a dark and moody color palette like this Upper East Side family home, these sumptuous sheets can’t be beat.

I’ve put on Sleep Week for the last couple of years, where AD editors share the essentials for styling a bedroom. Needless to say, I’ve tried a lot of sheets from our roster of beloved bedding brands to match my ever-changing space. I arguably have too many sets to count, some new and some old, to give my space personality—and my colleagues can say the same. After all this research, I can confidently share this concise list of our all-time favorites in the bamboo category.

For consistency, all of the prices reflect queen sizes. However, many brands reach up to a king size.

Cozy Earth is a personal favorite of mine for bamboo sheets—the brand’s bread and butter. They’re so breathable—even when I layer with a thick down comforter on top and throw blanket at the foot of the bed when it’s cold outside or when my AC is blasting—and have deep pockets to fit my notoriously tall mattress and topper combo. My taste just so happens to overlap with Oprah’s, if you’ve peeked at her Favorite Things list recently, which feels like a good omen, too.

I’m not the only fan, this set has earned the top overall pick in this story because pretty much every Shop AD writer, editor, and contributor who’s tried the Cozy Earth bamboo sheet set stands behind it for a few reasons: It is temperature-regulating, long-lasting (even after several cycles in the washing machine), and some of the softest bedding out there. Contributor Yelena Moroz Alpert calls her relationship to Cozy Earth a “borderline obsession” because of the natural fibers and neutral colorways that give the bedding an “earthy sophistication.” Shopping editor Audrey Lee calls the sheets “super light and airy, so it’s mostly a summer staple.”

Contributor Megan Wahn adds that the set is a favorite of hers because it feels much more expensive than it actually is: “I honestly thought these sheets were silk when I first slept on them, that’s how smooth and buttery soft they feel.” I love the walnut shade since my bedroom leans more moody, but if you’re into a clean, neutral shade like 2026’s color of the year, Cloud Dancer, I recommend the white or gray color for a calm, minimal bedroom. Their shore set is another style for anyone who likes a more coastal look. But Alpert says it best: “Not sure how Cozy Earth does it but the sheets feel like velvet without being heavy.”

Quince is the luxury-on-a-budget brand I’ve become pretty familiar with over the last two years of trying their duvets, sheet sets, and even their bath towels—and the bamboo sheets don’t fall short. Breathable bedding is a must for my sleep setup, and I’ve been a little surprised by how well I sleep through the night with this set. It feels smooth and cool to the touch, but still cozy enough to bundle up with during the winter months. One of my best friends is a hot sleeper, so whenever he visits, I use these (and the matching bamboo duvet cover) to make his bed. He says these truly keep him at a comfortable temperature throughout the night, so he’s never throwing the covers off mid-REM sleep.

This is the brand I’d recommend if you want to style your bed to a tee. I have the bamboo sheets in a warm terracotta hue to give my bedroom a serene, almost desert Airbnb-like aesthetic in the summertime. There are almost a dozen neutral and vibrant earth tones to match your bedroom’s design, though. These points, on top of the sub-$150 price tag, earned them a top spot in this list. However, if you’re like me and wear oily sheet masks and use thick body butters at night, you might notice some light staining on the sheets.

Because of their glossy sheen, these Luxome sheets probably look the closest to silk out of all of the picks in this list. I have the bedding in a truffle color, but what makes this option so unique are the jewel tones available, like sapphire, that are hard to find with other brands. The generous size of these sheets (they have a 17-inch pocket depth) gives them an even more elegant drape for a glam bedroom.

The neutral tones are compelling, too. For Sleep Week this March, the Shop AD team and I explored how to design a bedroom with a narrative, so we designed rooms inspired by classic novels. One of those storybook sanctuaries took inspiration from Dune, and I think Luxome’s sheets (which happen to have the same name) have a cool, chrome-like finish for fans of futuristic Sci-Fi novels like this one. All in all, these are also soft and breathable, a welcome relief for me when I’m combating hot NYC days.

Yes, we like heritage brands that have been acclaimed for ages at AD, but we’re always on the hunt for new and budget-friendly brands that are worthwhile, too. Sijo launched an Amazon-exclusive line a few years ago that offers a luxe feel and sustainable material sourcing at a lower price point. This basic Doz by Sijo set of bamboo sheets is another staple for my sleep setup. My skin has gotten pretty sensitive over time, and I love how soothed I feel when I sink into these sheets. They have a little more of a polished texture than other picks in this list, so I glide into bed. It’s hard to tell the difference between these and other high-end picks on the market by virtue of their material; it feels like a softer, less slippery satin. The pockets aren’t as deep as Cozy Earth’s, and the colors aren’t as striking as Quince’s burgundy and terracotta options, but for the price, I say it’s still a nice and simple set to shop.

Even though contributor Blake Bakkila prefers more textured bedding like linen, she says Cariloha’s “Aloha” bedding has been a saving grace to keep her cool. “These were the perfect sheets to layer on our bed during a heat wave that rocked Southern California, and they only got softer with each wash,” she shares. After Bakkila and her husband adjusted to the supple texture, she says the “temperature-regulating bamboo” sheets have been a great choice to keep in rotation since they’re “exceptionally smooth and lightweight” with a “cooling effect.” Keep in mind that these are truly thin sheets and almost have a transparent appearance. So if you’re looking for bedding that’s a bit weightier, the Quince or Cozy Earth in this list might be a better way to go.

These sheets are made of 100% bamboo infused with what Bearaby calls “smart botanicals,” including extracts like peppermint and marine proteins. They don’t feel sticky like some silky sheets can when it’s hot outside—they’re airy and refreshing to sleep on and the minty essence only enhances the feeling of freshness. These botanicals do more than smell good—they eliminate up to 99.9% of bacteria directly within the fabric. As your heat rises, the botanicals activate to cool you while keeping the material clean. And—to answer the million-dollar question—the sheets do live up to their “second skin” name—the fabric is so soft and fluid, it feels like it's melting on my body.

Here at Shop AD, we don’t test sheets in a lab—we test them in our very own homes. Why? Because that’s where you’ll be using them too. Each of the sets in our Best Of roundups has been washed, dried, and slept on for countless nights by one of our editors to ensure that they’ve put them through the ringer. The sets are selected to reflect a variety of price points, materials, and styles, and we pride ourselves in giving each set firsthand, real-world use.

Whether you’re a bamboo bedding faithful or it’s your first time exploring the category, there are a few factors I recommend keeping in mind below.

When it comes to the world of linens, bamboo fibers are made from the bamboo plant, which requires far less water than cotton and regrows quickly, so bamboo sheets are often considered one of the more eco-friendly options available. There are few popular bamboo materials: bamboo viscose, rayon, and lyocell. All are antimicrobial, soft to the touch, and absorbent. Depending on if and what they are blended with, they can take on more linen- or cotton-like properties. Here’s a breakdown of each:

The fabric’s weave will change the texture, softness, and general propensity for wrinkling. Below, here are more details on the two most popular weaves: twill, sateen, and percale.

To keep laundry day simple, the bedding in our list simply requires a machine wash and tumble dry. We generally recommend washing on cold and drying on low, as these gentler settings can help preserve the bedding’s quality. However, be sure to check the labels on the sheets for any specific care instructions.

While all bamboo sheets do a superb job of temperature regulating, your best bet (for overall comfort plus silkiness!) is to find a 100% bamboo viscose or lyocell set—like the Cozy Earth pick—with a thread count between 250 and 350, made with a sateen or percale weave. This typically indicates a durable weave that won’t wear easily over time.

As enthusiasts for all things interiors, we recommend looking for bamboo sheet sets that come in colors, prints, and patterns that can match your bedroom’s design. So, if you lean more minimalist, try neutrals like ivory. If you love maximalist style, go big with bold designs and hues—from embroidered details to splashy colors like terracotta or butter yellow.

You can usually find a set of this bedding type between the $50 to $400 range. To get the most out of your purchase, we recommend looking for eco-friendly certifications to help make sure your sheet set has the best quality. For example, the Oeko-Tex Standard 100 applies to bedding that is free of harmful chemicals.

In essence, yes—but how much so really depends on the quality of your set. Bamboo bedding is known for being cool to the touch overall, but the weave itself will ultimately determine the breathability of the sheets. Keep an eye out for bamboo sheets with a 300 thread count weave or above, as our editors mention that these typically have the most airflow for a good night’s rest.

Is Bamboo Viscose Better Than Egyptian Cotton?

Bamboo sheets, because of the strength of their fibers, actually have better longevity than cotton sheets, and offer superior moisture-wicking and temperature regulation to cotton sheets. Bamboo, especially bamboo viscose, also brags a smoother, more soothing feel against the skin compared to cotton, which can require more washes to get the the super soft or silky feel you desire.

Yes! The nature of bamboo is hypoallergenic, making bamboo bed sheets perfect for those with sensitive skin, eczema, or anyone prone to allergies. Bamboo’s super-fine fibers inherently resist things like dust mites, mold, and mildew. Even at the molecular level, it’s a moisture-wicking material, helping you stay sweat-free while also creating an environment inhospitable for dust mites (that need moisture). The fibers are naturally smooth, too, making for a soft sleeping experience.

How Do Bamboo Sheets Compare to Linen in Terms of Softness and Durability?

In our experience, both materials are durable when the sheets are made of 100% natural fibers instead of blends, but bamboo sheets are much softer. Unless it’s stonewashed, linen is usually known to feel a bit scratchy at first and then soften with wear and care. Bamboo sheets are softer down to their microscopic makeup, as their very fibers are smoother than linen.

#4815. 10 Subconscious Reasons You Buy Things You Don't Need

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 15, 2026 | Reviewed by Jessica Schrader

Justin Owens offers a brilliant sociological diagnosis that categorizes the propensities of people from different social classes: “Rich people buy time, poor people buy things, and middle class people buy the appearance of being rich, which is why they stay middle class… Performing wealth costs you the real thing while producing only the appearance of it.”

Would you ever admit to performing being wealthier than your bank account affords?

For those of us without infinite income, every purchase costs hours of our precious finite lives. When we shop for something other than essential items, often we’re actually also shopping for belonging, escapism, repairing the past, justifying the present, trying to control the future, rebelling, and for many other reasons not listed on the packaging. The dopamine spike of each purchase is real enough; however, it simply doesn’t address the root causes of our consumption or recognize that each shopping victory has an expiration date that’s probably much sooner than we imagine.

Here are 10 reasons we may buy things we don’t need.

Status coding is the everyday practice of sending signals to our fellow tribe members; its anxious version is status climbing, which is when we project a class above the one we were born into in the hope that the higher-status tribe we admire will recognize us as one of their own.

We reward ourselves for a long and arduous workweek with something that temporarily promises to make the time-sacrifice worthwhile. Then on Monday morning, we’re back on the same hamster-wheel adding value to billionaires’ stock portfolios when Friday night’s Dubai chocolate is long gone.

We want others to think we’re virtuous sustainability warriors and not just self-indulgent hedonists, so we buy the “right” brand or the carbon offset or donate to charity to signal to our friends that we are really decent humans.

Babies are easily distracted by shiny objects, and adults can be just like babies with larger wallets.

We grew up “limited” and couldn’t have everything we wanted when we wanted it, so now we feel it symbolizes success to not have to ration and budget like we did when the only money we had was what we earned from our newspaper routes.

We see a new object and believe it has the magical ability to make us look or feel younger. My body doesn’t naturally produce enough quercetin, NAD, NAC, curcumin, fish oils, or any of the other 10 million supplements that promise to make me look and feel like a teenager again. But do they really work and how much is the placebo effect?

We actually suffer from low self-esteem and are wary that other people might find out that we really didn’t sign up for this thing called adulthood with its thousands of responsibilities and constant crisis management, so we pad our grins with the markers of financial happiness and the agreed-upon symbols of putative success so that others can’t see the inner doubt and misery.

We were thrown into an economic system that we didn’t choose, yet we can’t imagine any other, which is why Burning Man or “all-inclusive” holidays where you don’t need or can’t use money are so psychologically interesting. Go seven days without buying anything if you want some insights into who you really are when you’re not a wallet with legs.

Purchasing things we don’t need provides the illusion of autonomy and control. We want to feel like the masters of our destinies and clicking on Amazon buttons makes us feel like Captain Kirk commanding the Starship Enterprise.

In the 1980s, there was a bumper sticker that read, “Whoever dies with the most toys wins.” Some people are playing capitalism as if it were a me-against-them game.

Every day, we grow closer to dying, and yet we don’t want to believe it. Filling our lives up with possessions that require our attention lessens the dreadful time we can spend pondering the nature of our brief existences, how we are interacting with others, and how we are impacting the earth.

Owens’ piercing insights about consumers should help raise consciousness around what we’re really trying to accomplish when we shop and especially when we impulse-buy. Have we been duped into believing magical characteristics of consuming that don’t really exist? Do we believe that each next purchase represents opening a new and potentially happier chapter of our lives?

Keeping up with the Joneses is both futile and exhausting.

A deep breath while watching a magnificent sunset doesn’t require a financial transaction.

We might want to spend less time trying to fill our lacks with consumption and more time filling them with what our souls really crave: loving relationships.

Fussell, P. (1983). Class: A painfully accurate guide through the American status system. Summit Books.

Ira Israel, LMFT, is a therapist and author.
