#4813. 7 Best Moroccan Rugs, Vetted by AD Editors (2026)

The Moroccan rug is one of the easiest ways to add texture and intrigue to any room. Originally made to protect floors against the harsh cold of the Atlas Mountains, we see these lush, woolly carpets as a staple in the modern design world because of the range of color and style options available. The rug type has become synonymous with traditional undyed cream colors, vibrant jewel tones, and geometric patterns. It’s no wonder they pop up across tons of our home tours—like this artful Brooklyn studio and floral Victorian house—and have become a favorite among artists like Claudio Bravo and celebs like Patrick Dempsey.

Boucherouite, the hand-weaving technique employed to make these rugs, was created by semi-nomadic Amazigh tribes, and the style can be found in abundance in the busy souks of Marrakech. Now, with many direct-to-consumer and vintage Beni Ourain-inspired styles on the market, the perfect Moroccan rug is just one click away. With all the options at your disposal, it may be intimidating to know where to start. Fear not, we’ve rounded up a few top picks below along with advice on how we’d style them.

Give your guests the coziest welcome with this Azilal rug from Revival Rugs. Its long, narrow design makes it ideal for tight entryways. And while the oblong diamond pattern speaks to geometric patterns you’ll see across traditional Moroccan rugs, the pink tassel is a niche, colorful detail that won’t overpower surrounding decor. This is an authentic hand-knotted Moroccan rug, with wool locally sourced from the Atlas Mountains. Because of its small size, you may also consider hanging it up to add a textural element to your wall decor.

Break up the monotony of home office decor by placing an eclectic rug under your desk setup. While the soft grey and subtle design still create a distraction-free workspace, the cheeky oxblood hues add a touch of personality to make you smile between emails and Zoom meetings.

You don’t have to get a custom carpet straight from Marrakesh to have a high-end rug. The Saira rug from Quince is a handwoven option at an affordable price. The geometric motif is heavily inspired by Moroccan design and the medium-pile, hand-spun wool is designed to feel plush under your feet.

Calling all maximalists and color drenchers. The rich, vibrant colors from this rug come from a family of third-generation dyers, and the irregular shapes of the boxes infuse a quirky texture to whatever room they are placed in. Either have this rug as the standout piece in a room that is otherwise neutral, or pair it with other green pieces like this couch from Ellison Studios to engulf yourself in a verdant indoor forest of your own making.

Even though this muted rug may look understated at first glance, it brings visual interest thanks to the grid design along the border. The motif takes inspiration from Mediterranean tile patterns, in case you were wondering. We can see this as a soft, elegant layer underneath your dining table. Water-resistant and machine washable, this rug can survive stains and spills without making you bat an eye.

Why should the adults have all the fun? Bring whimsical design to your child’s bedroom or playroom with this Moroccan area rug from Benisouk. We have a feeling that the high pile will feel comfy for the kids to run around on and durable enough to withstand a little wear and tear from playtime. The dreamy creamsicle hues of this rug can match youthful decor, but the abstract minimalist design is elevated enough to be a piece that grows with them through the years.

If you find yourself longing for sleek, beige interiors—or are in the midst of remodeling your home to mirror an Aman resort—look no further. This handmade sheep’s wool rug is a minimalist dream. The neutral cream brings a layer of calm, while the organic shapes offer a wabi-sabi element that may be necessary in a pristine white room.

#4811. A Brooklyn Townhouse Celebrates a Forgotten Design Pioneer

In the early 1920s, the Pittsburgh-based manufacturer Armstrong Cork Company (now Armstrong World Industries) made the prescient move of tapping a young arts educator named Hazel Dell Brown to act as its in-house decorator. Hers would be an under-recognized yet wildly influential career. Over the course of more than three decades, she designed countless rooms for advertisements, booklets, catalogs, and more that showcased the brand’s products, chief among them linoleum flooring. With their bold colors, fearless mix of patterns, and daring pastiche, her interiors shaped an entire generation of homes in the Rust Belt and beyond.

For two contemporary artists setting out to renovate a Brooklyn town house for their young family, Brown’s little-known work recently served not only as inspiration but also as a litmus test. When interviewing architects to take on the project, the couple first presented examples of vintage Armstrong schemes. “A lot of people would take one look and then show me something different,” reflects the wife, who learned about Brown while working on a project in Pittsburgh. Not so in the case of Tal Schori and Rustam Mehta of GRT Architects. The AD100 duo instantly responded to Brown’s surface-heavy approach and inward-facing embrace of small spaces, or as Schori puts it, “rooms within rooms.” As for their interior designer, Penelope August, a friend of the wife’s since preschool—she implicitly passed with flying colors.

A circa 1980 Gio Ponti chandelier crowns the living room, which is furnished with a vintage Vladimir Kagan sectional sofa and a Jens Risom chair upholstered in a custom fabric based on a Macgregor Harp painting; artwork by Rose Marcus and wallpaper by Antoinette Poisson.

The kitchen features art-glass cabinet fronts by Paul Wissmach Glass Company and a custom terrazzo floor inspired by ancient roman mosaics of unswept debris.

Hembus wallpaper in a custom blue distinguishes the primary bedroom; painting (above bed) by Michael Wang.

The project evolved into a dynamic collaboration as the couple and their team gutted the town house, set in Carroll Gardens and previously renovated in the 1990s. Together they took care to preserve historic details like the original doors and staircase, with its original mahogany newel posts and handrails. Wherever appropriate, they embraced imperfections, filling in the gaps between old floorboards with contrasting tinted epoxy and leaving levels ever so askew. “This house shows signs of age and that’s not a bad thing,” says Schori. Adds Mehta, “As artists, they understood what is and isn’t precious so that the home does not feel overly restored.”

The backyard was updated by Brook Landscape, with a shed and swing set by GRT Architects.

In the son’s room, shaker-style cabinetry reveals a reading nook.

A refurbished tub from The Demolition Depot anchors the primary bath, which is lined in tiles by Balineum (on walls) and Artistic Tile (on floor).

Brown was nothing if not a gleeful problem solver, presenting flexible solutions to the needs of everyday life, often through inventive secondary spaces. (“Your rumpus room can be anything you like” proclaimed one Armstong campaign.) True to that strategy, GRT devised a floor plan full of versatile surprises. Adjoining the dining room is a cave-like lounge for watching television or just relaxing, with a pass-through to the kitchen for the easy arrival of snacks. Opposite the cookspace—more on that showstopper later—is a cozy breakfast area, with a load-bearing skylight that offers glimpses of the roof terrace (and sometimes curious children) above. And on the second level, a secret passage leads from the primary suite into the library, one column of shelving concealing a door. “All these appendages to the main spaces allow multiple things to happen at once,” says Schori.

In the daughter’s room, Shaker-style trim is painted in Farrow & Ball’s Blue Ground.

The kids’ bath mixes terrazzo walls with custom floor tiles by Haand.

Oak millwork incorporates vintage Piero Fornasetti panels in the dining room, where Guillerme et Chambron chairs surround an antique farm table; painting by Jo Nigoghossian, and custom fireplace fabricated by Daniel Armitano.

Surfaces and furnishings tend toward the eclectic and one-of-a-kind, with a mix of wallpapers and fabrics, some deadstock, setting the stage for vintage treasures. In the living room, a Vladimir Kagan sectional sofa found at auction doubles as a jungle gym of sorts. The primary bath is anchored by a salvaged earthenware tub, since glazed a light blue to complement the retro checkerboard tiles and scalloped vanity. And the dining room’s custom millwork incorporates a series of midcentury Fornasetti panels. Doorknobs, lighting, motifs, art by their circle of creative friends—it’s all a mishmash. “We liked things independently of how they worked together,” says the wife. Adds August: “It was so fun to stitch all these elements into an old house—I love a space where you come upon something unexpected.”

A detail of the kitchen’s terrazzo floor shows stone and metal shapes (some abstract, some legible) inspired by detritus.

A custom sofa frames the lounge off the dining room, which has a pass-through to the kitchen; carpet by Stark.

In the living room, a Gretta Johnson painting hangs above the fireplace, which is flanked by artworks by Cletus Johnson (left) and Ted Mineo (near right) and Miles Huston (Far right).

The husband’s office/music room is cocooned in cork; painting (at left, bottom) by MacGregor Harp.

The stairwell’s painted checkerboard floor hints at classic Americana.

Floor to floor, the mood shifts. The son’s and daughter’s rooms each riff on classic Shaker woodwork, with rails and built-in cabinetry that respond to the exposed ceiling joists. Enlivened by a splatter-painted floor and celestial configuration of ceiling lights, the plywood-lined attic operates as a kids’ club, craft room, and family theater in one. (How’s that for a rumpus room, Hazel?) Thanks to some clever excavation and engineering on the part of GRT, the combined garden level and cellar is now a double-height studio for the wife, complete with a gantry crane for hoisting heavy sculptures. Outside, meanwhile, Brook Landscape transformed the backyard into a romantic swath of greenery.

Vintage globe lights from a one-time optometry office add a surreal touch to the powder room.

Loose arrangements of greenery frame the steps to the backyard.

GRT Architects combined the garden level and cellar into a double-height art studio with a register of exposed brick.

A vintage Vilhelm Lauritzen ceiling light punctuates the primary bedroom; painting by Margaux Ogden.

As for that kitchen, here the team took inspiration not only from Brown but from two additional touchstones. Cabinet fronts nod to art glass at the Upper East Side’s late great Glaser’s Bake Shop, the salvaged interiors of which August and the wife chanced upon at The Demolition Depot. The floor, meanwhile, riffs on ancient Roman mosaics of unswept detritus, an example of which can be found at the Vatican Museums. “We wanted a kitchen that wouldn’t look bad if it got messy,” says the wife. Working closely with GRT and August, she and her husband evolved that historic precedent into a contemporary series of shapes, some abstract and some legible (a knife, a carrot, a wine glass). Rendered in scraps of stone or metal, their forms now appear to float in poured terrazzo.

“The way artists think about objects and architecture is as a constant work in progress,” says Mehta. “These are visual people with an incredible mental library of references.” Would Brown approve? Without a doubt. As the designer once wrote: “Whatever you do, use your own ideas. Don’t be afraid of them.”

#4808. Yamaha unveils new NMAX E, bringing electric power to its popular motorbike lineup

Yamaha is expanding its electric two-wheeler lineup with a much more capable machine, unveiling the new NMAX E as an electric version of its popular NMAX sport scooter.

The 2027 Yamaha NMAX E is being positioned as a 125cc-equivalent electric scooter, putting it firmly above the low-speed urban electric scooters that have made up much of Yamaha’s electric lineup thus far.

At its heart is Yamaha’s new compact eAxle drive unit, which sends power to the rear wheel through a belt drive. The electric motor produces 6.7 kW (9 hp) of nominal power and peaks at 7.5 kW (10 hp), along with 33.3 Nm (24.6 lb-ft) of torque.

Yamaha hasn’t published a top speed in the specifications, but the NMAX E’s A1 classification puts it in the same general category as 125cc combustion scooters rather than urban mopeds.

Power comes from a centrally mounted 3.5 kWh lithium-ion battery, which Yamaha says provides up to 104 km (65 miles) of range on the WMTC Class 1 testing cycle. The L-shaped battery is mounted low and centrally in the chassis to help maintain a low center of gravity.

Unlike many electric scooters that rely on removable batteries, the NMAX E uses a fixed pack. Yamaha has compensated for that with an onboard 800W charger, allowing riders to plug the scooter directly into a household outlet or an AC Type 2 public charging station. A full 0-100% charge takes around 5.5 hours.

Riders can choose between three riding modes. Sport (S) mode offers the quickest acceleration, Touring (T) provides smoother power delivery for everyday riding and carrying a passenger, and Eco (E) reduces acceleration and top speed while maximizing range. Regenerative braking is also included.

The rest of the scooter is fairly well equipped, including traction control, ABS, 245mm front and rear disc brakes, 13-inch wheels, a 33mm fork, dual rear shocks, and even a reverse mode to make low-speed maneuvering easier.

Nice, the Nmax has always been the premium scoot we try to get when traveling in southeast asia. Always felt like it would've been even better electrified. No removable battery seems a bit of a miss. That might constrain a big chunk of demand.

A five-inch TFT display includes smartphone connectivity through Yamaha’s Motor On app, providing turn-by-turn navigation as well as battery status, ride information, maintenance reminders, and other connected features. There’s also keyless ignition, USB-C charging, LED lighting, and illuminated underseat storage large enough for a full-face helmet, depending on its size.

Perhaps most importantly, the NMAX E looks like a proper Yamaha scooter rather than an electric experiment. Its dual headlights and distinctive side panels closely follow the styling language of Yamaha’s existing MAX family.

That could prove important as established motorcycle manufacturers increasingly attempt to bring electric models into their existing lineups rather than treating them as separate curiosities.

Pricing and exact market availability haven’t yet been announced, but Yamaha is positioning the NMAX E as a 2027 model for the European market.

#4807. When the Bully Is an Adult

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 15, 2026 | Reviewed by Jessica Schrader

Teachers are often underappreciated and underpaid. In high school, I (EB) had the celebrated teacher and author of Angela’s Ashes (1996), Frank McCourt. And he was as good as they say, if not better. He was inspirational, built my confidence, and taught me to read and write in ways I am forever grateful for. I had less famous, but also fantastic, teachers, who inspired me to learn and grow. At the same time, I have had teachers who filled me with dread. I still remember my kindergarten teacher who, among other things, made me stand, as a 4-year-old, so that the other kids could see the “baby” because I briefly put my thumb in my mouth. Many of us harbor fondness for some of our teachers and anger towards the ones who lacked empathy, even if their behavior did not rise to the level of “abuse,” but rather was adult-sized bullying.

Teachers, and other adults in the lives of children, can treat children in ways that are hurtful. Adult observers are often reluctant to see and act on the warning signs, despite the direct and indirect impact on kids. Some of the warning signs include: insulting comments, even when couched in humor, humiliating remarks or directives, making children cry, publicly calling attention to children’s foibles, and being physically aggressive and controlling, even if it isn’t assaultive. If a behavior would be inappropriate when directed to another adult, it is probably inappropriate when directed toward a child, unless specifically related to normative school-oriented activities. In other words, if an interaction would feel upsetting or humiliating to an adult, it likely would to a child as well. Children may also indicate a problem with a particular adult by telling concerning stories, changing their behavior around the adult, or avoidance of the adult or the activity the adult is involved in.

Witnesses have to assess whether it is serious enough to have a conversation. When I was a young psychologist, I made extra income by doing psychological testing in a public school. There was a very inappropriate teacher, who anyone, even visiting the school only briefly, could hear yelling and making derogatory comments toward her students. The principal approached me and asked whether I thought there were problems with this teacher. I responded, “Don’t you know?” He nodded. It is hard to take action in such situations, even when it is obvious.

As a psychologist, I continue to hear stories about adult bullying that trouble me. Teachers and other adults in children’s lives are special as they play such a significant role in the lives of the children with whom they interact, most often creating positive experiences. They are afforded status with children which makes the few who are bullies, or otherwise inappropriate, have ample opportunity to use their power to do harm.

McCourt, F. (1996). Angela’s Ashes. Flamingo.

#4806. What “Food Is Medicine” Actually Means, According to a Cardiologist

Plus, Chef Marcus Samuelsson shares easy ways to sneak more plant-based foods into your diet.

Despite what fitfluencers and gym bros might have you believe, eating healthy is about a lot more than counting macros and calories. Every day, scientists are learning more about how our eating habits can influence health beyond just our weight, including blood pressure, cholesterol, blood sugar, and risk of developing heart disease.

That's part of the idea behind a buzzy approach to health care known as “food is medicine,” which contends that what we put on our plates can be a meaningful part of preventing and managing disease.

The challenge is understanding what actually helps and turning that knowledge into meals we want to keep eating. To unpack this, Future You host and editorial director of Men’s and Women’s Health Rich Dorment sits down with Marcus Samuelsson, the acclaimed restaurateur and author of the new cookbook, Back to Green, and Louai Razzouk, MD, a cardiologist at NYU Langone Health.

Here’s a look at some of the themes covered in the episode:

Samuelsson says that beso is an Ethiopian drink that combines roasted barley with water or milk of your choice. “You just shake it and this is the best protein shake you can have,” he says. You can find the more specific recipe in Back to Green.

He grew up eating foods like beso as well as shiro, a stew made from chickpea flour, and other Ethiopian “superfoods” that he incorporates into his dishes today.

Dr. Razzouk says it doesn't mean that you can treat a disease with food alone. “It does mean that you may be able to prevent it by having a healthier and more balanced diet, and possibly minimize how many medications you need to be on,” he says.

Dr. Razzouk says that while you may still need to be on medication, food can make a “huge” impact when it comes to managing conditions like diabetes or high cholesterol. “Food impacts our level of inflammation, our insulin sensitivity, our weight,” and our blood pressure, he says.

The first warning sign that a patient’s diet may be less than ideal is if they get most of their food from Uber Eats or another delivery service. “Most of the stuff that we order in, especially living in New York City, contains a lot of saturated fats, a lot of added salt, and a lot of processed food,” he says. He discusses a couple more red flags in the episode, including meal timing.

In his early 20s, traveling in Southeast Asia and Singapore opened his eyes to a new way of eating—and the idea that there’s great food to be found all over the world.

A plant-forward diet doesn't have to exclude animal protein, he says, pointing to the Mediterranean and DASH diets as examples of eating plans that incorporate some meat. “What I tell patients is that typically if you have a choice between getting a source of protein from an animal source versus from plants, then I would try to get it from plants,” he says.

“What I tell patients is that I want you to enjoy your life,” Dr. Razzouk says, adding that fad diets typically don’t work. “What we would like you to do is find a balance,” he says, eating in a way that's enjoyable and that benefits your health.

It's a lentil salad with a coconut turmeric vinaigrette—and Samuelsson breaks down why the flavors work so well together, plus ingredients you may have in the fridge or pantry that can level it up even further.

He’ll often bring home new ingredients (e.g. spirulina) and encourage his son to look up the health benefits before they cook with it together.

Dr. Razzouk says diets high in fiber not only may lower your risk of colon cancer, but they also may lower your level of LDL cholesterol and triglycerides, which impact your risk of cardiovascular disease. It also helps keep you full.

Cooking together can be an enjoyable family activity, Samuelsson says. “When I cook waffles and pancakes with my kids, we have fun,” he says. “The kitchen is a mess, but iPads are down, we're spending time together.”

Samuelsson suggests chickpeas, sea moss, and the fruit soursop. Dr. Razzouk suggests nuts (walnuts in particular) and extra-virgin olive oil.

Caitlin is a health and fitness journalist based in New York City. She writes for publications including The Wall Street Journal and Runner’s World. She's completed 12 marathons, including the six World Marathon Majors, is semi-fluent in French, and volunteers as a greeter on The High Line. Follow her on Instagram or LinkedIn.

#4805. Maximo milestone: friendly robot has completed 300,000 tasks

After completing the first half of Amazon’s Bellefield complex last summer, Maximo engineers have put their helpful robot friend into high gear, with the latest version 3.0 robot-equipped construction crews installing as many as 24 solar panel modules per hour, per person. And now, the company is reporting that Maximo has completed its 300,000th construction task. (!)

If that 24 modules per hour, per person rate sounds fast, that’s because it is. At full tilt, the latest Maximo robot-equipped crews have nearly doubled the output of traditional installation methods at similar solar locations throughout Southern California. Now, with 300,000 tasks under its belt, the robot is proving to be both fast and durable.

“The future of solar construction depends on bringing together the experience of our people and new technology,” explains Matt Tetrault, President of Primoris’ Renewables business.

Like just about everyone these days, Maximo is playing up its machines’ NVDIVIA hardware, re-branding its robots’ repetitive automation as “physical AI.” That’s not to say the robots don’t work, however – as their deployment at Amazon’s Bellefield solar field is proving.

(By) leveraging NVIDIA AI infrastructure together with NVIDIA Omniverse libraries and NVIDIA Isaac Sim open robotics simulation framework, the Maximo team was able to develop, test and refine robotic capabilities through physics-based simulation and AI driven modeling before deploying updates in the field. The combination of AI, vision, robotics and simulation driven engineering reduced development and validation timelines and increased confidence in field performance as the robotic fleet scaled.

Amazon Web Services (AWS) powered the development, deployment, and operation of Maximo’s AI-driven field systems. AWS provides scalable computing, automated software delivery, and advanced data analytics, including real-time construction intelligence, enabling Maximo to collect operational robotics data and continuously improve performance.

The rapid deployment of solar panels at utility scale projects like this one, which is set to eventually reach over a GW of solar generating capacity, will help companies like Amazon reduce their carbon footprint, of course – but also save them big money as the price of fossil fuels continues to climb.

So Primoris’ Renewables is the name of a business, Maximo is the name of a solar robotics company incubated by The AES Corporation. Maximo makes a robot (also called Maximo I think) using NVDIVIA hardware and software, and also AWS cloud software, and Bellefield is a solar field owned by Amazon. Or so I gather so far.

The Maximo team says it has plans to extend its robots potential use cases beyond solar and into construction tasks that require similar pick and place and feeding and attaching movements, site logistics, and site cleanup. And like just about every other business that demands a high degree of physical labor, the construction industry is facing huge labor shortages, making machines like Maximo that provide real efficiency gains welcome additions to the job site.

#4802. Former CAKE team is back in the kitchen with a new electric motorcycle

If the new ReBake Abler electric motorcycle looks like it might share a bit of DNA with Sweden’s dearly departed CAKE, there’s a good reason for that.

ReBake Motors has officially unveiled its first electric two-wheeler at EICMA 2026 in Milan, introducing the Abler as a highly modular, utility-focused electric moped and light motorcycle.

Behind the new Swedish company is founder and CEO Karl Ytterborn, who spent eight years at CAKE from its founding in 2016 through 2024. CAKE famously built some of the most interesting minimalist electric motorcycles and utility bikes on the market before ultimately collapsing after failing to secure additional funding. Ytterborn says ReBake is intended to apply many of the lessons learned during that experience, while eliminating unnecessary cost and complexity.

Its chunky 42mm aluminum tube frame isn’t just a styling exercise. ReBake designed it as a mounting system, allowing cargo accessories to attach directly to the frame at multiple locations. That means riders can configure the bike for everything from carrying groceries and passengers to more serious utility duties.

There are two versions. The Abler45 is an EU L1e-B moped with a 45 km/h (28 mph) top speed, 4 kW nominal motor and 5.12 kW peak output. It starts at €3,990 including VAT.

The €4,990 Abler80 steps up to the A1 light motorcycle category, increasing nominal power to 6.14 kW and peak power to 7.68 kW while pushing the top speed beyond 80 km/h (50 mph). Both versions use a direct-drive rear hub motor.

Energy comes from removable 2 kWh lithium iron phosphate (LFP) batteries weighing 17 kg (37.5 lb) apiece. The Abler45 comes standard with one battery but can carry two, while the Abler80 includes two for 4 kWh of total capacity. With both batteries, ReBake claims up to 160 km (99 miles) of low-speed urban range on the Abler45 or 114 km (71 miles) on the Abler80. Each battery can be charged either on or off the bike from a normal 230V outlet, reaching a full charge in four hours.

The rest of the hardware includes 14-inch cast wheels, 140mm of front suspension travel, dual rear shocks, front and rear disc brakes with combined braking, regenerative braking, and full LED lighting.

Perhaps more interesting than any individual specification, though, is simply seeing another Swedish electric motorcycle startup emerge from CAKE’s ashes.

CAKE demonstrated that there was plenty of appetite for unconventional, lightweight electric motorcycles. Unfortunately, it also demonstrated just how difficult it is to turn that enthusiasm into a sustainable business.

ReBake appears to be getting another crack at that equation – this time with eight years’ worth of lessons already baked in.

#4801. Tesla wins trade zone status for Texas Megapacks, but China tariffs stay

Tesla’s Megapack factory in Brookshire, Texas, is now officially a foreign-trade zone, a US customs program that lets manufacturers defer duties on imported parts. The FTZ Board’s approval, published in the Federal Register on Monday, runs for 14 months.

It’s easy to read this as Tesla getting a pass on tariffs for the Chinese LFP cells inside its Megapacks. Tesla’s own application says otherwise: parts hit by Section 301, 232, or 122 tariffs still pay them.

Tesla filed the request on May 20 for Subzone 84AM, part of FTZ 84 in the Houston area. That’s the factory where Tesla started Megapack 3 production in August, with a planned capacity of 50 GWh per year.

The application lists roughly 80 imported components Tesla wants to bring in under zone procedures, from coolant pumps and transformers to busbars, wiring harnesses, circuit boards and battery cells. Duty rates on those parts go up to 6.5%, while finished Megapacks and Bento inverters range from duty-free to 3.4%.

That gap is the usual reason companies set up an FTZ. A manufacturer in a zone can normally elect to pay the finished product’s lower rate instead of the rate on each imported part.

But the application spells out the limit. Components subject to duties under Section 122 of the Trade Act, Section 232 of the Trade Expansion Act, or Section 301 “require subject merchandise to be admitted to FTZs in privileged foreign status,” according to the notice.

Privileged foreign status locks in a part’s own classification and tariff treatment when it enters the zone. So a Chinese LFP cell that ends up in a Megapack sold in the US still pays the Section 301 tariff on Chinese non-EV lithium-ion batteries, which jumped from 7.5% to 25% on January 1, 2026. Tesla just pays it later, when the finished Megapack leaves the zone.

Here’s what Tesla actually gets out of it:

The approval came with the same wording the FTZ Board has used all year. Production was “authorized on a limited basis,” and “further subject to a fourteen-month time period.”

Tesla got an identical decision for Megapack and inverter production at Fremont, Livermore and Lathrop in California this summer, with a one-year limit instead. Eastman Chemical, battery pack maker Rose Batteries, and chipmaker Qorvo all received similar time-limited, “limited basis” approvals in 2026.

The 14-month clock puts the expiry around the end of 2027. That’s roughly when LG Energy Solution is due to start supplying Tesla with LFP cells from Michigan under a $4.3 billion contract for Megapack 3, with mass production expected in the second half of 2027.

Tesla hasn’t hidden the issue. When tariffs escalated in April 2025, CFO Vaibhav Taneja said the impact on energy storage would be “outsized since we source lithium iron phosphate (LFP) battery cells from China.” We wrote at the time that Tesla’s only growing business was kneecapped by Trump’s tariffs.

Tesla has since brought up its own LFP cell factory in Nevada, but Taneja said it would only cover a fraction of Tesla’s installed capacity. And Brookshire alone is designed for 50 GWh a year, on top of the 40 GWh Lathrop factory in California.

Tesla’s Megapacks are bringing big batteries to the grid, but you can get the same benefits at home by pairing solar with storage. With electricity rates up almost 10% last year and expected to keep climbing, going solar is one of the best ways to protect yourself against rising costs. #4799. The 40 Best Sci-Fi and Fantasy Books of 2026 So Far

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

Featuring stories about app-based love, A.I., and way more. Sound familiar?

AS WE START to inch closer and closer to the end of the year, we know you’re going to be looking to feel cozy as the weather starts to get colder. Luckily, there are plenty of exciting sci-fi and fantasy books to curl up with a big mug of something hot with and read next to a fire.

There's new releases from mainstays like Jim Butcher and Matt Dinniman (two in fact, plus an upcoming TV show). There's all manner of space operas, cyberpunk thrillers, quests to kill gods, and even a wolf who sets out to kill the human race. And toward the end of the year, Emily St. John Mandel of Station Eleven fame returns with a new hopeful futuristic tale. As summer winds down, new books will continue to offer a much-needed escape throughout 2026.

Admittedly, there's been a lot of media begging for our attention this year, like The Odyssey, the World Cup, and soon Avengers: Doomsday and Dune: Part Three. But once the adrenaline of those action-packed watches wear off, it'll be the perfect time to crack open a new read and lock into assassins dealing with an alien invasion or a botched quest to clone the founding fathers (seriously).

Below is our list of the best reads of the year no matter what kind of sci-fi or fantasy you're interested in. Keep checking back as the year continues to progress for more updates.

A group of civilians are chosen to accompany veteran astronauts on a trip to Saturn's moon, Titan. They struggle to orbit the planet, and after some struggle, they manage to get back on course and return to Earth. But the Earth they left doesn't seem to be the planet they remember.

The 18th (yes, 18th!) book in Butcher's long-running Dresden Files series finds its titular character, Harry Dresden, struggling with grief. But Chicago is struggling, his brother is seriously ill, and he's now betrothed to a vampire. He'll have to put his own feelings aside to be the wizard everyone needs him to be.

Think dating is hard right now? Maybe you'll find solace in For Human Use, where a new app helps people find love with cadavers. You read that right. And as the app gains popularity, two men behind its funding and growth are pitted against one another.

Best known for his Dungeon Crawler Carl series, Matt Dinniman is back with a standalone novel. Oliver lives on the planet New Sonora, but when Earth decides to evict the planet's tenants, they make a deadly game of it, allowing Earth's gamers to use war machines for fun. Oliver decides to try and save his home no matter what.

Leena can see the dead, but keeps her secret closely guarded. When her brother falls ill, she decides to bargain away her secret to a dangerous merchant. Her trade leads her to a mysterious estate, where she has to fight for her life to escape the merchant's grasp.

The first in a new trilogy, Tao Wong's The First Step follows a young farmer who joins a school of cultivation, and begins a magical journey filled with martial arts, Chinese mythology, and a chance at immortality.

It's post-WWI Britain, and what people hope to be a time of peace is overtaken by a new threat. Fae are taking children, and one vet decides he'll be the one to bring them back.

If you don't normally read short story collections, now's your chance. Featuring award-winning and nominated stories, this book may be the perfect read to get you out of a reading slump with tales you can finish in one sitting.

A grief support group gets dark with the newest release from horror writer Eric LaRocca. After the death of his husband, Simeon becomes enmeshed with The Wretches, whose intense methods to process the death of loved ones leads Simeon to a mysterious man who promises they can all get one last moment with a loved one for a steep price.

The graduate of a deadly academy is the protagonist of Mark Lawrence's newest novel. Rue, now an old woman, is finally at peace with her violent upbringing. But as soon as she thinks she's entered retirement, the sins of her past come back to haunt her.

Trees can drag you to your death. Artificial monsters stalk the lands. And alien men wander around town. John Maraintha is dropped on the planet Scythia and has to find a way to adapt and survive.

Fans of classic folktale retellings will enjoy this take on The Little Mermaid. Rather than following Ariel, it centers on her granddaughter, who is awash in complex politics after the assassination of her father. To save her family's legacy, she makes a deal with the famed sea-witch Ursla.

If you're a fan of The Substance, you may enjoy this new book. A young struggling writer named Maggie meets an established producer who's looking to revive her career. The solution to both of their problems is an experimental procedure–that takes Maggie's youth and gives it to the older producer–along with a $3 million payout that helps with Maggie's money troubles. But both women quickly realize that the blood transfusion is anything but a simple transaction.

Space operas are a sci-fi subgenre that never get old. When a starship's crew returns to their home galaxy after a decade away, they find the once peaceful space they knew is now on the brink of war–and they're expected to fight in it. It's up to the ship's captain to make life or death decisions that may have irrevocable impacts on her crew.

A ten-year-old girl goes on a journey with her AI toy to reach New York in this novel all about the complex relationship between man and machine.

This debut follows a billionaire who has prepared for the apocalypse and is ready to survive in his bunker with his family. But above ground, raiders are also fighting to stay alive, and aren't too happy with their new neighbors.

Earth is in ruins. A colony ship on the way to its new home keeps its original civilians in cryostasis, while their descendants maintain the vessel on its four hundred yearlong journey to a new planet. But midway through the trip, the crew of descendants starts to wonder why they're spending their lives in service of a world they'll never see.

A beloved actor in an epic fantasy franchise where much of the characters believe the world is real makes a bold choice to break out of the false reality the studio has built, and convince his co-stars to go with him.

The eighth, highly anticipated book in the Dungeon Crawler Carl series picks up on the tenth floor, where Carl and Donut try to make sense of the system AI's mysterious plans and learn more about why it calls the eleventh floor 'A Parade of Horribles'.

If you're yearning for an epic tale of political intrigue, this new Ann Leckie read is for you. The empire of Radch is dealing with internal turmoil, and when a major decision is made on the religious site called Radiant Star, that turmoil could result in irrevocable changes to everyone in the city of Ooioiaa.

#4798. Are Psychological Evaluations Needed for AI Developers?

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 15, 2026 | Reviewed by Jessica Schrader

On March 24, 2015, a Germanwings pilot with severe mental issues purposefully crashed a passenger plane with 150 people on board in the French Alps, resulting in the tragic deaths of all passengers. This horrible incident highlighted how important it is that people who work in high-risk fields and have a responsibility for the survival of others are mentally stable.

To ensure this, psychological evaluations are occupationally mandated in some jobs (American Psychological Association, 2017). Jobs in which psychological evaluations are common before starting a job in many states of the U.S. include:

Moreover, in some jobs, psychological evaluations may be conducted with people who are already employed if they show signs of impaired mental health or thinking abilities. This includes, for example, physicians and other healthcare workers.

Should people in certain high-risk AI development roles undergo psychological evaluations?

One group of people that does not routinely undergo mental health screenings is programmers involved in the creation of AI. Over the course of the last few weeks, there has been a lot of discussion in the media about the extent to which rogue AI agents may potentially cause harm to humans in the future or even kill all of humanity. While experts debate about whether AI agents turning on humanity is a real risk or not, one potentially more likely risk of AI often gets ignored.

Just like a pilot could cause a plane to crash on purpose, an AI developer could purposefully build AI models that lack the necessary checks and balances to prevent them from hurting humans, or even an AI that exists with the purpose to harm humans, e.g., by performing hacking attacks on critical infrastructure. Thus, AI does not need to turn rogue by itself to harm humans; one developer doing so on purpose could lead to massive economic and personal harm. Since a scenario like this seems more likely to occur in the near future than AI turning rogue by itself, measures to prevent such an incident are needed. Because there are mental health conditions like antisocial personality disorder, which has been linked to violating the rights of other people without feelings of guilt, or dark personality traits such as psychopathy, such a scenario is not entirely unlikely.

I believe occupationally mandated psychological evaluations of AI developers should be considered.

Given that the big AI companies themselves warn about the potential risks of AI, more psychological research is needed into whether severe, untreated mental health conditions in AI developers could lead to the risk of real harm for other human beings. While no one knows how likely catastrophic events akin to the Germanwings airplane catastrophe would be in the field of AI development and what impact they would have, occupationally mandated psychological evaluations of AI developers could help ensure that only mentally stable people are involved in the development of frontier models with the potential to cause real harm to humans. Thus, professional organizations for psychologists, companies that make hiring and employment decisions in relation to the development of AI tools, and government agencies involved in the regulation of tech companies should consider occupationally mandated psychological evaluations of AI developers.

American Psychological Association. (2017, February 24). Professional practice guidelines for occupationally mandated psychological evaluations. https://www.apa.org/practice/guidelines/psychological-evaluations

#4797. Kia racks up another record month as its new $30K electric SUV hits dealers

Kia is eyeing its fourth consecutive annual US sales record after another record-breaking October. With the new 2027 EV3 arriving, Kia is “confident” the momentum will carry into Q4 and well into 2027.

“With six consecutive months of record-breaking sales, Kia is accelerating toward a fourth straight annual sales record,” Kia America vice president of sales Eric Watson said on Tuesday.

Kia sold 80,008 vehicles in the US last month, up 16% year over year and marking its best October sales month ever.

Kia said electrified and hybrid vehicles drove the growth, with sales up 112% and 114% YOY, both setting new sales records.

Several Kia models posted strong YOY sales growth, including the three-row electric EV9 (+111%), Telluride (+63%), EV6 (+58%), Seltos (+43%), Sorento (+31%), Carnival (+17%), K4 (+14%), and Sportage (+12%).

Regarding its fully electric vehicles. Sales of the EV9, Kia’s three-row electric SUV, are now up 5% year-to-date, with 13,835 units sold through October.

Although EV6 sales rebounded slightly last month, they are still down 39% YTD, with 7,067 units sold through October.

Kia’s new entry-level electric SUV, the EV3 (check out our review), is now in showrooms nationwide. After it went on sale in mid-August, Kia sold 1,496 EV3s in its first two months.

The 2027 EV3 is Kia’s most affordable fully electric vehicle, starting at $29,890. That’s for the base EV3 Light model, which uses a 58.3 kWh battery that provides up to 221 miles of range.

Higher-priced trims, including the Wind, Land, GT-Line, and GT, use an 81.4 kWh battery for up to 321 miles of range.

Front-wheel-drive (FWD) models have a single 201-horsepower electric motor. Upgrading to all-wheel-drive (AWD) costs $3,200 and adds a rear electric motor for a combined 261 horsepower.

All variants have a native NACS port so that drivers can use Tesla Superchargers without a separate adapter. Charging the 58.3 kWh battery from 10% to 80% takes around 29 minutes, while the larger 81.4 kWh battery takes about 31 minutes.

Inside, all EV3 models feature Kia’s latest ccNC (connected car Navigation Cockpit) with standard wireless Apple CarPlay and Android Auto.

The setup provides nearly 30″ of combined digital screens, including a dual 12.3″ instrument cluster and touchscreen infotainment, plus a 5″ climate control display.

Kia describes the EV3 as an “entry-level EV SUV that offers many of the advanced amenities of the EV9,” but in a smaller, more affordable package.

The 2027 Kia EV9 starts at $54,990. That’s also for the base EV9 Light short-range trim, which uses a 76.1 kWh battery with up to 230 miles of range.

Higher-priced trims, including the Light Long Range, Wind, Land, and GT-Line, use a larger 99.8 kWh battery that delivers up to 305 miles of range in RWD variants.

I'm leasing, so I'm getting my 2nd KIA EV next year (EV3 GT-Line) -- I can't even find a Subaru Uncharted GT anywhere within 100 miles of my house and I am not interested in Toyota's C-HR because even their top trim has less features than the Subaru's version. Plus the KIA interior is so much better!!!!

Kia’s three-row electric SUV offers up to 42.8″ of legroom in the second row, which is even more than the 2025 Cadillac Escalade. Buyers can choose from six- and seven-seat configurations.

“An expanding hybrid lineup, the arrival of the all-new, all-electric EV3, and the success of the all-new Telluride and Seltos are adding even more momentum to our growth,” Watson said. He added, “We’re confident this record-setting pace will carry us through the fourth quarter and well into 2027.”

#4794. This electric microcar just got a 50 mph upgrade, plus a secret Turbo mode

The adorable little Eli ZERO electric microcar is growing up. At this year’s EICMA motorcycle show in Milan, Eli Electric Vehicles is unveiling a faster, more powerful version of its pint-sized electric car, along with a sporty new limited-edition model that even includes a hidden Turbo mode.

First teased last week, the new Eli ZERO MX is the company’s first L7e-class electric vehicle, allowing it to reach speeds of up to 80 km/h (50 mph). That’s a significant jump from the 45 km/h (28 mph) top speed of the original L6e version that we originally reviewed, opening the door to a much wider range of driving scenarios.

And perhaps most importantly, the company managed to squeeze that extra performance into the same tiny package.

The Eli ZERO MX retains the original’s ultra-compact 2.25-meter (7.4-foot) length, making it considerably smaller than even the smallest conventional passenger cars.

But under that tiny exterior, the MX now packs up to 20 kW (27 hp) of peak power, compared to the 12 kW (16 hp) of the original Eli ZERO.

The upgraded model also features a 14.4 kWh lithium iron phosphate (LFP) battery, providing a claimed range of up to 200 km (124 miles) per charge.

That’s a pretty respectable figure for a vehicle designed primarily for short urban and suburban trips.

The rear-wheel-drive microcar features selectable ECO and SPORT driving modes, a 30% climbing capability, and a tight 7.2-meter turning circle. Charging is handled by a 3.3 kW onboard charger, allowing owners to plug in at home or use public AC charging stations.

Despite its diminutive size, the Eli ZERO MX also includes a surprisingly long list of features normally associated with much larger cars.

Those include air conditioning and heating, a panoramic moonroof, soft-close doors, keyless entry and start, electric power steering, ABS, front and rear disc brakes, and an electronic parking brake.

There’s also an 8.8-inch digital instrument cluster, a reversing camera with rear radar, and an optional 10.1-inch infotainment display with Apple CarPlay.

The MX is available in four colors: Pearl White, Arctic Silver, Graphite, and Champagne.

The biggest difference, though, is the vehicle’s classification. While the original Eli ZERO falls under Europe’s L6e light quadricycle category, the new MX qualifies as an L7e heavy quadricycle. That means it generally requires a higher-level driving license, but also allows for considerably more performance.

For drivers who love the idea of a tiny electric runabout but need something capable of keeping up with faster suburban traffic, the MX could be a much more compelling option.

Eli isn’t stopping at a faster version of its microcar.

The company is also introducing the Eli ZERO Race Edition, a limited-production model available in both the original L6e and the new L7e configurations.

Finished in an exclusive Turbo Red paint job, the Race Edition features racing stripes across the hood, roof, and body panels, along with a rear spoiler and special wheel badging.

Inside, the sporty treatment continues with a two-tone Race Red microsuede and light gray interior, including special upholstery, dashboard accents, and embroidered seats.

But the most interesting feature isn’t immediately visible.

Eli has included a secret Turbo mode activated by pressing and holding the hazard light button. After four quick chimes, the digital instrument cluster switches to Turbo mode and the car’s acceleration response becomes sharper.

Importantly, the feature doesn’t increase the vehicle’s top speed or exceed its regulatory performance limits. Instead, it changes the way the car responds to accelerator inputs, giving it a sportier driving feel.

It’s a fun little Easter egg in a vehicle that already has plenty of personality.

Eli is also offering a new Carbon Aero Kit, consisting of a carbon fiber front splitter, side skirts, and rear wing. The kit is compatible with both the Race Edition and other Eli ZERO models.

Eli has spent years developing its vision of a smaller, lighter, and more efficient alternative to conventional cars, particularly for the short trips that make up much of our daily driving.

The company has already launched its second-generation Eli ZERO, which shares the same 96V electrical architecture and aluminum chassis as the new MX.

By expanding into the L7e category, Eli is addressing one of the biggest limitations of its original design: speed.

A 28 mph (45 km/h) microcar makes plenty of sense in dense urban centers, but its usefulness quickly drops off when drivers need to venture onto faster roads. A 50 mph (80 km/h) version, on the other hand, could make the same tiny vehicle practical for considerably more people.

In the US, this could go pretty much anywhere that didn't require highway driving. Perfect one-and-a-halfth car.

The Eli ZERO family is currently available through authorized dealers in several European markets, including Italy, Norway, and the Netherlands, with prices starting at €10,500 (approximately US $12,000) for the base model. Pricing for the new MX and Race Edition hasn’t yet been announced.

Eli also has a pilot program underway in the United States, though the new models are being introduced primarily for the European market.

Both the Eli ZERO MX and Race Edition will be on display at EICMA 2026 in Milan from November 3–8.

For a vehicle that takes up less than half the space of a conventional car, the Eli ZERO is certainly finding ways to make a bigger impression!

#4791. Zero Motorcycles unveils $8K electric motorcycle, street-legal dirt bike, and much more

Zero Motorcycles is celebrating its 20th anniversary with a major wave of announcements at EICMA 2026, unveiling several new electric two-wheelers alongside some important technology upgrades that could make its existing motorcycles even more appealing.

The biggest news is an entirely new lightweight electric street motorcycle known as Project Ventura, designed to bridge the gap between the increasingly popular world of electric bikes plus lightweight e-motos and the full-size electric motorcycle market. But that’s far from the only news coming out of Zero’s booth in Milan.

The electric motorcycle maker is also announcing a new street-legal version of its XE electric dirt bike that was previously only street-legal in Europe, introducing its LS3 electric scooter to Europe, introducing a redesigned smartphone app, and sharing more details about its upcoming DC fast-charging system.

There’s quite a bit to unpack here, so let’s get into it.

Perhaps the most exciting announcement is Project Ventura, a new lightweight electric motorcycle that Zero hopes will attract an entirely new generation of riders.

With supermoto-inspired styling, 17-inch wheels, and a more approachable design than the company’s larger street motorcycles, Ventura appears aimed squarely at riders who want something more substantial than a typical lightweight e-moto but aren’t necessarily looking for a full-size, high-performance motorcycle.

And with a targeted price of less than US $8,000, it could be one of Zero’s most accessible street motorcycles yet.

The bike features a new electric powertrain producing over 20 kW (27 hp) of peak power, delivering a sustained top speed of 65 mph (105 km/h) and a momentary maximum speed of 81 mph (130 km/h).

That’s a particularly interesting distinction, as it positions the bike as being able to briefly reach highway-capable speeds with the occasional burst of extra performance, but perhaps not for longer sustained highway stretches.

Importantly, we don’t yet know the battery capacity, though Zero is giving us a hint by publishing a range estimate. The company is quoting an estimated real-world range of 93 miles (150 km), but without a real battery capacity figure to parse, it’s hard to take that range estimate at face value.

We do know that the bike will feature onboard Level 2 charging, allowing riders to plug into public AC charging stations without carrying around a separate external charger – something more common on the smallest of electric motorcycles that attempt to cut down on weight and cost by using an off-board charger.

Other features include standard ABS, Bluetooth connectivity, integration with Zero’s upcoming smartphone app, and a chassis designed to accommodate a passenger.

The riding position is upright, and the bike’s supermoto-inspired geometry is intended to deliver agile handling and a playful riding experience.

Perhaps most interestingly, Zero isn’t hiding the fact that it sees the explosion of e-bikes and smaller electric motos as a potential gateway to motorcycle ownership.

“When we set out to build Project Ventura, we had three guiding principles: first, build a bike so good-looking you’d want its poster above your bed. Second, make it a proper electric motorcycle that feels like a natural next step for the millions of riders who’ve spent the last four or five years on e-bikes and e-motos. And third, make it ridiculously fun to ride,” explained Jim Callahan, Zero’s Chief Product and Partnerships Officer.

That’s a notable strategy for a company that has historically focused heavily on higher-performance electric motorcycles. However, the launch of Zero’s smaller Sur Ron-style electric dirt bikes last summer showed that the motorcycle maker did see the writing on the wall in terms of the success of the e-moto market compared to larger flagship bikes.

Instead of trying to convince existing gasoline motorcycle riders to make the electric switch, Zero is increasingly looking toward younger riders who may have already fallen in love with electric two-wheelers and are ready for something faster and more capable.

But for those interested in the new Ventura, they’ll have to wait just a bit longer. The motorcycle is expected to become available in summer 2027.

Another major announcement is a new street-legal version of Zero’s XE electric dirt bike.

The original XE was introduced as part of Zero’s more affordable X Line of lightweight electric off-road motorcycles, offering a more accessible entry point to the brand than its traditional street and dual-sport models. While the model was available in street-legal format in Europe, the US versions were purely off-road affairs.

Now Zero is taking that same general concept and making it legal for public roads, opening up significantly more riding possibilities.

The new XE is fully homologated and features the necessary street equipment, including a headlight, taillight, and centrally mounted dashboard. A small integrated flyscreen and black bodywork give the motorcycle a more refined appearance, while wide dual-sport tires help it transition between pavement and dirt.

Zero has also revised the gearing, using a rear sprocket designed to balance off-road performance with the higher speeds required for street riding.

The result is a claimed top speed of 60 mph (97 km/h), making the XE capable of considerably more than simply putting around the neighborhood, though with a slight torque sacrifice.

For riders who live near off-road riding areas, the appeal is obvious. Instead of loading a dirt bike into a truck or trailer to reach the trails, owners could potentially ride directly from home to the trailhead, provided local regulations allow it.

That’s a particularly useful capability for a lightweight electric dirt bike, where much of the appeal comes from its simplicity, low maintenance, and ability to explore places that larger motorcycles might struggle to reach.

The new street-legal XE is expected to launch in spring 2027, with pricing starting at US $6,295.

Zero is also expanding its electric scooter offerings with the European debut of the LS3, a faster and more capable scooter designed for urban transportation and longer commutes.

While Zero has traditionally been associated with electric motorcycles, its growing interest in scooters represents another effort to reach riders who prioritize everyday transportation over outright performance.

The LS3 offers a top speed of 75 mph (121 km/h), which puts it well beyond the typical low-speed urban electric scooter category.

Zero also quotes a range of up to 93 miles (150 km), powered by a battery system consisting of three onboard batteries with a combined capacity of 5.6 kWh.

Interestingly, one of those batteries is removable and located beneath the seat. Riders can remove it to free up additional storage space, offering some flexibility between maximum battery capacity and cargo-carrying convenience.

That could prove useful for commuters who don’t always need the scooter’s full range but would appreciate extra room for everyday items.

The LS3 also comes equipped with heated grips and a heated seat, features that should be particularly welcome to European riders commuting through colder months.

Connected technology rounds out the package, including compatibility with Zero’s upcoming smartphone app.

The LS3 is expected to become available in Europe in spring 2027.

Beyond the new vehicles, Zero is also working on a redesigned smartphone application that promises to give riders considerably more control over their motorcycles.

The new app is being developed for the LS3, 2027 Street and Dual Sport motorcycles, and Project Ventura.

One of the more interesting features is the ability to customize riding modes. In addition to preset modes that adjust power delivery and regenerative braking, riders will be able to create a custom mode and individually tune parameters including speed, power, torque, and regeneration.

The app will also include a charging-station finder, allowing riders to search for nearby chargers and filter them by charging speed, network, and availability. Apple Maps integration will provide route guidance to selected charging locations.

An optional ride-history feature can automatically record trips, including their distance, duration, and route previews.

Riders will also be able to customize their motorcycle’s dashboard layout, choosing which information appears in each quadrant of the display.

Battery management is getting some attention, too. The app will provide controls for scheduled charging, target charge levels, charging limits, and long-term battery storage settings.

Zero is also adding integrated feedback and support tools that allow owners to report problems or suggest improvements directly through the app, including photos and diagnostic logs.

The redesigned app is expected to arrive in 2027.

Perhaps the most consequential technology announcement, at least for riders who want to take their electric motorcycles beyond short commutes and weekend rides, is Zero’s continued development of DC fast charging.

Zero says it is targeting a 2027 launch for its new fast-charging system, which will support compatible Street and Dual Sport motorcycles.

The system is being designed to charge compatible batteries from 20% to 80% in less than 30 minutes.

That’s a major improvement in convenience compared to the longer AC charging stops that have traditionally been associated with Zero motorcycles.

Importantly, the company is developing the system for both North America and Europe, with different charging connectors for each market.

European motorcycles will use the CCS standard, while North American models will feature NACS connectors.

The latter is particularly interesting given the growing adoption of NACS charging infrastructure across North America, potentially making it easier for electric motorcycle riders to access a wider range of charging locations.

Zero is also developing a retrofit option for eligible existing motorcycles, meaning some current owners may eventually be able to upgrade their bikes rather than purchasing entirely new models to take advantage of the technology.

The company hasn’t yet announced pricing or a complete list of compatible motorcycles, and the retrofit option remains under development.

Still, the prospect of adding significantly faster charging to existing electric motorcycles could be an important development for the brand.

There are several exciting announcements here, but I think the biggest takeaway is that Zero seems to be increasingly focused on making electric motorcycling more accessible through smaller and more affordable options.

For much of its 20-year history, Zero has been a pioneer in the electric motorcycle industry, often offering impressive performance and technology, but at prices that made its motorcycles difficult for many riders to justify. The bikes have long won acclaim, but $20k was a big ask.

The company’s more affordable X Line bikes have already demonstrated that Zero is interested in attracting riders who might never have considered one of its larger electric motorcycles. The street-legal XE takes that concept a step further, and Project Ventura could be an even more important addition.

I’m particularly intrigued by Zero’s explicit acknowledgment that millions of people have spent the last several years riding e-bikes and smaller electric motos, and that many of those riders may be ready to graduate to something bigger.

That’s an audience I’ve been watching grow for years. These are riders who are already comfortable with electric drivetrains, who appreciate the simplicity of twist-and-go riding, and who don’t necessarily have any attachment to gasoline engines or traditional motorcycle culture.

For those riders, a lightweight, fun electric motorcycle capable of 81 mph and priced below $8,000 could be an awfully compelling next step.

The new street-legal XE is similarly exciting, especially for riders who want to combine urban transportation with off-road adventures without needing a truck to haul their bike around.

During the late 60’s to the early 70’s the in thing in motorcycles was the street legal dirt bike. I had a 1971 model Kawasaki 125 street legal dirtbike that I bought for $525 and it was great for getting around as a kid. When this street legal type of EV bike goes below $5000 it could be the next big thing.

And while the new motorcycles are likely to get most of the attention, I wouldn’t overlook the DC fast-charging announcement. Charging speed has long been one of the biggest limitations facing electric motorcycles, particularly for riders interested in longer trips. A sub-30-minute charging stop isn’t quite gasoline-level convenience, but it’s getting much closer to something that can work for real motorcycle touring.

Of course, several important details remain unknown, and we’ll have to wait until 2027 to see how these products and technologies perform in the real world.

But taken together, these announcements suggest Zero is making a serious effort to expand beyond its traditional audience and build electric motorcycles for a much broader range of riders.

And after two decades of helping pioneer the electric motorcycle industry, that’s exactly the kind of evolution I’d like to see.

#4790. BYD’s 3,000-hp EV crashes at about 280 mph — Watch the driver escape unharmed

BYD’s electric supercar delivers an insane nearly 3,000 hp from four electric motors. During a high-speed test, the Yangwang U9 Extreme crashed at about 280 mph, but the driver walked away unharmed.

The Yangwang U9 Xtreme is a “dream car,” as BYD calls it, a souped-up, track-focused version of its electric hypercar.

After it became the world’s fastest production car last year, reaching a top speed of 496.22 km/h, or about 308 mph, BYD is attempting to beat it by breaking the 500 km/h (310 mph) mark.

The Yangwang U9 Xtreme features the world’s first mass-produced 1,200V ultra-high-voltage platform and BYD’s e4 platform.

The quad-motor setup is the first in the world rated at 30,000 rpm, BYD said, delivering nearly 3,000 hp (3,000 PS) combined.

While BYD is preparing to attempt a run this month, the company shared a video of the Yangwang U9 Xtreme crashing in a violent high-speed test.

During the first run, it clocked over 350 km/h (217 mph), no problem. On the second run, it hit over 420 km/h (261 mph).

However, during the third run, BYD was attempting to reach over 450 km/h (280 mph) when the vehicle violently crashed into the guardrail and continued spinning out of control. You can see some of the footage from inside the vehicle.

BYD said that after a comprehensive medical exam, the hospital found the driver in good health with no serious injuries.

When I was still in law enforcement, we did some pursuit driving training and you learn a lot about the dynamics of a vehicle. It's amazing just how quickly and badly something can go wrong at just 100 mph let alone nearly triple that speed. The fact is that the average person can't drive to the max limits of a basic car like a Corolla let alone super cars.

Is it time to reign in the HP and speed wars? Do we need vehicles that can do 0-60 in less than 3 seconds? Unless these companies begin to require 40+ hour specialized training courses in order to buy/operate these high HP vehicles today, then maybe it's time to stop the silly push for more.

And BTW. No matter what the performance levels are of any cars today, I still feel that it would be smart to push legal driving age to at least 17 or 18 instead of 16 and make getting a DL harder to do with way more emphasis on driver's education training.

The vehicle’s high-strength carbon-fiber cabin also held up impressively despite the front and rear damage.

It didn’t take long for BYD to try again, reaching a top speed of 457.89 km/h (284.5 mph) on the next run. Jiang Gong, one of Yangwang’s riders, said he sees some leeway and believes it can break 500 km/h (310 mph).

At the end of the video, BYD said (translated into English), “The ultimate pursuit has no end; the summit will eventually be reached.”

#4789. Empowering a Competitive Advantage to Optimize Team Play

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 15, 2026 | Reviewed by Abigail Fagan

This post is part two of a series. Read part one here.

Welcome back for a dive into the Miami Mindset, a methodology for shaping a mental approach to competition trained by Travis Steele, the head basketball coach of the Miami RedHawks, a basketball team that has compiled a 36-0 regular-season record, extending back to the 2024-25 season.

These are the four core values of the Miami Mindset:

An explanation of each value follows, shared by Coach Steele in an interview for these two posts.

“I need guys that live for competition,” explained Steele. “Competition brings out the best in each other. It’s how we practice. It’s how we lift. It’s how we do academics. Every day’s an audition.”

“We always talk about being the hunter, and not the hunted in everything we do, and it starts with having that competitive mindset,” Steele continued. “We recruit guys that have that gene, but we make sure that comes to life with how we do practice. We create a very, very competitive practice structure that makes that come to life.”

“I use those words because I want it to be a family environment and I want these guys to be great teammates both on and off the court,” detailed Steele. “Elite teammates have a common characteristic, amongst them, that they can make (optimize) others around them. ‘One plus one equals three.’ I can make you better than you are by yourself."

“We get to three if we’re truly invested in each other,” Steele shared. “Pushing each other, holding each other accountable. Accountability is a really important piece, being very direct with teammates.”

“We want guys that are giving energy to others,” Steele said. “How do we make this come to life? If a teammate falls down on the floor, everybody on the floor sprints over and picks him up. We acknowledge the passer. The guy that scores the basket is the one that gets all the credit, so we point to the passer, which I think is important to show connectivity. Another way (for brotherhood) is tight huddles during timeouts and stoppage of play.”

“You’ve got to embrace the process,” Steele explained. “Everybody’s going to go through tough times. Even if you’re a great shooter, you’re going to miss three or four in a row, and the next time you’re open, you’ve got to be able to shoot.”

“We talk a lot about mental toughness,” he said. “We train our guys about how to respond (to adversity) in different ways. ‘What is your response to adversity?’ because we’re all going to face it. It’s a lot easier to talk about it, but you have to apply it. So, we do a lot of adversity training.”

“We coach the response piece to adversity harder than we do our schematic stuff because, ultimately, our team is going to go through tough times in the middle of the game,” shared Steele. “How can we reset our minds back to neutral and be able to continue to do our jobs at a very high level?”

“I put them in adverse situations every day,” he explained. “I want practice to be harder than the game. You have to work on your response to adversity every single day, and that’s been critically important to our success.”

“Embodying undeniable confidence is done by embracing the process and being mentally tough and understand that your response (to adversity) has to be bigger than the event that just happened,” Steele summarized.

“We talked about adversity,” said Steele as he introduced this final core value. “Handling prosperity is just as important. Sometimes guys take their foot off the gas pedal when they’ve won 10 games in a row, or maybe the guy’s playing really well for three or four games in a row, and he takes his foot off the pedal. That’s a pattern for a lot of people, and we have to fight that urge.”

He fights that urge with a picture of a guy driving a car from an inside-the-car view. "Fellas," he explains, "the rearview mirror, there’s a reason why they’re so small. All the games that have happened are in the past ... There’s a reason why the front windshield’s so gosh darned big, because we’ve got to keep our eyes forward. We got to learn from the past, but we’ve got to keep our eyes ahead to see what’s in front of us."

One of the players added, “If we’re listening to everybody telling us how good we are, it distracts us from keeping in front of us.”

“That’s what happens if everybody starts listening to all the people around,” Steele elaborated. “They’re telling them how good they are and then you take the foot off the pedal. You gotta keep your eyes forward. Keep your eye on the ball, or you’re going to strike out. Our guys really understood that."

“My job as one of the leaders of the team, along with our captains, is to make sure that if we sense just an ounce of complacency," Steel explained. "We have different things we can do within practice to ratchet it up quick."

“I try to always gauge the temperature of the room,” he continued. “Where’s their mindset at? Before practice I go up to the players, talk to them, look them in the eyes. I may have to make adjustments. I’ve got a few drills that will raise the competitive spirit in the practice."

“I think the 'Strive for Magis' got really tested last year, more than any year,” Steele reflected, "just because of the run we were on, and I give our guys a lot of credit. We had great player leadership, and we did a great job staying focused on the task at hand and through the whole run that we had.”

As NBA legend Michael Jordan shared with golfer Tiger Woods: “No matter how good they say you are, always keep working on your game.”

Those four core values closely parallel an Acceptance and Commitment Training (ACT) concept called psychological flexibility, defined as:

The ability to stay present with difficult circumstances, thoughts, and feelings, without letting them dictate your behavior, so you can respond effectively.

Coach Steele and his Miami RedHawks basketball team have embraced and empowered all that!

#4788. Tesla cuts FSD’s speed margin from 50% to 10% over the limit in Europe

Tesla’s driver-assistance system in Europe can no longer be set to drive 50% over the speed limit. The Netherlands’ RDW, which approved the system in April, confirmed today that it signed off on an update capping the speed margin at 10% and officially renaming “FSD Supervised” to Tesla Assisted Driving.

The changes land weeks before EU member states could vote on approving the system across the bloc, and they match the conditions Germany set last month.

The RDW’s notice is the clearest official confirmation of how the system was configured when the Dutch regulator approved FSD Supervised on April 10. The “speed margin,” which lets the system deviate from the detected limit for “safety and traffic flow,” could go up to +50%. On a 100 km/h highway, that’s 150 km/h. In a 30 km/h city zone, it’s 45 km/h.

Now the margin tops out at +10%. It also resets to 0% every time the car starts, and the RDW says other speed-related functions are now disabled by default. It didn’t say which ones.

The new name shows up on the in-car display, in the owner’s manual, and in other driver information on HW4 vehicles. Tesla pushes all of it over the air.

The RDW describes the update as “several minor changes” that Tesla asked it to assess. The regulator says it checked the implementation, found no adverse or unintended effects, and folded the changes into the existing type approval without amending or extending it. It also stresses that the rename doesn’t change what the system does. It’s still driver assistance, and the driver is still responsible.

The RDW notified the nine member states that recognize its approval: Belgium, Croatia, Czechia, Denmark, Estonia, Latvia, Lithuania, Slovakia, and Slovenia. That’s 10 countries counting the Netherlands, up from eight at the start of October.

The EU-wide decision still sits with the Technical Committee on Motor Vehicles (TCMV). The RDW says those talks are confidential and won’t comment on them. As we reported, the EU pushed the vote to December at the earliest, and passing it takes a qualified majority: 15 of 27 member states representing at least 65% of the EU population.

Speed has been the biggest problem for Tesla’s file in Brussels. In June, Sweden asked the EU to reject FSD unless Tesla removed the ability to set speeds above the posted limit. In September, a Brussels pedestrian safety group found the system speeding in 55% of the 30 km/h segments it tested, in a country that had already approved it. France has cited urban speeding as one of its reasons for refusing to recognize the Dutch approval.

Then, on October 6, Germany’s Transport Ministry said it would back EU approval after Tesla agreed to drop the “Full Self-Driving” name, which the ministry called misleading, and to cap speed tolerance at 10%. Today’s RDW update delivers both.

But 10% isn’t what Sweden asked for. Sweden wanted no setting above the limit at all. Several European countries are known for enforcing speed limits strictly.

Good, but that’s only part of the problem. Tesla’s biggest issue with FSD right now is that it doesn’t properly detect the speed limits.

Doing 10% over the limit in North America is almost expected. However, in Europe you can get a speeding ticket for 5 kms over the limit by an automated camera/radar system. When I rent a car over there I drive the posted limit.

Being locked at 10% over the limit doesn’t mean much if you don’t know the actual limit.

The name change is needed globally and it is past due. “Tesla Assisted Driving” describes what the system does. Meanwhile, Tesla still sells the exact same kind of software in the US as “Full Self-Driving (Supervised)” for $99 a month. If it’s misleading in Berlin, it’s misleading in California.

Practically, this update gets Tesla Germany’s vote. The question for December is whether a 10% cap is enough for France, Italy, and Spain, or whether Sweden’s zero-tolerance line wins out.

If you’re buying an EV, powering it with home solar can cut your fuel costs to nearly zero. With electricity rates up almost 10% last year and expected to keep climbing, going solar is one of the best ways to protect yourself against rising costs.
