#4786. Hormone Therapy May Help Improve Menopause Brain Fog, Mood Changes

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Menopause hormone therapy (MHT), also known as hormone replacement therapy (HRT), helps manage various menopause symptoms.

Currently, MHT is approved by the Food and Drug Administration (FDA) to help relieve hot flashes, night sweats, vaginal dryness, and dyspareunia (pain during sex).

However, recent research has found that MHT may also help improve mental symptoms like “brain fog” and mood changes.

Other research also found that MHT does not affect dementia risk. This research followed the FDA’s removal of the black box warning on these products, which suggested they may increase the risk.

Together, these studies show that MHT may help people experiencing menopause in ways not previously realized and that it is generally safe to use.

Research published in Scientific Reports on September 1 suggests that an estrogen hormone treatment that combines estriol and progesterone may help ease the symptoms of brain fog during menopause.

Brain fog may involve memory lapses, difficulty concentrating, and slower thinking.

Just about two-thirds of women experience cognitive difficulties, like brain fog, during the menopausal transition. However, there is currently no Food and Drug Administration (FDA)- approved treatment for these symptoms.

This study was an observational case series that followed 20 menopausal women with an average age of 53. Each participant was given a baseline assessment for cognitive symptoms prior to treatment.

They were then treated for 12 months with a customized regimen of estriol and progesterone.

Estriol is a natural form of estrogen that occurs during pregnancy and has been used in Europe and Asia to treat other symptoms of menopause.

After 12 months, the participants reported significant reductions in:

“Estrogen plays a well-documented role in protecting the brain, yet there are still no approved type and dose of treatment that specifically targets the cognitive symptoms so many women experience during menopause,” Rhonda Voskuhl, MD, neurologist, member of the Comprehensive Menopause Center at UCLA Health, and senior study author, said in a press release.

“Women are often told to simply live with brain fog, when in fact there is a neuroscience basis for it, and potentially a way to address it. That gap in care is what motivated us to look more closely at estriol,” Voskuhl said.

This was a small, preliminary case series without a placebo group or randomization. More research is needed to confirm the benefits for cognitive symptoms.

A study published September 9 in Menopause, the journal of the Menopause Society, showed that hormone therapy may help improve mood changes in women in midlife.

The researchers noted that mood disturbances, such as depression, irritability, anxiety, and mental exhaustion, affect up to 68% of women during menopause.

“The erratic changes and loss of estrogen associated with this hormonal transition can lower serotonin and dopamine levels, which [are] associated with depression and cognitive changes, including poor concentration, brain fog, and memory loss,” said Sherry Ross, MD, board certified OB/GYN and Women’s Health Expert at Providence Saint John’s Health Center in Santa Monica, CA, who wasn’t involved in the study.

The study included 260 individuals with an average age of 52 years. Just over half of the participants had a history of anxiety or depression, and 24% were using an antidepressant medication at the start of the study.

Each participant completed the Menopause Rating Scale (MRS) both before and after initiating hormone therapy for FDA-approved indications, such as hot flashes and night sweats.

The average follow-up during the study was 4.5 months. Between the baseline and the follow-up, there was a significant reduction in the MRS psychological scores. The percentage of participants with severe mood symptoms decreased from 62.3% to 24.6%.

Treatment response didn’t appear to differ by age, menopausal stage, psychiatric history, or antidepressant use. These findings suggest that hormone therapy may help improve mood symptoms in midlife, especially for those with higher severity of symptoms before treatment.

“This study supports the evidence that starting hormone therapy for the typical disruptive symptoms of menopause may bring collateral benefits, including improvement in mood,” Ross said. “Communicating with your healthcare provider is always best to manage your symptoms and prepare yourself properly for this next stage of life.”

For decades, women have been warned about the serious potential risks associated with menopause hormone therapy (MHT). This included an FDA “black box” warning citing complications like cardiovascular disease, cancer, and dementia.

However, a recent meta-analysis commissioned by the World Health Organization (WHO) that included data from more than a million women, and was published in The Lancet Healthy Longevity, found that MHT neither increased nor decreased dementia risk.

“The World Health Organisation currently provides no guidance on MHT and dementia risk, leaving a critical gap for women, clinicians, and policymakers. Our work will help inform upcoming WHO guidelines on reducing the risk of cognitive decline and dementia,” Melissa Melville, a PhD candidate and aging researcher in the Department of Clinical Health Psychology at the University College London, and first author of the study, told Healthline in a previous interview.

In the same interview, Melville stressed that the importance of this work is twofold: for clinicians, there is an urgent need for higher-quality research, and for patients, decisions about MHT should be guided by symptoms and well-established benefits and risks.

#4785. Volkswagen’s affordable ID. Polo wins German Car of the Year 2027 as EVs dominate

Volkswagen’s new ID. Polo took the overall title of “German Car of the Year 2027,” starting at just €24,995 ($28,000).

After winning in its class among entry-level models (up to €25,000), the VW ID. Polo beat out strong international competition to take the top spot as German Car of the Year for 2027 among all vehicles.

The Volkswagen Group has won two years in a row with a compact EV. Last year, it was the Skoda Elroq.

Volkswagen opened orders for the ID. Polo with the 52 kWh battery at the end of April. In July, it added the entry-level model with the 37 kWh battery, starting at just €24,995 ($28,000) with a WLTP range of up to 207 miles (334 km).

The Life and Style variants are also available with the 37 kWh battery, starting from €29,195 ($32,500).

With DC fast charging speeds of up to 90 kW, the ID. Polo with the 37 kWh battery can recharge from 10% to 80% in about 23 minutes.

The prestigious award “confirms what the ID. Polo stands for,” VW brand CEO Thomas Schäfer said. He added the entry-level EV “brings electric mobility to where it can make the greatest impact: right into people’s everyday lives.”

Since orders opened at the end of April, Schäfer said “well over 50,000 people have ordered their ID. Polo.”

As the first of what the company calls “a true Volkswagen again,” the ID. Polo is the first to bear an established name and the brand’s new Pure Positive design language.

The jury includes nearly 50 German and international auto journalists, and the award is considered one of the most prestigious in the industry.

The reason this class of vehicles is winning awards is that Europe buys a lot of B-segment vehicles (small hatchbacks) that aren't intended as city-only transport. Many families use them as their only vehicle.

And, up till now, there haven't been many BEVs available in that category.

It is compact, reasonably powerful, reasonably fast charging, acceptable charging rate, has good luggage capacity and feels nice to drive.

In other segments, electric cars swept the awards, with the Kia PV5 van winning in the compact class (€25,000 and €40,000), and Mercedes-Benz GLC Electric taking the top spot in the premium segment (€40,000 to €70,000). Hyundai’s electric sports car, the IONIQ 6 N, won German Performance Car 2027.

Martin Sander, the Group’s sales boss, said earlier this month the company is “seeing a significantly accelerated trend towards all-electric vehicles” in its home market.

VW Group’s Electric Urban Car Family has received nearly 150,000 orders after series production began in June. The lineup currently includes the VW ID. Polo and ID. Cross, plus the Cupra Raval and Skoda Epiq, all based on the MEB+ platform.

While those in the US won’t see the entry-level ID. Polo, Volkswagen is set to launch the ID. Tiguan in early 2027 as a replacement for the ID.4.

#4783. Heart Failure Is Becoming More Common in Adults Under 65, Report Finds

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

An estimated 56 million people worldwide were living with heart failure in 2023, more than double the number in 1990, according to a new Global Burden of Disease (GBD) analysis published September 30 in JAMA Cardiology.

Heart failure is a chronic condition in which the heart cannot pump enough blood to meet the body’s needs. Despite its name, heart failure doesn’t mean the heart has stopped beating.

A separate 2026 report from the Heart Failure Society of America (HFSA) found that heart failure contributed to more than 450,000 deaths in the United States in 2023, with particularly sharp increases in mortality among adults under 65.

Approximately 7.7 million U.S. adults (3%) are living with heart failure, according to the HFSA report. That number is projected to rise to 8.7 million by 2030, 10.3 million by 2040, and 11.4 million by 2050.

The GBD researchers said the findings highlight the growing burden of heart failure associated with population growth and aging, as well as the need for prevention and treatment strategies targeting common causes such as ischemic heart disease and hypertension (high blood pressure).

Between 2010 and 2022, heart failure hospitalization rates increased among adults under 65 while declining among those 65 and older. Between 2001 and 2020, estimated heart failure mortality rates more than doubled among adults ages 25 to 44 and nearly doubled among those ages 45 to 64.

“I think we’re seeing the impact of cardiovascular risk factors developing earlier in life. Conditions such as obesity, diabetes, hypertension, and sleep apnea are affecting younger adults at higher rates than in previous generations,” Kevin Shah, MD, Program Director of Heart Failure Outreach at MemorialCare Heart & Vascular Institute at Long Beach Medical Center in Long Beach, CA, who wasn’t involved in the analysis, told Healthline.

“When those risk factors are present for decades, they can eventually lead to heart failure at a younger age,” he said.

Amit Blumfield, MD, a cardiologist at Manhattan Cardiology in New York City who was also not involved in the analysis, noted that improvements in early detection may also play a role.

“Our tools to detect early heart failure are also improving with time, and as a result, patients may receive an earlier diagnosis and can hopefully start medical therapy to prevent progression or possibly even reverse the process entirely.”

Heart failure contributed to 452,573 deaths in the United States in 2023, accounting for approximately 14.6% of all deaths that year, according to the HFSA report.

Age-adjusted heart failure mortality rates increased by approximately 37% between 2010 and 2023, with preliminary 2024 data suggesting the upward trend is continuing.

This increase appears to have reversed more than a decade of previous progress in reducing heart failure mortality.

Ischemic heart disease and hypertension were among the most common causes of heart failure in older adults, according to the GBD report. However, the underlying causes varied considerably by age group and region.

Among children and young adults, congenital heart abnormalities and rheumatic heart disease (RHD) were common causes of heart failure. Heart failure associated with alcohol use was most prevalent during middle adulthood.

The analysis also identified regional differences in the underlying causes of heart failure:

The global burden of heart failure is expected to grow as populations age.

While not all cases of heart failure can be prevented, managing certain risk factors can help reduce your risk. These include:

According to Shah, other important steps to help prevent heart failure include maintaining a healthy weight, staying physically active, and managing conditions such as diabetes and high cholesterol.

He added that regular medical care is important because many of these risk factors can be identified and treated long before heart failure develops.

“Heart failure is often treatable and may even be reversible with early diagnosis and treatment. It is important for patients to seek help early if they have concerning symptoms, such as exertional shortness of breath, chest pain, leg swelling, or anything else they’re concerned about. Talk with your doctors early and often,” said Blumfield.

#4781. Modernizing Nursery Rhymes for the 21st Century

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 15, 2026 | Reviewed by Jessica Schrader

Back in the 1960s when I was growing up, nursery rhymes were an initial gateway to music for children. Their simple melodies, repeated lyrics, and predictable rhyming schemes gently introduced infants and toddlers to the fundamentals of music. What the words actually meant was of little, if any, concern to us young listeners. But times change and these traditional rhymes fell out of favor.

Revitalizing these classic verses—by preserving their developmental benefits while modernizing their themes—has become the life's passion of early childhood music educator Pam Gittleman. Raised in a musical family, Gittleman was a talented flutist through her college years. However, she did not plan on a career in music. After earning an MBA in marketing, she spent a decade in the corporate sector at a prominent advertising agency before leaving to raise her family.

Drawing on a desire to work with children, she leveraged her musical background to reinvent herself as a preschool music teacher. Over seven years at a summer day camp, she experimented with various teaching methods for children aged three to five, ultimately designing a curriculum centered on singing, movement, and call-and-response activities. As her own children grew up, she pivoted to teaching at nursery schools during the academic year.

Over the years, Gittleman noticed a striking trend: the young children in her classrooms were unfamiliar with the traditional nursery rhymes that prior generations knew by heart. Curious, she questioned parents and fellow educators and discovered that the internet had changed how families vetted content. Today’s parents were consulting childhood development experts and sharing their findings online. Many of them found traditional nursery rhymes to be outdated, dark, and inappropriate for young minds, so they avoided the genre entirely.

While recognizing the validity of these concerns, Gittleman’s professional training and instincts told her that the time-honored nursery rhymes still held value for teaching language skills as well as social and emotional intelligence. To bridge this gap, she began rewriting the lyrics.

Her venture received a major boost in 2020 when she enrolled in the year-long Advanced Leadership Initiative (ALI) at Harvard University. Despite pandemic-related disruptions, she had access to advanced graduate courses in education, thus establishing a firm theoretical foundation for how music and storytelling contribute to social and emotional learning (SEL) in early childhood.

Gittleman emphasizes that the linguistic structure of nursery rhymes is vital for early language acquisition. For instance, in the verses of “This old man, he played one, he played knick-knack on my thumb," children begin to learn the concept of rhyming. Educators can then challenge them to find other words that rhyme with "one," effectively expanding their vocabulary. Furthermore, because "one" and "thumb" don’t rhyme strictly speaking, children also learn that the concept of rhyming has flexibility.

Since many nursery rhymes are songs—set in a rhythmic structure and vocalized to musical notes—they stimulate important neurological pathways. Rhythm, for example, is processed simultaneously in the auditory and motor regions of the brain. Nursery rhymes capitalize on this connection, helping children regulate their motor functions through alternating periods of movement and rest. Gittleman notes that mastering this physical self-regulation translates to better behavioral and social functioning with others.1

Nursery rhymes can introduce basic math skills, too. Returning to the example of “This Old Man,” the song helps children learn to count, reaching ever higher numbers as they grow older. The rhymes are also repetitive—as children catch on to patterns, they learn to predict and reproduce them on their own. The excitement and anticipation of what comes next feeds the motivation and reward centers of the brain, allowing young children to experience joy while making music and learning language.

Yet another contribution of nursery rhymes is found in call-and-response verses. The call-and-response structure mirrors conversational patterns, teaching young children the social importance of taking turns.2

Equipped with decades of classroom experience along with academic knowledge obtained through her program at Harvard, Gittleman has systematically updated classic lyrics to be of service to social and emotional learning.3 Prioritizing kindness, empathy, and gratitude, she transformed "Three Blind Mice" to "Three Kind Mice" and, under her pen, the story of “Humpty Dumpty” has been reimagined so that when he falls off the wall his friends come to his aid, rather than leaving him in pieces on the ground.

Gittleman has since published several volumes of her work under the title Nursery Rhymes for Kinder Times®.4,5 These books feature engaging illustrations for young children and structured text to help growing children develop literacy skills. Pairing each rhyme with emojis and guiding questions, she provides adults with tools to enhance their children’s engagement with the stories while nurturing rich conversations that support emotional intelligence. When she introduced the curriculum to a local preschool, an informal survey of parents and teachers confirmed a positive impact on the children's social and emotional learning.6

Seeking to expand her reach, Gittleman utilized her advertising background to pitch her updated rhymes to the music industry. Her efforts won major support in 2022 when legendary children's performer Raffi—along with his protege, Lindsay Munroe—recorded 15 of her modernized rhymes. Since then, prominent children's recording artist Laurie Berkner has also recorded some of her material.

1 Williams, K.E. (2018). “Moving to the beat: Using music, rhythm and movement to enhance self-regulation in early childhood classrooms.” International Journal of Early Childhood, 50(1), 85-100. https://doi.org/10.1007/s13158-018-0215-y.

2 Wolf, D. (2017). “Why Making Music Matters,” research paper commissioned by Carnegie Hall. https://wolfbrown.com/wp-content/uploads/Why-Music-Matters.pdf.

3 Visit https://www.forkindertimes.com/ to learn more about Pam Gittleman’s work and products.

4 Gittleman, Pam (2023). Nursery Rhymes for Kinder Times, Volume 1. Herndon, VA: Mascot Books.

5 Gittleman, Pam (2025). Nursery Rhymes for Kinder Times, Volume 2. Herndon, VA: Mascot Books.

6 Gittleman adds that parents of bilingual learners found the rhymes particularly useful for enhancing their kids’ English skills.

#4780. Viola Davis Says Shingles Pain Was a ‘9 Out of 10’: How It Changed Her Life

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Emmy, Grammy, Oscar, and Tony Award-winning actress and producer Viola Davis was 50 years old and at the height of her career when she developed shingles.

“All of a sudden, I got a rash in the middle of traveling and juggling [back-to-back] movies that started in the middle of my stomach [and] wrapped around my back,” she told Healthline. “And the pain was definitely at a 9 out of 10.”

Focused on work, Davis didn’t see a doctor right away. Her symptoms eventually subsided but later returned on her upper back.

“That is when I was like, ‘Oh, my goodness, I just got bitten by a poisonous spider,” said Davis. “It felt like really bad nerve pain…then someone mentioned the word shingles.”

After seeing a doctor, Davis learned she had shingles, which is caused by the same virus responsible for chickenpox. She also learned that the risk increases with age as the immune system naturally weakens.

“It can also lead to herpetic neuralgia, lifelong pain that can result from a shingles outbreak,” Robyn Liu, MD, member of the board of directors at the American Academy of Family Physicians, told Healthline.

Shingles can affect daily life, including work and sleep.

“Many people don’t realize that shingles can be associated with nerve pain, and for some, that pain can last for months or even years after the rash has resolved due to potential long-term complications,” Jennifer Caudle, DO, family medicine physician and associate professor at Rowan University School of Osteopathic Medicine, told Healthline.

Nearly one in three people in the U.S. develops shingles in their lifetime.

“Anyone who has had chickenpox can get it, but the risk goes up as we age,” said Liu.

After learning about shingles, Davis said she realized that she could no longer ignore her body.

“You just cannot. I mean, I know that people say it at nauseam, but, for me, it meant slowing down,” she said. “But then the shingles slowed me down…even though I was getting up, I was working out, [the] pain and the sluggishness was awful.”

Now 61, Davis has teamed up with GSK for its “Shingles, actually” campaign to raise awareness about shingles.

“[I’m] sending the message out there for women to really honor themselves and honor their bodies,” she said. “Their bodies literally speak. They’re the other character in their lives.”

After her experience with shingles, Davis began scheduling regular annual checkups.

“Before that time, I did absolutely go to my doctor, but only when it was absolutely necessary, you know, to get my colonoscopy, get my mammogram, but I never went if something felt odd,” said Davis.

Now, whenever she feels off, she doesn’t hesitate to see her doctor. She makes it a point to be honest with them about what’s going on with her body, and she is not shy about asking questions.

“I feel that it’s my job. It’s what I owe myself, and not only [do] I owe myself, it’s what I owe my daughter, it’s what I owe my husband, it’s what I owe my elderly mom, who is in advanced dementia [and] I take care of her,” Davis said.

Caudle stressed the importance of bringing questions to your doctor.

“That’s what we’re here for. Sometimes patients ​tell me they don’t realize they can ask questions or introduce a topic that their healthcare provider didn’t bring up first,” she said. “But a simple question like, ‘Am I at risk for shingles?’ can be a great place to begin.”

Davis has made stress reduction a priority. When she developed shingles, she was juggling an intense workload and adjusting to life as a new mom after adopting her daughter.

“I was someone who just ignored stress,” said Davis. “[When] we meet each other, it becomes a contest: Who can work through their stress the best? I felt like I had to strike while the iron was hot.”

Today, Davis makes stress reduction a priority through activities such as spraying her pillows with lavender, taking Epsom salt baths, meditating, going to therapy, exercising, spending time outdoors, using vagus nerve stimulation, and connecting with family and friends.

“It’s like I overdo it now because I feel like I have to overdo it in order to compete with my schedule. But that’s what I owe myself,” Davis said.

Still, her profession can be intense and time consuming due to shooting and promotional schedules. She still makes time to de-stress and focuses on projects that bring her joy.

“Your career is only as important as how much you enjoy it; how much it adds to your life,” said Davis. “[For] me, it used to be something that I felt defined my worth. I don’t feel like it defines my worth now…I feel that I’m already worthy… I was born worthy. That’s how I see it.”

Vaccination is the best way to protect against shingles, Liu said. The shingles vaccine is more than 90% effective at preventing the disease.

“For children, that protection begins with the chickenpox vaccine, which can be given along with other routine childhood immunizations. Preventing chickenpox also lowers the risk of developing shingles later in life,” she said.

Liu recommends two doses of the shingles vaccine for adults 50 and older, as well as adults 19 and older with weakened immune systems.

“Some patients may have more questions because side effects can be more noticeable than with other vaccines, so it’s important to understand the tradeoff: short-term discomfort from the vaccine versus the risk of lasting complications from shingles,” said Liu.

Side effects may include headache or redness, swelling, itching, and soreness where the shot was given.

“I tell my patients that these reactions are common and usually clear up within a day or two. They’re a normal sign that your body is building protection,” said Liu.

#4779. Hyundai’s electric SUV finds some footing, with IONIQ 5 sales topping 33,000 in 2026

The Hyundai IONIQ 5 remains one of America’s most popular EVs outside Tesla, with October sales up 21%.

While the Tesla Model Y and Model 3 still dominate the US electric vehicle market, accounting for about 50% of all sales through the third quarter, it was the Hyundai IONIQ 5 that ranked third.

The market has slowed since the $7,500 federal tax credit was removed last September, with several automakers reporting fewer EV sales than in 2025 when buyers rushed to claim the credit.

Hyundai responded by cutting prices on the 2026 IONIQ 5 by up to nearly $10,000 on some trims, with a new starting MSRP of around $35,000.

As the market shows signs of recovery, IONIQ 5 sales bounced back in October, rising 21% to 1,981 units.

Through the first 10 months of 2026, Hyundai sold 33,093 IONIQ 5s in the US, still down 23% from the more than 42,700 it sold during the same period last year, when credit was still available.

Hyundai said the IONIQ 5 helped drive growth as the company posted its best October in the US ever with 73,452 vehicles sold, a 5% increase YOY.

While Hyundai sold only 169 IONIQ 9s last month (-47%), sales are still up 49% through October, with nearly 6,700 units sold.

Hyundai’s hybrids set a new October sales record, rising 20% from last year and accounting for 29% of total sales in October. Through October, Hyundai’s total US sales are up 3% YOY at 770,916 units.

Since not all automakers report monthly sales figures, it’s hard to determine where the IONIQ 5 stands through October.

Earlier today, Hyundai’s sister brand Kia reported that sales of its three-row electric SUV, the EV9, were up 11% year over year in October. EV9 sales are now up 5% year-to-date, with 13,835 units sold through October.

The cheapest 2027 Hyundai IONIQ 5 SE Standard Range starts at $35,250 and offers up to 245 miles of range.

If you want more range, upgrading to the extended-range SE RWD model costs an extra $2,500, but you get up to 318 miles of range on a single charge.

#4777. Pennsylvania is way ahead on NEVI charging – now comes another $37M

Pennsylvania is putting $37 million in federal NEVI funding toward what PennDOT says will be 515 new public EV charging ports across the Philadelphia region, including curbside chargers.

The awards cover Bucks, Chester, Delaware, Montgomery, and Philadelphia counties. PennDOT says the projects will expand public charging availability by 27% across the region and 53% in Philadelphia.

For drivers without a driveway, curbside charging gives them somewhere to plug in while parked near home.

It’s Electric PA received $2.93 million for 157 on-street Level 2 ports across 13 Philadelphia neighborhoods. Voltpost received funding for another 62 curbside ports across the four suburban counties.

Fast charging is getting funding, too. PositivEnergy received $7.2 million for 54 DC fast-charging ports at 11 Philadelphia Parking Authority lots. Tesla received two $392,000 awards for eight ports each at Royal Farms in Broomall and Wawa in Plymouth Meeting.

The money comes from the federal National Electric Vehicle Infrastructure (NEVI) program, created by the Biden administration’s Bipartisan Infrastructure Law. Pennsylvania’s five-year allocation is $171.5 million.

NEVI initially focused on fast charging along designated highway corridors. States can spend the money more broadly after the Federal Highway Administration certifies that their corridor charging requirements have been met. Pennsylvania received that certification on September 22, 2025.

In fact, Pennsylvania has built 54 EV charging stations using NEVI funds so far – more than double the number of any other state.

PennDOT’s community charging program supports both Level 2 and DC fast charging. Its published program minimum requirements include at least four ports per proposed station, at least 6 kW per port, two ADA-compliant parking spaces, and J1772 or CCS connectors on every port. Equipment must also meet Buy America requirements.

PennDOT lists 71 awards in this southeastern round as part of its roughly $100 million statewide community charging effort.

Applications for the western region close November 20. Central and eastern Pennsylvania applications close March 12, 2027.

thanks for posting. PA continues to lead the country in turning this program into actual chargers. The statewide coverage is night and day from just 2-3 years ago.

For the southeastern projects, PennDOT’s estimated schedule puts environmental reviews in November 2026 through January 2027, followed by agreement execution in January and February. Drivers will still have to wait for construction and commissioning before these awards become places to plug in.

#4775. PMOS, Formerly PCOS, May Be Associated with Alzheimer's Disease Risk

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Polyendocrine metabolic ovarian syndrome (PMOS), formerly known as polycystic ovary syndrome (PCOS), has been linked to changes in brain function and cognition that may overlap with those seen in Alzheimer’s disease.

However, a review published October 13 in the Journal of the Endocrine Society suggests that metabolic conditions commonly associated with PMOS, rather than PMOS itself, may help explain a potential link to Alzheimer’s disease and dementia risk.

“Researchers are exploring whether some metabolic conditions commonly seen in women with PMOS, such as insulin resistance, obesity, type 2 diabetes, [and] elevated cholesterol, could play a role in dementia, since these are already known risk factors for cognitive decline,” Yesika Garcia, MD, board certified endocrinologist with Medical Offices of Manhattan, who was not involved in the study, told Healthline.

“In other words, any potential connection may be related to these underlying metabolic issues rather than PMOS itself. I think it’s important to clarify that an association does not mean causation,” she added.

The researchers searched PubMed for studies published through 2025 examining brain structure and function, cognitive performance, biomarkers associated with neurodegenerative diseases, and dementia risk in people with PMOS.

Because PMOS is a newer term, the researchers searched for studies using both PMOS and PCOS.

The review identified studies suggesting that people with PMOS may experience mild differences in certain cognitive abilities, including:

Some brain imaging studies using MRI and PET scans identified differences in brain structure and function among people with PMOS. Researchers also observed hormonal changes associated with differences in gray matter.

Some studies identified elevated biomarkers associated with Alzheimer’s disease in people with PMOS. These findings appeared to overlap with metabolic changes seen in type 2 diabetes, particularly those associated with obesity and insulin resistance.

The researchers also found that neurological differences were less pronounced when people with PMOS were compared with metabolically healthy control groups, suggesting that associated metabolic conditions may play an important role in these findings.

Overall, the review suggests that metabolic conditions associated with PMOS may contribute to changes in brain health, although more research is needed to determine whether PMOS independently affects Alzheimer’s disease or dementia risk.

“I think we need to be careful about drawing conclusions too early. The research raises some good questions, but it doesn’t prove that PCOS [PMOS] causes Alzheimer’s disease,” said Dung Trinh, MD, internist, of MemorialCare Medical Group and Chief Medical Officer of Healthy Brain Clinic in Irvine, CA, who wasn’t involved in the study.

“What it does reinforce is how important metabolic health may be when it comes to protecting our brains as we get older,” he added.

Sherry Ross, MD, board certified OB/GYN and Women’s Health Expert at Providence Saint John’s Health Center in Santa Monica, CA, who also wasn’t involved in the study, agreed. She also added that more attention and research need to be given to PMOS, brain changes, and future risks of dementia.

There are various ways that PMOS and dementia may share pathways and overlap, according to the researchers. Some of these shared pathways include:

Trinh explained that insulin resistance may be one of the most important connections between PMOS and dementia.

Although insulin is commonly associated with diabetes and blood sugar regulation, it also plays an important role in brain function. When insulin signaling doesn’t work properly, it may interfere with how brain cells use energy, potentially contributing to problems with memory and thinking over time.

Chronic inflammation, which can occur in people with PMOS, may also play a role in Alzheimer’s disease, Trinh told Healthline.

“Researchers are looking at how these metabolic changes may affect amyloid and tau, two proteins associated with Alzheimer’s. Some brain imaging studies have even found differences in brain structure and function among women with PCOS [PMOS],” he said.

Ross added that elevated androgen levels, which are common in people with PMOS, may also play a role in dementia risk.

“An important distinction is that these risk factors are not present in every woman with PMOS, and having PMOS does not automatically mean someone will experience cognitive decline,” cautioned Garcia.

Garcia noted that many of the dementia risk factors associated with PMOS are modifiable.

“Regular exercise, including both cardiovascular activity and strength training, can improve insulin sensitivity, help with weight management, and support cardiovascular and brain health,” she added.

“A Mediterranean-style diet, with plenty of vegetables, fruits, whole grains, healthy fats, and lean sources of protein, is a reasonable place to start,” he said.

Managing blood sugar, cholesterol, and blood pressure is also important, Trinh and Garcia noted.

“Medication, like metformin, can also improve insulin resistance, which may further reduce the risk of dementia,” Ross added.

Getting adequate sleep, avoiding smoking, and staying mentally and socially active are additional ways to support long-term brain health, Garcia told Healthline.

“For someone with PCOS [PMOS], I would encourage regular visits with their healthcare provider to monitor metabolic health and address problems early,” said Trinh.

“While we cannot say that these interventions specifically prevent dementia in women with PMOS, we know they benefit overall metabolic and cardiovascular health, which are closely connected to brain health,” explained Garcia.

#4774. BYD’s flagship luxury EV with 1,000+ km of range hits 4,000 sales in just 19 days

BYD wasted no time, with sales of its new flagship electric sedan reaching 4,000 in just 19 days since its launch.

With a record of more than 300,000 electric vehicles sold last month, BYD sold more EVs than the US did in the entire third quarter, combined.

A big part of the higher demand is thanks to BYD’s latest breakthrough technology, including its second-generation Blade Batteries and Flash Charging technology.

BYD began equipping vehicles with the new battery and charging tech in March, and over the past six months it has posted higher year-over-year sales growth. Through October, BYD sold 1,953,700 vehicles, up 6% compared to the same period in 2025.

On October 13, BYD launched the Great Han, or “Da Han” in China, the first fully electric D-segment flagship sedan in its Dynasty lineup.

Just 19 days after its launch, BYD said Great Han EV sales reached 4,000 units. At that rate, sales could reach 10,000 or more in its first full month.

With “class-beating high value,” BYD said, “it has firmly established itself in the mid-to-high-end sedan segment above 250,000 yuan,” which is about $37,500.

The Great Han is available in three variants, priced from 235,900 yuan, or about $35,000. Buyers can choose from single- or dual-motor powertrains. The standard rear-wheel-drive (RWD) Premium trim offers up to 1,008 km (626 miles) of CLTC range, while the all-wheel-drive (AWD) Flagship variant offers up to 880 km (546 miles).

All models use a 102.326 kWh BYD Blade 2.0 battery pack. The RWD models have a single 370 kW (496 hp) motor, while the AWD variant adds a 200 kW (268 hp) front motor for a combined 570 kW (764 hp).

Like most of its new vehicles, the Great Han has a roof-mounted LiDAR and BYD’s God’s Eye B, a more advanced version of its intelligent driving system.

The ADAS system offers safety and convenience features, including Navigate on Autopilot on highways and in cities, plus remote autonomous parking.

All variants also include premium features, such as BYD’s DiSus-A dual-chamber air suspension system, rear-wheel steering, and zero-gravity front seats.

Measuring 5,256 mm in length, 1,999 mm in width, and 1,510 mm in height, with a 3,130 mm wheelbase, the Great Han is slightly bigger than the Tesla Model S and Lucid Air. It’s closer in size to the long-wheelbase Mercedes-Benz S-Class.

Thanks to BYD’s second-gen Blade Batteries and Flash Charging technology, the vehicle’s battery can charge from 10% to 70% in just 5 minutes. A full recharge (10% to 97%) takes just 9 minutes.

The Great Han joins the Great Tang SUV as the flagship sedan under BYD’s Dynasty lineup. BYD announced that Great Tang sales reached 14,000 units in October, marking four months of sales above 10,000.

#4773. Are Your Dating Standards Too High?

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 15, 2026 | Reviewed by Jessica Schrader

Here's something that you never see on social media: men accusing women of having dating standards that are too high. Yep, that's never happened. Neither has women accusing men of the same. In fact, no one of any gender has ever accused anyone else of such things, right?

Umm, not exactly. Anytime someone posts something about having certain dating requirements—unless it's something irrefutable like "I would like someone human"—it's not too surprising to see responses like "you are being unrealistic," "you are too high-maintenance," "no wonder you are single," or "look at yourself." Then the person may fire back with something like "Well, sorry for having standards," and then back and forth it goes.

The question then is where you fall on the whole "got to have some kind of standards" to "being unrealistic" spectrum. That ranges from people who clearly should lower their standards—because a supermodel may not want to date a guy who spends all his days in pajamas posting about red pills on Reddit—to those who actually should raise their standards. Well, here are some signs that your standards may be too high:

If your standards focus on superficial things like looks, then it could be impeding your looking for a partner. For example, a 2020 survey by Pew Research Center found that 15% of respondents “Definitely/probably would not” date someone of a different race/ethnicity—which could end up ruling out lots of people who could be great fits from personality and values standpoints. The same might happen when you base your standards around hair (beyond asking that it be washed occasionally), clothes (with the possible exception of ruling out those who wear a hazmat suit all the time), and other stuff that in the long run won't matter that much for a relationship.

Many superficial things like looks, hobbies, and fame can change with time—as could your perception and feelings about them. So, be careful about anchoring your preferences on something that may be fleeting versus enduring—especially since the enduring stuff is what often becomes most endearing.

Beware of sticking rigidly to any rules such as the so-called "6-6-6 rule" that I detailed before for "A Funny Bone to Pick." If you are completely unwilling to consider anyone who's a bit under any of the sixes—height, salary, or abs—ask yourself why. Otherwise, you could end up like the guy I knew who specified that his wife needed to look a certain way, speak at least five languages, and have certain types of degrees, but kind of neglected putting "kind" and "loyal" on that list. Guess what ended up happening?

The "6-6-6 rule" is just one example of a standard that you may have heard from elsewhere and then adopted it without really thinking why. Using standards set by others may make others—like fake friends, advertisers, politicians, and Hollywood execs—happy. But what about you?

Of course, don't summarily reject all suggestions from others—especially when those who are close to and care about you tell you repeatedly that your standards are too high. That includes your therapist, whom you are presumably paying to care about you.

Another potential sign is when dates for you have been about as rare as Halley's Comet sightings. Sure, there may be legit reasons for your dry spell—such as a lack of singles or diversity around you. But what exactly is keeping you from dating the single Halley, who's already expressed interest in you?

A flip-side of this sign is treating dating like casting calls for the next James Bond or Wonder Woman—where you go through a ton of first looks but very, very few second looks. You may believe that dating is a numbers game in some ways. But too exacting standards may be wasting a number of people's time.

Speaking of numbers, how about those social media posts that use census data to calculate the actual chances of meeting someone based on your dating standards? Or calculators like the one from Keeper? These may be rough calculations, but they can show how rough things may be for you—especially when your probabilities of success dip below 1%.

It's a bad sign if you've never, ever seen anywhere on Earth anything that comes close to what your standards indicate. And someone from Middle Earth, a persona portrayed by some celebrity, or any other fantasy character doesn't count. Reality is a lot messier. So, use the successful pairings around you, instead, to help benchmark what's possible.

Remember, too, that your standards should be based in part on what you currently offer in return. If you yourself aren't loyal and haven't yet gone through an emotional healing journey, then, maybe, just maybe, you can't expect the same out of others. And remember each demand of yours demands more trade-offs from you. If, for example, you want someone driven to achieve boatloads of money and high social status, expect to play second fiddle to that person—or maybe even third or fourth fiddle.

Finally, check how well your standards match what truly would be a match for you. Will your criteria help or hurt your chances of finding someone with a personality and core values that fit well with yours? If, for example, a primary interest of yours is finding someone who will be very loyal, then maybe being super rich and having a hot, hot, hot body shouldn't be such absolute criteria. You could remove at least one of the "hots" from there.

Ultimately, it is important to have standards. But they do have to stand—stand the test of time and really stand for who you are. If not, you might end up with someone you simply cannot stand.

#4772. What does a blue Texas sweep mean for Elon Musk, Tesla & SpaceX?

After a historic election defeat in one of the most expensive elections in US election history, the state of Texas has turned blue. Along with this come potential implications for Elon Musk and his companies.

The US midterm election was yesterday, and ended up in a stunning result, with Democrats taking control of both houses of Congress by wide margins, and flipping seats that had been considered safe around the nation.

Perhaps most notably, many of these seats were in Texas – a republican stronghold which saw a clean sweep, with the Democrats winning every statewide contest. Republicans maintained some state legislative power and won the State Senate, but as of this writing Democrats are currently ahead in the count for the State House.

The highest profile contest was for Texas Senate, where James Talarico won by a 55-43 margin. This race set the record for the most expensive Senate race in the nation’s history, with over half a billion dollars spent on this one seat alone.

About half of the total money spent on the race came courtesy of republican PACs, a method by which companies and the ultra-wealthy can launder money into bribes to buy elections. And most of the PAC money in the race came from one man, Elon Musk, who spent $212 million to support the enormously unpopular and scandal-ridden outgoing Texas Attorney General Ken Paxton

That’s nearly half of the $474 million dollars that Musk put into this election cycle, suggesting that he saw this race of particular importance. And yet, his candidate lost miserably.

But the rout did not stop with Paxton. Paxton’s old job as AG is now going to be filled by Democrat Nathan Johnson. And Greg Abbott, the outgoing Governor, was defeated by Gina Hinojosa. Even the less high-profile races for court seats were swept by Democrats – three seats on the 15th Court of Appeals and four seats on the Texas Supreme Court.

And those results, even beyond the big-spending Senate race, could all matter quite a lot to Elon Musk.

In 2021, Musk announced that Tesla would move its headquarters to Texas, after Musk got in a spat with California government because he didn’t want to follow COVID restrictions for worker safety.

But the move wasn’t only about COVID restrictions – it was also about Musk’s vision of having more control over the business environment. And, by making allies with Ken Paxton and Greg Abbott, he has had quite a bit of control in Texas.

The two have rolled out the red carpet for Musk, giving him carte blanche in his adopted state.

Autonomous vehicle regulations have been changed, allowing driverless Robotaxis to operate where other states with more advanced regulations and transparency requirements would not. And many other laws benefitting Tesla and SpaceX have been passed. Paxton even sued Sam Altman, Musk’s chief AI rival, just weeks ago.

Perhaps more notably for Musk personally, Texas has promised to allow Musk to get away with more financial shenanigans than states with more established business regulations might.

With Tesla both headquartered and incorporated in Texas, it can now use special “business courts” instituted by Abbott at Musk’s own urging. If cases from these courts are appealed, they go to the 15th Court of Appeals, a court created by Abbott and filled with 3 hand-picked judges, intended to reduce the chance that a Democratic district court (say, one in Austin, whereTesla is headquartered) gets final say on any issue.

But, the 15th Court of Appeals just had elections last night – and its three seats were flipped to Democratic control.

The 15th Court of Appeals also, just last week, stopped an inquiry into thousands of pages of emails between Abbott and Musk. The emails had been released, but are heavily redacted, with Abbott referring to the redacted portions as having “intimate” information in them.

And who made that request for inquiry into the emails? Gina Hinojosa, a name you might recognize, as she is the next Governor of Texas, as of last night.

As of yet, those emails have not all been released, so we don’t actually know exactly how much influence Musk was able to exercise over the state’s outgoing Governor. But given the changes we know about, the money Musk spent in Texas, and the thousands of pages of emails that have been redacted, it seems likely that there is something more to be found in them.

So what does this all mean for Tesla, our main area of coverage here at Electrek?

On a Texas state level, probably not all that much. There’s no reason that Democrats in the state would interfere with Tesla’s production of electric vehicles – in fact, they’d probably encourage it more than republicans do. Talarico has stated that he supports Tesla – it is a large employer in the state, after all.

And certainly, the national wins by Democrats could be a boon for EVs, and for climate action, given how hostile republicans have been to them (and Musk has been the single largest supporter of that hostility towards EVs, which has cost Tesla billions).

The same likely goes for SpaceX – perhaps a blue Texas will be more restrictive of the company’s improper usage of protected habitats, or building of data centers, or whatever the hell is going on on twitter. But the core businesses of space launches and Starlink don’t seem likely to go anywhere.

For Elon Musk, though, things could be different. Given his “intimate” relationship with Abbott, the extreme amounts of money he spent on Paxton, and the highly personal nature of his truly ridiculous attacks against Talarico, it seems unlikely that he will have a similar level of allyship with Texas government actors as he did in prior years. Talarico’s main anti-corruption message was seemingly aimed straight at Musk’s attempts to buy elections, after all.

And given his high levels of spending on a party that is outright hostile electric vehicles, it seems clear that his personal fortunes are far more important to him than the well-being of his companies.

as a texan, I had zero faith that talrico would win, especially with the amount of money musk dumped in the race at the last minute. I’m so heartened to see that money cannot buy everything and that we, as a country, are finally pushing back against the trump/musk insanity. maybe we can start upholding the constitution and ensuring the law applies to all citizens regardless of wealth.

This is all important to note, because Musk has been working towards a scheme to merge SpaceX and Tesla, a deal which would likely be a detriment to both companies and all of their shareholders. Musk wants the deal so he can exercise more control over, and extract more personal wealth from, the combined entity.

But that scheme could rely on a favorable legal environment in Texas courts, an environment that just got less favorable.

And, given Musk’s penchant for childish lashings-out anytime he deems people insufficiently obsequious to his imagined greatness, could we see another situation where Musk encounters the tiniest amount of pushback from state government and then picks up his toys and leaves, as he already did in both California and Delaware? Time will have to tell. Keep your popcorn buckets primed and ready.

#4771. RJ Mitte's Death Highlights Mental Health Challenges for People With Cerebral Palsy

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

“Breaking Bad” actor and disability advocate RJ Mitte has died at age 34.

Mitte reportedly died by suicide, his representative told People on October 12. However, local authorities have said the official cause and manner of his death remain under investigation.

Mitte, who was best-known for playing Walter White Jr. on the Emmy Award-winning television series “Breaking Bad,” lived with cerebral palsy and advocated for disability inclusion.

There is currently no evidence to suggest that cerebral palsy caused the actor’s death. Still, Mitte’s untimely passing highlights the mental health challenges that people with cerebral palsy may face.

Research has demonstrated elevated risks of anxiety and depression among those with the condition, alongside barriers to diagnosis, social participation, and employment.

“Living with cerebral palsy can affect mental health through several interacting pathways,” said Shannel Elhelou, neuropsychologist and founder of Brain Centric Neuropsychology in Westlake Village, CA.

“Chronic pain, fatigue, disrupted sleep, and the effort required to navigate daily activities can take an emotional toll. Experiences of bullying, discrimination, social exclusion, and difficulty accessing care can add to that burden. Also, the early brain injury underlying cerebral palsy may contribute to difficulties with emotional regulation, although each person’s experience is different,” Elhelou told Healthline.

If you or someone you know is in crisis and considering suicide or self-harm, please seek support:

If you’re calling on behalf of someone else, stay with them until help arrives. You may remove weapons or substances that can cause harm if you can do so safely.

If you’re not in the same household, stay on the phone with them until help arrives.

Mitte played a key role in challenging stereotypes by portraying a teenager with cerebral palsy on “Breaking Bad.”

Outside of acting, Mitte advocated for people with disabilities through organizations including United Cerebral Palsy (UCP) and Shriners Children’s.

He also led the Mitte Foundation, a non-profit corporation founded in 1994 by his grandparents, Roy F. and Joann Cole Mitte, that improves the quality of life for people in Central Texas.

Mitte’s advocacy emphasized representation, inclusion, and opportunities for people with disabilities.

The legacy he leaves behind underscores the need to recognize mental health as an essential part of care for individuals with cerebral palsy and other disabilities.

Cerebral palsy (CP) is a group of conditions affecting movement, posture, and coordination.

Most people with cerebral palsy have typical intelligence, although some also have intellectual disabilities or other comorbid physical and mental health conditions.

A 2018 study published in JAMA Neurology found that adults with cerebral palsy had a 28% higher risk of developing depression and a 40% higher risk of anxiety than adults without the condition.

A 2024 study in JAMA Network Open found higher recorded rates of anxiety and attention deficit hyperactivity disorder (ADHD) among children and young adults with cerebral palsy.

The researchers also identified comparatively low rates of depression and suicidal ideation, highlighting concerns that these conditions may be underdiagnosed.

“Research shows that adults with cerebral palsy have an increased risk of depression and anxiety,” Elhelou said.

“However, these conditions should never be viewed as an inevitable part of having a disability. One concern is ‘diagnostic overshadowing,’ when symptoms of depression, anxiety, or other mental health conditions are attributed to cerebral palsy itself, leading those conditions to go unrecognized and untreated. Someone’s fatigue, withdrawal, or changes in behavior deserve careful assessment rather than the assumption that ‘this is just their cerebral palsy,'” she continued.

“While these findings help us understand mental health risks among people with cerebral palsy, they cannot tell us what any one person was experiencing. Many interacting factors may contribute to someone dying by suicide, and it would be inappropriate to attribute an individual’s death to their disability.”

A 2019 study published in Developmental Medicine & Child Neurology found that children with cerebral palsy had higher odds of anxiety disorders and behavioral challenges than children without the condition.

Difficulties with friendships and bullying were associated with some of these differences.

Factors that may affect well-being include:

The researchers also found that supportive friendships may help reduce some of these disparities, reinforcing the importance of inclusive schools and communities.

A 2021 study published in the American Journal of Preventive Medicine found that U.S. adults with disabilities faced increased risks of suicidal thoughts, planning, and attempts compared with adults without disabilities.

The 988 Lifeline received more than 8 million contacts through calls, texts, chats, and American Sign Language videophone in 2025, according to the Substance Abuse and Mental Health Services Administration.

Federal funding for the Lifeline’s dedicated LGBTQ+ youth service ended in July 2025, removing the option to press 3 for specialized support.

After pressure from Congress, the service was recently restored at the end of September, according to the Kaiser Family Foundation (KFF).

Employment and financial independence can influence well-being for anyone, particularly those with cerebral palsy.

However, a 2024 study found that just 30.4% of young adults with cerebral palsy who participated in vocational rehabilitation programs were employed when they exited those programs.

Workplace accommodations, accessible hiring, vocational rehabilitation, and inclusive social opportunities can help address these barriers.

“Depression and anxiety are treatable, and they should never be dismissed as an inevitable part of living with a disability,” Elhelou said.

“If you notice persistent sadness, anxiety, hopelessness, or withdrawal, reach out to someone you trust and a mental health professional. You deserve a clinician who takes your concerns seriously and adapts treatment to your communication, mobility, or other access needs. Care should also address pain, fatigue, and sleep difficulties, which can affect emotional well-being.”

Organizations such as United Cerebral Palsy offer information and support for people with cerebral palsy and their families.

Healthcare professionals can also improve outcomes by screening for mental health concerns, using appropriate assessment tools, and connecting patients with accessible treatment.

Improved access to care, inclusive communities, and meaningful opportunities can help people with cerebral palsy and other disabilities thrive.

“Asking for help does not diminish your resilience or your contributions. If you are experiencing thoughts of suicide or feel overwhelmed, you do not have to wait until things feel unbearable to reach out,” Elhelou said.

“Supportive relationships, peer groups, and disability communities can offer connection and understanding. Staying engaged in activities that bring enjoyment and meaning can also help.”
